UGC NET Psychology — Past Papers & Syllabus

A faithful reference pack for the UGC NET (subject code 04) Psychology paper: the complete official 10-unit syllabus reproduced as a clean outline, a plain-language guide to how the exam works distilled from the NTA FAQ, and three full past papers transcribed question-by-question. The December 2019 paper has every answer worked out and explained (the key once printed against it did not line up with the paper); the June 2019 and 2020 papers had no answer key at all, so every answer there is worked out and explained the same way. Use this to learn the real shape, weight and wording of the exam.

In this book
  1. The official syllabus (all 10 units)
  2. How the exam works
  3. December 2019 NET paper (answers worked out)
  4. June 2019 NET paper (answers worked out)
  5. 2020 NET paper (answers worked out)

1. The official syllabus (all 10 units)

This is the University Grants Commission (UGC) NET Bureau syllabus for Psychology, Code No. 04, reproduced in full. The paper draws its questions from these ten units. Topics are listed exactly as in the official syllabus.

High-yield

Every NET Psychology question maps to one of these ten units. Knowing the unit a question belongs to is half the battle: it tells you which body of theory the examiner is testing. Units 1 (Emergence/systems), 2 (Methodology & Statistics) and 3 (Testing) are disproportionately heavy in the papers in this book.

Unit 1. Emergence of Psychology

  • Eastern systems: Psychological thought in major Eastern systems — Bhagavad Gita, Buddhism, Sufism and Integral Yoga.
  • Academic psychology in India: Pre-independence era; post-independence era; 1970s (the move to addressing social issues); 1980s (Indigenization); 1990s (paradigmatic concerns, disciplinary identity crisis); 2000s (emergence of Indian psychology in academia). Issues: the colonial encounter; post-colonialism and psychology; lack of distinct disciplinary identity.
  • Western: Greek heritage, medieval period and modern period. Structuralism, Functionalism, Psychoanalytical, Gestalt, Behaviorism, Humanistic-Existential, Transpersonal, Cognitive revolution, Multiculturalism. Four founding paths of academic psychology — Wundt, Freud, James, Dilthey. Issues: crisis in psychology due to strict adherence to the experimental-analytical paradigm (logical empiricism); Indic influences on modern psychology.
  • Knowledge paradigms: Essential aspects — ontology, epistemology, and methodology. Paradigms of Western Psychology: Positivism, Post-Positivism, Critical perspective, Social Constructionism, Existential Phenomenology, and Co-operative Enquiry. Paradigmatic controversies. Significant Indian paradigms on psychological knowledge: Yoga, Bhagavad Gita, Buddhism, Sufism, and Integral Yoga. Science and spirituality (avidya and vidya). The primacy of self-knowledge in Indian psychology.

Unit 2. Research Methodology and Statistics

  • Research: Meaning, purpose, and dimensions. Research problems, variables and operational definitions, hypothesis, sampling. Ethics in conducting and reporting research.
  • Paradigms of research: Quantitative, Qualitative, Mixed methods approach.
  • Methods of research: Observation, Survey (interview, questionnaires), Experimental, Quasi-experimental, Field studies, Cross-cultural studies, Phenomenology, Grounded theory, Focus groups, Narratives, Case studies, Ethnography.
  • Statistics: Measures of central tendency and dispersion. Normal probability curve. Parametric (t-test) and non-parametric tests (Sign test, Wilcoxon signed-rank test, Mann-Whitney test, Kruskal-Wallis test, Friedman). Power analysis. Effect size.
  • Correlational analysis: Correlation (Product Moment, Rank Order), partial correlation, multiple correlation. Special correlation methods: Biserial, Point-biserial, tetrachoric, phi coefficient. Regression: simple linear regression, multiple regression. Factor analysis: assumptions, methods, rotation and interpretation.
  • Experimental designs: ANOVA (one-way, factorial), Randomized Block Designs, Repeated Measures Design, Latin Square, Cohort studies, Time series, MANOVA, ANCOVA. Single-subject designs.

Unit 3. Psychological Testing

  • Types of tests.
  • Test construction: Item writing, item analysis.
  • Test standardization: Reliability, validity and norms.
  • Areas of testing: Intelligence, creativity, neuropsychological tests, aptitude, personality assessment, interest inventories.
  • Attitude scales — Semantic differential, Stapel, Likert scale.
  • Computer-based psychological testing.
  • Applications of psychological testing across settings: Clinical, Organizational and business, Education, Counseling, Military, Career guidance.

Unit 4. Biological Basis of Behavior

  • Sensory systems: general and specific sensations, receptors and processes.
  • Neurons: structure, functions, types, neural impulse, synaptic transmission. Neurotransmitters.
  • The Central and Peripheral Nervous Systems — structure and functions. Neuroplasticity.
  • Methods of Physiological Psychology: Invasive methods (anatomical methods, degeneration techniques, lesion techniques, chemical methods, microelectrode studies); Non-invasive methods (EEG, scanning methods).
  • Muscular and glandular system: types and functions.
  • Biological basis of motivation: hunger, thirst, sleep and sex.
  • Biological basis of emotion: the limbic system, hormonal regulation of behavior.
  • Genetics and behavior: chromosomal anomalies; nature-nurture controversy (twin studies and adoption studies).

Unit 5. Attention, Perception, Learning, Memory and Forgetting

  • Attention: Forms of attention, models of attention.
  • Perception: Approaches to the study of perception (Gestalt and physiological). Perceptual organization (Gestalt, figure and ground, laws of organization). Perceptual constancy (size, shape, colour); illusions. Perception of form, depth and movement. Role of motivation and learning in perception. Signal detection theory: assumptions and applications. Subliminal perception and related factors, information processing approach, culture and perception, perceptual styles, pattern recognition, ecological perspective on perception.
  • Learning process: Fundamental theories (Thorndike, Guthrie, Hull). Classical conditioning: procedure, phenomena, related issues. Instrumental learning: phenomena, paradigms, theoretical issues. Reinforcement: basic variables and schedules; behaviour modification and its applications. Cognitive approaches in learning: latent learning, observational learning. Verbal learning and discrimination learning. Recent trends: neurophysiology of learning.
  • Memory and forgetting: Memory processes (encoding, storage, retrieval). Stages of memory (sensory, short-term/working, long-term — declarative: episodic and semantic; procedural). Theories of forgetting (interference, retrieval failure, decay, motivated forgetting).

Unit 6. Thinking, Intelligence and Creativity

  • Theoretical perspectives on thought processes: Associationism, Gestalt, Information processing, Feature integration model.
  • Concept formation: rules, types, strategies; role of concepts in thinking. Types of reasoning. Language and thought.
  • Problem solving: types, strategies, obstacles. Decision-making: types and models.
  • Metacognition: metacognitive knowledge and metacognitive regulation.
  • Intelligence: Spearman; Thurstone; Jensen; Cattell; Gardner; Sternberg; Goleman; Das, Kar & Parrila.
  • Creativity: Torrance, Getzels & Jackson, Guilford, Wallach & Kogan. Relationship between intelligence and creativity.

Unit 7. Personality, Motivation, Emotion, Stress and Coping

  • Determinants of personality: biological and socio-cultural.
  • Approaches to personality: Psychoanalytical, Neo-Freudian, Social learning, Trait and Type, Cognitive, Humanistic, Existential, Transpersonal. Other theories: Rotter's Locus of Control, Seligman's explanatory styles, Kohlberg's theory of moral development.
  • Basic motivational concepts: instincts, needs, drives, arousal, incentives, motivational cycle. Approaches to motivation: Psychoanalytical, Ethological, S-R, Cognitive, Humanistic.
  • Exploratory behavior and curiosity. Zuckerman's sensation seeking. Achievement, affiliation and power. Motivational competence. Self-regulation. Flow.
  • Emotions: physiological correlates. Theories of emotion: James-Lange, Cannon-Bard, Schachter and Singer, Lazarus, Lindsley. Emotion regulation.
  • Conflicts: sources and types. Stress and coping: concept, models, Type A/B/C/D behaviors, stress-management strategies (Biofeedback, Music therapy, Breathing exercises, Progressive Muscular Relaxation, Guided Imagery, Mindfulness, Meditation, Yogasana, Stress Inoculation Training).

Unit 8. Social Psychology

  • Nature, scope and history of social psychology.
  • Traditional theoretical perspectives: Field theory, Cognitive Dissonance, Sociobiology, Psychodynamic approaches, Social Cognition.
  • Social perception (communication, attributions); attitude and its change within cultural context; prosocial behavior.
  • Group and social influence (Social Facilitation; Social Loafing). Social influence (Conformity, Peer Pressure, Persuasion, Compliance, Obedience, Social Power, Reactance). Aggression. Group dynamics, leadership style and effectiveness. Theories of intergroup relations (Minimal Group Experiment and Social Identity Theory, Relative Deprivation Theory, Realistic Conflict Theory, Balance Theories, Equity Theory, Social Exchange Theory).
  • Applied social psychology: Health, Environment and Law; personal space, crowding, and territoriality.

Unit 9. Human Development and Interventions

  • Developmental processes: nature, principles, factors, stages of development. Successful aging.
  • Theories of development: Psychoanalytical, Behavioristic, Cognitive. Various aspects of development: sensory-motor, cognitive, language, emotional, social and moral.
  • Psychopathology: concept, Mental Status Examination, classification, causes.
  • Psychotherapies: Psychoanalysis, Person-centered, Gestalt, Existential, Acceptance Commitment Therapy, Behavior therapy, REBT, CBT, MBCT, Play therapy, Positive psychotherapy, Transactional Analysis, Dialectical Behavior Therapy, Art therapy, Performing Art Therapy, Family therapy.
  • Applications of theories of motivation and learning in school. Factors in educational achievement. Teacher effectiveness. Guidance in schools: needs, organizational set-up and techniques.
  • Counselling: process, skills, and techniques.

Unit 10. Emerging Areas

  • Issues of gender, poverty, disability, and migration: cultural bias and discrimination. Stigma, marginalization, and social suffering; child abuse and domestic violence.
  • Peace psychology: violence, non-violence, conflict resolution at the macro level, role of media in conflict resolution.
  • Wellbeing and self-growth: types of wellbeing (Hedonic and Eudemonic), character strengths, resilience and post-traumatic growth.
  • Health: health-promoting and health-compromising behaviors, lifestyle and chronic diseases (Diabetes, Hypertension, Coronary Heart Disease), psychoneuroimmunology (Cancer, HIV/AIDS).
  • Psychology and technology interface: digital learning; digital etiquette; cyber bullying; cyber pornography (consumption, implications); parental mediation of digital usage.

2. How the exam works

UGC-NET (the National Eligibility Test) is conducted by the National Testing Agency (NTA) on behalf of the University Grants Commission. It determines eligibility for Assistant Professor only, or for Assistant Professor and Junior Research Fellowship (JRF) both, in colleges and universities. Below is the practical guidance distilled from the official NTA FAQ document.

Format and mode

  • Mode: Computer Based Test (CBT) only.
  • Sittings: Two shifts on exam day — roughly 09:00–12:00 and 15:00–18:00. The date and slot are allotted randomly by computer (needed for the normalization process); a candidate cannot choose them.
  • Two papers in one session: Paper 1 (general teaching/research aptitude, common to all subjects) and Paper 2 (the Psychology subject paper based on the 10-unit syllabus above). Both are attempted in the same sitting.
  • Subjects: NET is conducted in roughly 80+ subjects; Psychology is subject code 04.
High-yield

There is NO negative marking in UGC-NET. The official FAQ states plainly: "No. There is no negative marking for wrong answers." So you should attempt every question — leaving a question blank can only cost you, never protect you. (This is the opposite of many other entrance tests, so do not carry a "skip if unsure" habit into NET.)

Marking and result

  • Negative marking: None. Every question carries positive marks only; wrong answers are not penalised.
  • Qualifying criterion: Per UGC policy, the top 6% of candidates who appear in both papers and obtain the minimum qualifying marks (in aggregate of both papers) are declared NET-qualified.
  • No all-India rank: The FAQ states "Rank is not assigned in UGC-NET." Results are pass/qualify based, with JRF allocated subject-wise and category-wise.
  • Challenging the key: After the test, the question paper, recorded responses and the provisional answer key are released. Candidates may challenge an answer by paying a non-refundable fee (Rs 200 per question challenged) within the given window. The final result uses the answer key vetted by subject experts. No re-evaluation or re-checking is done after results.

Eligibility and age

  • Subject: Candidates should appear in the subject of their post-graduation. If their PG subject is not in the NET list, they may appear in a related subject.
  • JRF age limit: Not more than 31 years (as on the first day of the month in which the exam concludes), with relaxations for reserved categories. There is no upper age limit for the Assistant Professor eligibility.
  • Category concessions: Candidates in the non-creamy layer whose category is in the central OBC list, and SC/ST/PwD candidates, get fee and (where applicable) qualifying-mark concessions.

Fees (as per the cited cycle)

CategoryFee
General / UnreservedRs. 1100
Gen-EWS / OBC-NCLRs. 550
SC / ST / PwD / Third genderRs. 275

Payment is online only (debit/credit card or net banking). Fees are non-refundable and applications cannot be withdrawn. Documents and most details can be edited later through the correction window.

Mnemonic

Remember the three numbers that define NET: 0 (zero negative marking), 6 (top 6% qualify), 2 (two papers in one session). Attempt everything, aim for the top slice, and budget time across both papers.

Trap

NET does not give you a "rank" or a "score out of 100" that ranks you nationally for admission the way a CET does. Qualifying is a percentile-style cut-off (top 6%). Do not confuse a NET qualification with an entrance-exam merit rank.

3. December 2019 NET paper (answers worked out)

This is the UGC NET Psychology December 2019 question paper, reproduced from the source document. This page used to print an answer key against it, but that key does not line up with this paper's questions: it gave the pituitary as Descartes' seat of mind-body interaction, the key word system for acronyms, and agreed with the textbook answer on only 26 of 97 questions, about what random answers would get. So every answer below is now worked out from the standard textbooks: 71 of the 97 differ from the old key, and the two it marked withdrawn are answered too. Each question shows the answer and who it comes from, the concept in short chunks, and one line on why each other option is wrong. The source document ran from Q1 to Q100 with one numbering gap, so 99 questions are reproduced here.

Trap

These answers are worked out, not an official key. Best answer marks the 18 questions that are ambiguous or come from a niche source; read the "Not certain" line there, it names the runner-up option. "Old key" lines show what this page said before, so you can unlearn it.

  1. In what sequence are the Social Combination rules followed for group success?

    • 1Additive, disjunctive, compensatory, conjunctive
    • 2Additive, compensatory, disjunctive, conjunctive
    • 3Disjunctive, compensatory, conjunctive, additive
    • 4Compensatory, conjunctive, disjunctive, additive
    ✓ Best answer: (2) Additive, compensatory, disjunctive, conjunctive
    Ivan Steiner, typology of group tasks (1972)
    The concept

    Why this oneThis is the standard order of Ivan Steiner's combination rules, from the most to the least group success: additive first, conjunctive last.

    What they areSteiner classified tasks by how members' inputs are combined. Additive tasks sum efforts (tug of war), so the group beats its best member; compensatory tasks average judgements, so the group beats most members.

    The other twoDisjunctive tasks need one correct answer, so the group can only equal its best member. Conjunctive tasks need everyone to finish, so the weakest member sets the pace, like a roped climbing team.

    Exam tipPooling (additive, compensatory) helps groups most; conjunctive tasks expose the weakest link. Steiner also named discretionary tasks, where the group picks its own rule.

    Not certainIf 'equal to the best member' (disjunctive) is ranked above 'better than most members' (compensatory), disjunctive moves to second place, which gives (1).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Additive, disjunctive, compensatory, conjunctiveSwaps compensatory and disjunctive; the standard sequence places compensatory (averaging judgements) second and disjunctive (relying on the best member) third.
    • (3) Disjunctive, compensatory, conjunctive, additiveReverses the scale by ending with additive; additive tasks, where every member's effort adds up, give the highest group output and come first.
    • (4) Compensatory, conjunctive, disjunctive, additiveEnds with additive and puts conjunctive second; additive tasks should head the list, and conjunctive tasks, limited by the weakest member, come last.

  2. Which mnemonic device is used for the expression of different letters of a word?

    • 1Acoustics
    • 2Acronyms
    • 3Key word system
    • 4Clustering
    ✓ Answer: (2) Acronyms
    First-letter mnemonics (acronyms)
    The concept

    Why this oneAn acronym takes the first letter of each item to be remembered and makes a word or name from them, so each letter cues one item.

    ExamplesROY G BIV gives the colours of the spectrum (red, orange, yellow, green, blue, indigo, violet). HOMES gives the five Great Lakes.

    Exam tipAcronym = letters form a word; acrostic = letters begin the words of a sentence; method of loci = familiar places; peg-word = rhyming pegs.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) AcousticsAcoustic coding means storing information by its sound, typical of short-term memory; it is not a memory aid built from letters.
    • (3) Key word systemThe keyword method links a new (often foreign) word to a similar-sounding familiar word plus a vivid image; it is used for vocabulary learning.
    • (4) ClusteringClustering is grouping items by meaning or category during recall, such as all fruits together; it organises items rather than using their letters.

  3. Which of the following was described by R.B. Cattell using P-Technique of Factor Analysis?

    • 1Differential effects of stimulus situations on personality
    • 2Group formation
    • 3Social interactions
    • 4Individual differences among people
    ✓ Answer: (1) Differential effects of stimulus situations on personality
    Raymond Cattell, P-technique factor analysis
    The concept

    Why this oneP-technique factor-analyses many measures taken on one person across many occasions, so it shows how that person's personality shifts with changing situations.

    How it worksOrdinary R-technique correlates tests across many people, giving individual differences. Q-technique correlates people across tests to find types. P-technique stays inside one individual over time.

    Exam tipRaymond Cattell used P-technique to separate fluctuating states from stable traits. P = Person over occasions; R = many people; Q = people as variables.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Group formationGroup formation is studied through sociometry and small-group research, not by factor-analysing one person's scores over time.
    • (3) Social interactionsPatterns of social interaction are mapped by observation methods such as Robert Bales' interaction process analysis, not by P-technique.
    • (4) Individual differences among peopleDifferences between people come from R-technique, which correlates traits across a large sample; P-technique deliberately studies a single person.

  4. Which of the following would be the best method for studying the relative contribution of heredity and environment?

    • 1Comparing identical and fraternal twins reared together
    • 2Comparing identical and fraternal twins reared apart
    • 3Comparing siblings reared together and reared apart
    • 4Comparing identical and fraternal twins, half of whom have been reared apart
    ✓ Answer: (4) Comparing identical and fraternal twins, half of whom have been reared apart
    Twin and adoption designs (behaviour genetics)
    The concept

    Why this oneUsing identical and fraternal twins, with half reared apart, varies genes and environment separately, so the effect of each can be pulled apart.

    How it worksIdentical (MZ) twins share all their genes, fraternal (DZ) twins about half. If MZ twins stay more alike than DZ twins even when reared apart, heredity matters; if twins reared together are more alike than those reared apart, shared environment matters.

    Exam tipThe fullest design has four groups: MZ together, MZ apart, DZ together, DZ apart. Each comparison isolates one source of similarity.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Comparing identical and fraternal twins reared togetherTwins reared together share their home as well as their genes, so environment never varies; greater MZ similarity could partly reflect more similar treatment.
    • (2) Comparing identical and fraternal twins reared apartSeparating twins varies environment, but without a reared-together comparison you cannot gauge how much a shared upbringing adds.
    • (3) Comparing siblings reared together and reared apartOrdinary siblings all share about half their genes, so genetic similarity never varies and the role of heredity cannot be estimated.

  5. Which one of the following statements is true regarding the relationship of test length with reliability and validity?

    • 1As test length increases, only reliability improves.
    • 2As test length increases, only validity improves.
    • 3As test length increases, neither reliability nor validity improves.
    • 4As test length increases, both reliability and validity improve.
    ✓ Answer: (4) As test length increases, both reliability and validity improve.
    Spearman-Brown prophecy formula
    The concept

    Why this oneAdding more comparable items shrinks the share of random error, so reliability rises; because validity is limited by reliability, validity rises too, though more slowly.

    How it worksThe Spearman-Brown formula predicts the new reliability: rnew = nr / [1 + (n - 1)r], where n is how many times longer the test becomes.

    Exam tipA validity coefficient cannot exceed the square root of reliability, so a more reliable test has a higher validity ceiling. Gains level off as a test gets very long.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) As test length increases, only reliability improves.Validity also improves with length, because the ceiling on validity is set by reliability; reliability does not rise on its own.
    • (2) As test length increases, only validity improves.Reliability is the quantity most directly raised by lengthening (Spearman-Brown), so it cannot be that only validity improves.
    • (3) As test length increases, neither reliability nor validity improves.Lengthening with comparable items does improve both; only adding poor or unrelated items fails to help.

  6. Match the given concepts in List I with the description given in List II.

    • List I: (a) Turiyavastha; (b) Prajnavastha; (c) Swapanvastha; (d) Jagratavastha
    • List II: (i) The state of normal consciousness concerned with the material world; (ii) Subconscious faculty concerned with the subtler aspects of human knowledge and experience; (iii) State of super consciousness when the dichotomy between grossness and subtlety disappears; (iv) Steady state of consciousness when the essential nature of atman is experienced

    Codes:

    • 1(a)-(iii), (b)-(ii), (c)-(i), (d)-(iv)
    • 2(a)-(iv), (b)-(iii), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    • 3(a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    • 4(a)-(i), (b)-(iii), (c)-(ii), (d)-(iv)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a)-(iv), (b)-(iii), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    Mandukya Upanishad, four states of consciousness
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Turiyavastha = (iv) steady state where the atman is experienced; (b) Prajnavastha = (iii) super consciousness beyond gross and subtle; (c) Swapanvastha = (ii) subconscious, subtle experience; (d) Jagratavastha = (i) normal waking consciousness of the material world.

    What they areThe Mandukya Upanishad describes waking (Jagrat), dream (Swapna), deep sleep (Prajna or Sushupti) and the fourth state, Turiya, pure consciousness underlying the other three.

    Exam tipFrom gross to subtle: Jagrat → Swapna → Prajna → Turiya. Turiya literally means 'the fourth'.

    Old keyThis page used to show (4) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(iii), (b)-(ii), (c)-(i), (d)-(iv)Gives normal waking consciousness (i) to the dream state and the atman state (iv) to Jagrat, reversing the least and most subtle states.
    • (3) (a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)Swaps Turiya and Prajna; the steady experience of the atman belongs to Turiya, the fourth state, not to deep sleep.
    • (4) (a)-(i), (b)-(iii), (c)-(ii), (d)-(iv)Swaps Turiya and Jagrat; Jagrat is ordinary waking awareness of the material world, while Turiya is the state in which the atman is known.

  7. The Eudemonic view of Subjective Well-being holds that:

    • (a) true happiness is found in expression of virtue
    • (b) well-being refers to "striving for perfection"
    • (c) subjective well-being focuses on bodily pleasures like focus on appetite and self-interests
    • (d) well-being consists of fulfilling its true self

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1Only (c)
    • 2(c) and (d)
    • 3(a), (b) and (d)
    • 4Only (a)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a), (b) and (d)
    Aristotle's eudaimonia; Carol Ryff's psychological well-being
    The concept

    Why this oneThe eudaimonic view says well-being comes from living virtuously and realising one's true potential, so (a), (b) and (d) fit.

    Where it comes fromAristotle saw happiness as the expression of virtue. Carol Ryff described well-being as striving for perfection that realises one's true potential, and Alan Waterman tied it to living in line with the true self (daimon).

    Why (c) is outBodily pleasure, appetite and self-interest describe the hedonic view, which equates well-being with gaining pleasure and avoiding pain.

    Exam tipHedonic = feeling good (Ed Diener's subjective well-being); eudaimonic = functioning well (Ryff's six dimensions).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Only (c)Statement (c) is the hedonic view of pleasure and appetite, the opposite of the eudaimonic view.
    • (2) (c) and (d)(d) is eudaimonic, but (c) is hedonic, and the option leaves out (a) and (b), which are core eudaimonic ideas.
    • (4) Only (a)(a) is correct, but (b) and (d), striving for perfection and fulfilling the true self, are also eudaimonic, so (a) alone is incomplete.

  8. Read the following conversation and choose the best option. X: "What time is it?" Y: "I think it is about 9.30 AM."

    • 1Adult-adult transaction
    • 2Adult-adult complimentary transaction
    • 3Child-adult complimentary transaction
    • 4Child-adult transaction
    ✓ Answer: (2) Adult-adult complimentary transaction
    Eric Berne, transactional analysis
    The concept

    Why this oneX asks a factual question from the Adult to Y's Adult, and Y replies Adult to Adult. The reply comes from the ego state that was addressed, so the transaction is complementary.

    What it isIn Eric Berne's transactional analysis, a complementary transaction has parallel lines on the diagram: the expected ego state responds, so communication can go on smoothly.

    Exam tipComplementary = expected reply; crossed = reply from an unexpected ego state, which halts communication; ulterior = a hidden psychological message under the social one.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Adult-adult transactionTrue as far as it goes but incomplete; it leaves out that the transaction is complementary, which is the defining feature here.
    • (3) Child-adult complimentary transactionNeither speaker is in the Child state; both are calm, factual and reality-focused, which marks the Adult.
    • (4) Child-adult transactionX's question is a neutral request for information, not a Child expression of feeling, play or need.

  9. Which are the two basic features of Schema?

    • (a) Influencing memory
    • (b) Promoting forgetting
    • (c) Resisting change
    • (d) Promoting change

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a) and (b)
    • 2(a) and (c)
    • 3(a) and (d)
    • 4(b) and (c)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a) and (c)
    Frederic Bartlett; Robert Baron and Donn Byrne, social cognition
    The concept

    Why this oneSchemas influence memory (what we notice, store and recall), and they resist change, even when evidence contradicts them.

    Shaping memoryFrederic Bartlett's 'War of the Ghosts' study showed people reshaping an unfamiliar story to fit their own cultural schemas when they recalled it.

    Resisting changeThis stubbornness is the perseverance effect: schemas and beliefs persist even after the information that built them has been discredited.

    Exam tipSchemas can also act as self-fulfilling prophecies, leading people to behave in ways that make the expectation come true.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) and (b)Influencing memory is right, but promoting forgetting is not a defining feature; the second basic feature is resistance to change.
    • (3) (a) and (d)Schemas do the opposite of promoting change: they are stable and tend to persist against contradicting evidence.
    • (4) (b) and (c)Resisting change is right, but promoting forgetting is not a core feature; the other basic feature is their influence on memory.

  10. Which among the following are the levels of processing proposed by Craik and Lockhart?

    • 1Symbolic, Semantic and Acoustics
    • 2Physical, behavioral and Acoustics
    • 3Behavioral, Acoustics and Semantic
    • 4Physical, Acoustics and Semantic
    ✓ Answer: (4) Physical, Acoustics and Semantic
    Fergus Craik and Robert Lockhart, levels of processing (1972)
    The concept

    Why this oneFergus Craik and Robert Lockhart described processing from shallow to deep: physical (structural, how a word looks), acoustic (phonemic, how it sounds) and semantic (what it means).

    How it worksDeeper processing gives more durable memory. Judging a word's meaning leads to better recall than judging its rhyme, which beats judging its typeface.

    Exam tipShallow = structural; middle = phonemic; deep = semantic. Elaborative rehearsal supports deep processing, while maintenance rehearsal keeps it shallow.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Symbolic, Semantic and AcousticsSymbolic is not one of the levels; the shallowest level is physical or structural processing of how the stimulus looks.
    • (2) Physical, behavioral and AcousticsBehavioural is not a level of processing, and the deepest level, semantic processing of meaning, is missing.
    • (3) Behavioral, Acoustics and SemanticBehavioural is not a level, and the shallow physical (structural) level is missing from this set.

  11. According to Goleman, the 'Management of Emotions' component of Emotional Intelligence includes:

    • (a) Handling feelings so that they are appropriate.
    • (b) Ability to soothe oneself.
    • (c) Ability to shake off rampant anxiety, gloom or irritability.
    • (d) Recognizing a feeling as it happens.

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a), (b) and (d) only
    • 2(b), (c) and (d) only
    • 3(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 4(a), (b), (c) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a), (b) and (c) only
    Daniel Goleman, Emotional Intelligence (1995)
    The concept

    Why this oneIn Daniel Goleman's model, managing emotions means handling feelings so they are appropriate, soothing oneself and shaking off rampant anxiety, gloom or irritability: (a), (b) and (c).

    Why (d) is outRecognising a feeling as it happens is knowing one's emotions (self-awareness), the first and separate domain.

    Five domainsGoleman, building on Peter Salovey and John Mayer, lists: knowing one's emotions, managing emotions, motivating oneself, recognising emotions in others and handling relationships.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (b) and (d) only(d), recognising a feeling as it happens, is self-awareness, not management; and (c), shaking off anxiety or gloom, is left out.
    • (2) (b), (c) and (d) only(d) belongs to self-awareness, and the option omits (a), handling feelings so they are appropriate, the core of managing emotions.
    • (4) (a), (b), (c) and (d)(d) does not belong; recognising a feeling as it happens is self-awareness, a separate domain that comes before managing it.

  12. Read the following conversation and choose the correct option. X: "I am so hungry! I am like way starving." Y: "You might consider a small nutritional snack to suppress your appetite."

    • 1Adult-adult and child-child interaction respectively
    • 2Complimentary transactions
    • 3Child-child and adult-adult crossed transactions respectively
    • 4Child-adult and adult-child crossed transactions respectively
    ✓ Answer: (3) Child-child and adult-adult crossed transactions respectively
    Eric Berne, transactional analysis: crossed transaction
    The concept

    Why this oneX speaks from the Child (an excited, needy complaint) to Y's Child. Y answers from the Adult with factual advice aimed at X's Adult, so the transaction is crossed.

    What it isIn Eric Berne's transactional analysis, a crossed transaction occurs when the reply comes from an ego state other than the one addressed. The lines cross on the diagram and communication breaks down until someone shifts.

    Exam tipRead each line separately: which ego state is speaking and which it is aimed at. Expected reply = complementary; switched states = crossed.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Adult-adult and child-child interaction respectivelyX's excited 'way starving' is not an Adult statement and Y's measured advice is not a Child statement; the labels are reversed.
    • (2) Complimentary transactionsA complementary reply would come from Y's Child (for example, 'Me too, let's get pizza!'); Y switches to the Adult instead.
    • (4) Child-adult and adult-child crossed transactions respectivelyChild to Adult answered by Adult to Child gives parallel lines, which is a complementary transaction, so calling it crossed is self-contradictory.

  13. Match the following concepts with the corresponding bodily states.

    • List I: (a) Rebound Phenomena; (b) Phantom Limb; (c) Scotopic Process; (d) Apoptosis
    • List II: (i) REM sleep; (ii) Pain; (iii) Vision; (iv) Cell death

    Choose the correct option from those given below:

    • 1(a)-(i), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(iv)
    • 2(a)-(ii), (b)-(i), (c)-(iv), (d)-(iii)
    • 3(a)-(i), (b)-(iv), (c)-(iii), (d)-(ii)
    • 4(a)-(iv), (b)-(i), (c)-(iii), (d)-(ii)
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a)-(i), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(iv)
    Biopsychology: sleep, pain, vision and cell death
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Rebound phenomena = (i) REM sleep; (b) Phantom limb = (ii) pain; (c) Scotopic process = (iii) vision; (d) Apoptosis = (iv) cell death.

    Sleep and painREM rebound: after REM deprivation, people show more REM sleep than usual on later nights. Phantom limb: sensations, often pain, felt in a limb that has been amputated.

    Vision and deathScotopic vision is rod-based vision in dim light, without colour. Apoptosis is programmed, active cell death, as when surplus neurons die during development.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (a)-(ii), (b)-(i), (c)-(iv), (d)-(iii)Swaps REM sleep and pain between (a) and (b), and links scotopic to cell death; scotopic refers to rod vision in dim light.
    • (3) (a)-(i), (b)-(iv), (c)-(iii), (d)-(ii)Links phantom limb to cell death and apoptosis to pain; phantom limb is a pain sensation, and apoptosis is programmed cell death.
    • (4) (a)-(iv), (b)-(i), (c)-(iii), (d)-(ii)Links rebound to cell death and apoptosis to pain; rebound is the extra REM sleep after REM deprivation.

  14. What is the reason for the negative voltage of a neuron during Resting Potential? (Sodium = Na+; Potassium = K+; Chloride = Cl-; Organic anions = A-)

    • 1Na+ and Cl- ions outside the membrane have more negative charge than K+ and A- ions inside
    • 2Na+ and Cl- ions outside the membrane have less negative charge than K+ and A- ions inside
    • 3Na+ and A- ions outside the membrane have more negative charge than K+ and Cl- ions inside
    • 4Na+ and A- ions outside the membrane have less negative charge than K+ and Cl- ions inside
    ✓ Answer: (2) Na+ and Cl- ions outside the membrane have less negative charge than K+ and A- ions inside
    Ionic basis of the resting membrane potential
    The concept

    Why this oneAt rest, Na+ and Cl- are concentrated outside the neuron, while K+ and A- (large organic anions) are concentrated inside. The inside ends up more negative, about -70 mV.

    How it worksThe organic anions are proteins too large to leave the cell. The membrane leaks K+ out more easily than Na+ in, and the sodium-potassium pump pushes out 3 Na+ for every 2 K+ it brings in.

    Exam tipInside: K+ and A-. Outside: Na+ and Cl-. The resting potential is about -70 mV, inside relative to outside.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Na+ and Cl- ions outside the membrane have more negative charge than K+ and A- ions insideThe ion locations are right, but the charge comparison is reversed; the inside, not the outside, is the more negative side.
    • (3) Na+ and A- ions outside the membrane have more negative charge than K+ and Cl- ions insideOrganic anions (A-) are trapped inside the cell and Cl- is concentrated outside, so this ion distribution is wrong.
    • (4) Na+ and A- ions outside the membrane have less negative charge than K+ and Cl- ions insidePuts A- outside and Cl- inside; in fact the large protein anions stay inside and chloride is mostly outside.

  15. Descartes argued that body and mind interact. According to him, which is the site for this interaction?

    • 1Pancreas gland
    • 2Pineal gland
    • 3Pituitary gland
    • 4Adrenal gland
    ✓ Answer: (2) Pineal gland
    René Descartes, interactionist dualism
    The concept

    Why this oneRené Descartes held that the non-physical mind and the mechanical body interact at the pineal gland.

    Why the pinealHe thought it was the only brain structure that is single rather than paired, so it could unite the double input from the two sides of the body.

    Exam tipDescartes = interactionism; Baruch Spinoza = double aspect theory; Gottfried Leibniz = psychophysical parallelism.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Pancreas glandThe pancreas is a digestive and endocrine gland that secretes insulin; it plays no part in Descartes' theory.
    • (3) Pituitary glandThe pituitary is the master endocrine gland under the hypothalamus, not Descartes' seat of the soul.
    • (4) Adrenal glandThe adrenal glands sit on the kidneys and release adrenaline and cortisol in stress; Descartes did not link them to the mind.

  16. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Assertion (A): Sensitivity to targets reflects the placement of a flexible criterion and is measured in terms of 'hits minus false alarms'. Reason (R): When the consequences of making a Miss are very grave, we lower the criterion for considering something as a 'Hit'. In the light of the above two statements, choose the correct option:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true, but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false, but (R) is true
    ✓ Best answer: (4) (A) is false, but (R) is true
    David Green and John Swets, signal detection theory
    The concept

    AssertionFalse. In signal detection theory, sensitivity (d') reflects how well signal is separated from noise and does not depend on where the criterion is set; criterion placement is response bias (β).

    ReasonTrue. When a miss is very costly, such as missing a tumour on an X-ray, observers lower (relax) the criterion and say 'yes' more often, gaining hits at the cost of more false alarms.

    Exam tipPayoffs and expectations move the criterion; stimulus strength and the observer's acuity change d'. Hit rate minus false alarm rate is only a rough sensitivity index.

    Not certainIf the setter reads 'hits minus false alarms' as an acceptable sensitivity index and ignores the criterion clause, A passes as true and (2) would be chosen.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)A is false, because criterion placement reflects bias rather than sensitivity, so R cannot explain it.
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)A is not true, since sensitivity (d') is independent of where the criterion is placed; only R is correct.
    • (3) (A) is true, but (R) is falseReversed: A is false, while R is true, since costly misses push observers towards a lower, more lenient criterion.

  17. According to definition theory, for the development of a concept of an OFFICE, one must:

    • 1construct an ideal office
    • 2visit various offices
    • 3list all essential features of an office
    • 4transform a room into an office
    ✓ Answer: (3) list all essential features of an office
    Classical (defining-attribute) theory of concepts
    The concept

    Why this oneDefinition theory (the classical view) says a concept is a list of necessary and sufficient features; anything with all of them belongs, and anything lacking one does not.

    Rival theoriesPrototype theory (Eleanor Rosch) uses a best or typical example; exemplar theory stores many remembered examples and compares new items with them.

    Exam tipDefinition = checklist of essential features; prototype = typical average; exemplar = stored instances. Definition theory struggles with fuzzy categories such as 'game'.

    Old keyThis page used to mark this question as withdrawn. That key does not line up with this paper, so it is answered here like the rest.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) construct an ideal officeBuilding an ideal or typical office is prototype theory, where membership depends on similarity to the best example.
    • (2) visit various officesGathering experience of many particular offices fits exemplar theory, which compares new items with stored instances.
    • (4) transform a room into an officeConverting a room describes making an office, not how the concept is represented in the mind; no theory of concepts proposes it.

  18. Match the following Intelligence/Ability tests with the associated features.

    • List I: (a) Naglieri Non-verbal Ability test; (b) Raven's Progressive Matrices (Standard); (c) Torrance Test of Creative Thinking; (d) Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale
    • List II: (i) Provides deviation IQ; (ii) Open-ended test; (iii) Suitable for children; (iv) Adult Culture Fair Test

    Choose the correct option from those given below:

    • 1(a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    • 2(a)-(iv), (b)-(ii), (c)-(i), (d)-(iii)
    • 3(a)-(iv), (b)-(iii), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    • 4(a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(i), (d)-(ii)
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    Jack Naglieri, John Raven, E. Paul Torrance, David Wechsler
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Naglieri Nonverbal Ability Test = (iii) suitable for children; (b) Raven's Standard Progressive Matrices = (iv) adult culture-fair test; (c) Torrance Tests of Creative Thinking = (ii) open-ended; (d) WAIS = (i) deviation IQ.

    Non-verbal testsJack Naglieri's test is a non-verbal matrices test for school-age children. John Raven's Standard Matrices suit older children and adults; the Coloured version is for young children.

    Creativity and IQE. Paul Torrance's tasks are open-ended, scored for fluency, flexibility, originality and elaboration. David Wechsler replaced the ratio IQ with the deviation IQ (mean 100, SD 15).

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    • (2) (a)-(iv), (b)-(ii), (c)-(i), (d)-(iii)Calls Raven's open-ended and gives Torrance the deviation IQ; Raven's items have one right answer, while Torrance's divergent-thinking tasks are open-ended.
    • (3) (a)-(iv), (b)-(iii), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)Swaps (a) and (b); the Naglieri test is built for school-age children, while the Standard Raven's Matrices is the adult culture-fair test.
    • (4) (a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(i), (d)-(ii)Gives Torrance the deviation IQ and the WAIS the open-ended label; the WAIS yields deviation IQ and Torrance's creativity tasks are open-ended.

  19. Y has 'concrete experience' and 'reflective observation' as dominant learning abilities, views concrete situations from different viewpoints, has broad cultural interests and is imaginative and emotional. His experiential learning style is:

    • 1Assimilating style
    • 2Diverging style
    • 3Converging style
    • 4Accommodating style
    ✓ Answer: (2) Diverging style
    David Kolb, experiential learning theory (1984)
    The concept

    Why this oneDavid Kolb's diverging style combines concrete experience with reflective observation. Divergers view situations from many angles, brainstorm well, and are imaginative and emotional with broad cultural interests.

    Four stylesDiverging = CE + RO; assimilating = AC + RO; converging = AC + AE; accommodating = CE + AE (CE concrete experience, RO reflective observation, AC abstract conceptualisation, AE active experimentation).

    Exam tipKolb's learning cycle: concrete experience → reflective observation → abstract conceptualisation → active experimentation. Each style sits between two neighbouring stages.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Assimilating styleAssimilators combine abstract conceptualisation and reflective observation; they prefer logical theories and ideas to people and feelings.
    • (3) Converging styleConvergers combine abstract conceptualisation and active experimentation; they excel at practical problem-solving and prefer technical tasks to people.
    • (4) Accommodating styleAccommodators combine concrete experience and active experimentation; they learn hands-on, take risks and act on gut feeling.

  20. Match the given concepts with the relevant description.

    • List I: (a) Bottom up; (b) Top down; (c) Happiness set point; (d) Hedonic adaptation
    • List II: (i) Happiness due to circumstances tends to be temporary because we generally adjust fairly soon to new circumstances; (ii) Life circumstances influence happiness; (iii) Happiness begins in ourselves; (iv) Happiness levels are fixed and stable across time

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a)-(ii), (b)-(i), (c)-(iv), (d)-(iii)
    • 2(a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(i), (d)-(iv)
    • 3(a)-(iii), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)
    • 4(a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)
    ✓ Answer: (4) (a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)
    Ed Diener; Philip Brickman; David Lykken and Auke Tellegen
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Bottom up = (ii) life circumstances influence happiness; (b) Top down = (iii) happiness begins in ourselves; (c) Happiness set point = (iv) happiness levels are fixed and stable; (d) Hedonic adaptation = (i) we soon adjust to new circumstances.

    Two directionsBottom-up theories build happiness from good events and conditions; top-down theories (as reviewed by Ed Diener) say personality and outlook colour how we judge everything.

    Set pointThe set point idea (David Lykken and Auke Tellegen) holds that each person drifts back to a largely inherited baseline. Philip Brickman's studies of lottery winners illustrated hedonic adaptation.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(ii), (b)-(i), (c)-(iv), (d)-(iii)Mismatches (b) and (d): top down means happiness starts in the person, while hedonic adaptation means adjusting to new circumstances.
    • (2) (a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(i), (d)-(iv)Swaps (c) and (d): set point means a stable baseline (iv), while hedonic adaptation is the quick adjustment to new circumstances (i).
    • (3) (a)-(iii), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)Swaps bottom up and top down; bottom-up theories trace happiness to life circumstances, while top-down theories start from the person.

  21. The bipolar adjectives scale 'Fast……slow' in Osgood's semantic differential would belong to:

    • 1Evaluative factor
    • 2Potency factor
    • 3General factor
    • 4Activity factor
    ✓ Answer: (4) Activity factor
    Charles Osgood, semantic differential (1957)
    The concept

    Why this oneFast-slow describes motion and liveliness, so it loads on the activity factor.

    What it isCharles Osgood found three factors of meaning: evaluation (good-bad, pleasant-unpleasant), potency (strong-weak, heavy-light) and activity (fast-slow, active-passive).

    Exam tipRemember EPA: Evaluation, Potency, Activity. Evaluation accounts for the largest share of variance.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Evaluative factorThe evaluative factor covers judgements of worth, such as good-bad, nice-awful and pleasant-unpleasant.
    • (2) Potency factorThe potency factor covers strength and size, such as strong-weak, large-small and heavy-light.
    • (3) General factorOsgood's semantic differential has no general factor; its three factors are evaluation, potency and activity.

  22. Which of the following problem-solving techniques involves the analysis of the difference between the current situation and the desired set goal, then doing something to reduce this difference?

    • 1Heuristics
    • 2Sub goals
    • 3Means-end analysis
    • 4Brainstorming
    ✓ Answer: (3) Means-end analysis
    Allen Newell and Herbert Simon, means-end analysis
    The concept

    Why this oneMeans-end analysis compares the current state with the goal state, finds the biggest difference and picks an action to shrink it, repeating until the goal is reached.

    Where it comes fromAllen Newell and Herbert Simon built it into their General Problem Solver computer program; the Tower of Hanoi puzzle is a classic example.

    Exam tipIt is a heuristic that often creates subgoals, but its defining feature is reducing the gap between where you are and where you want to be.

    Old keyThis page used to show (4) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) HeuristicsHeuristics are the broad family of rule-of-thumb shortcuts; means-end analysis is one specific heuristic, so this answer is too general.
    • (2) Sub goalsSetting subgoals breaks a problem into smaller steps; it is used within means-end analysis but does not itself compare current and goal states.
    • (4) BrainstormingBrainstorming (Alex Osborn) generates many ideas without criticism, usually in a group; it does not measure the gap to a goal.

  23. According to the Decision Stage Model, how many steps are comprised in taking a decision?

    • 1Three
    • 2Four
    • 3Five
    • 4Six
    ✓ Best answer: (3) Five
    Irving Janis and Leon Mann, decision-making model (1977)
    The concept

    Why this oneIrving Janis and Leon Mann described sound decision-making as passing through five stages.

    Correct order(1) Appraising the challenge → (2) surveying alternatives → (3) weighing alternatives → (4) deliberating about commitment → (5) adhering despite negative feedback.

    Exam tipJanis and Mann also named vigilance as the ideal coping pattern, in contrast to defensive avoidance and hypervigilance (panic).

    Not certain'Decision Stage Model' is not a fixed textbook name; Herbert Simon's three phases (intelligence, design, choice) would give (1), and a four-stage group model (orientation, discussion, decision, implementation) would give (2).

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) ThreeThree matches Herbert Simon's intelligence, design and choice phases, not the five-stage decision model.
    • (2) FourFour is the count in some group decision models (orientation, discussion, decision, implementation), not in the five-stage decision model.
    • (4) SixSix steps describe the rational decision-making model of organisational behaviour texts, not the five-stage psychological model.

  24. Which of the following explains the tendency of traits that are encoded on the same chromosome to be inherited together?

    • 1Mitosis
    • 2Meiosis
    • 3Linkage
    • 4Gene expression
    ✓ Answer: (3) Linkage
    Genetic linkage (Thomas Hunt Morgan, fruit flies)
    The concept

    Why this oneGenes that lie close together on the same chromosome tend to be passed on as a unit; this is called linkage.

    How it worksDuring meiosis, crossing over can separate linked genes. The closer two genes are, the less often they are split, so the more often they are inherited together.

    Exam tipLinkage is an exception to Gregor Mendel's law of independent assortment, which holds for genes on different chromosomes or far apart on one.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) MitosisMitosis is ordinary cell division that produces two identical body cells; it does not explain which traits travel together across generations.
    • (2) MeiosisMeiosis forms sex cells and is when assortment happens, but the tendency of same-chromosome genes to stay together is called linkage.
    • (4) Gene expressionGene expression is the process by which a gene's code is turned into a protein and becomes active; it does not explain inheritance patterns.

  25. Arrange the following in temporal sequence.

    • (a) Establishment of Psychology department in Mysore
    • (b) Establishment of Psychology department in Calcutta
    • (c) Establishment of Psychology department in Madras
    • (d) Establishment of Psychology department in Pune
    • (e) First Indian Journal of Psychology

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(b) → (a) → (c) → (e) → (d)
    • 2(b) → (a) → (e) → (c) → (d)
    • 3(b) → (c) → (a) → (d) → (e)
    • 4(a) → (b) → (e) → (c) → (d)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (b) → (a) → (e) → (c) → (d)
    Narendra Nath Sengupta; history of psychology in India
    The concept

    Correct order(b) Calcutta University (1915, the first in India) → (a) Mysore (1924) → (e) Indian Journal of Psychology (1926) → (c) Madras → (d) Pune.

    Early landmarksNarendra Nath Sengupta set up the first experimental psychology department and laboratory at Calcutta University. M. V. Gopalaswamy started psychology at Mysore.

    The journalThe Indian Psychological Association was formed in 1925 and launched the Indian Journal of Psychology in 1926. Departments at Madras and Pune came later.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (b) → (a) → (c) → (e) → (d)Puts Madras before the Indian Journal of Psychology; the journal (1926) came first, before the Madras department was set up.
    • (3) (b) → (c) → (a) → (d) → (e)Places Madras before Mysore and the journal last; Mysore (1924) and the journal (1926) both came before Madras and Pune.
    • (4) (a) → (b) → (e) → (c) → (d)Starts with Mysore, but Calcutta University had the first psychology department in India, before Mysore.

  26. Choose the odd one for the pattern of sympathetic activation during emotions.

    • 1Increased heart rate
    • 2Pupil constriction
    • 3Increased breathing rate
    • 4Lowered skin resistance
    ✓ Answer: (2) Pupil constriction
    Walter Cannon, sympathetic fight-or-flight response
    The concept

    Why this oneIn emotion the sympathetic branch dilates the pupils to let in more light. Pupil constriction is a parasympathetic effect, so it is the odd one out.

    How it worksThe sympathetic system prepares the body for fight or flight: faster heart and breathing, more sweating, blood sent to the muscles, digestion slowed.

    Exam tipLowered skin resistance means more sweat on the skin; this is the galvanic skin response recorded by the polygraph.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are not the answer
    • (1) Increased heart rateA faster heartbeat pumps more blood to the muscles; it is a classic sympathetic sign and fits the pattern.
    • (3) Increased breathing rateFaster breathing brings in more oxygen for action; it is a sympathetic effect and fits the pattern.
    • (4) Lowered skin resistanceSympathetic activation of sweat glands lowers skin resistance (raises skin conductance); it fits the pattern and is what the polygraph records.

  27. A psychological currency that permits deviation from the group is called:

    • 1Conformity
    • 2Obedience
    • 3Idiosyncrasy credit
    • 4Compliance
    ✓ Answer: (3) Idiosyncrasy credit
    Edwin Hollander, idiosyncrasy credit
    The concept

    Why this oneIdiosyncrasy credit is the goodwill a member earns by conforming and contributing to the group. It can later be 'spent' to deviate without being rejected.

    How it worksLeaders and high-status members build up credits over time, so the group accepts their new ideas. A newcomer who deviates early has no credit and is punished.

    Exam tipEdwin Hollander's idea explains how an accepted leader can bring change: first conform, then innovate.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) ConformityChanging one's behaviour or beliefs to match group norms; it is the opposite of deviating, not a credit that allows it.
    • (2) ObedienceFollowing a direct command from an authority figure, as in Stanley Milgram's studies; it involves no store of credit.
    • (4) ComplianceGoing along with a direct request (foot-in-the-door, door-in-the-face), often without private agreement; it is not a licence to deviate.

  28. Which of the following is excluded from Berkowitz's frustration-aggression hypothesis?

    • 1Frustration always leads to aggression.
    • 2Social rules may inhibit aggression.
    • 3Frustration may result in other behaviour than aggression.
    • 4Cognitive factors can override aggression.
    ✓ Answer: (1) Frustration always leads to aggression.
    Leonard Berkowitz, revised frustration-aggression hypothesis
    The concept

    Why this oneThe original 1939 hypothesis of John Dollard, Neal Miller and colleagues said frustration always leads to aggression. Leonard Berkowitz dropped this claim.

    What Berkowitz saidFrustration produces negative affect (anger) and a readiness to aggress. Whether aggression follows depends on aggressive cues, learning, social rules and thinking.

    Exam tipIn his later cognitive neoassociation model, higher-order thinking (appraisal, attribution) can restrain or redirect the urge to aggress.

    Old keyThis page used to show (4) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are not the answer
    • (2) Social rules may inhibit aggression.Included: norms and fear of punishment can hold back aggression even when a person is frustrated, which the revised view accepts.
    • (3) Frustration may result in other behaviour than aggression.Included: frustration can lead to other responses such as withdrawal or problem solving, so aggression is only one possible outcome.
    • (4) Cognitive factors can override aggression.Included: in Berkowitz's cognitive neoassociation model, appraisal and higher-order thinking can override the first aggressive urge.

  29. Choose the correct sequence of events following prolonged stress.

    • (a) Corticotropin releasing factor
    • (b) Adrenocorticotrophic hormone
    • (c) Cortisol
    • (d) Immune suppression

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a) → (b) → (c) → (d)
    • 2(d) → (c) → (a) → (b)
    • 3(a) → (c) → (d) → (b)
    • 4(b) → (c) → (a) → (d)
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a) → (b) → (c) → (d)
    Hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis
    The concept

    Correct order(a) The hypothalamus releases CRF → (b) the anterior pituitary releases ACTH → (c) the adrenal cortex releases cortisol → (d) prolonged high cortisol suppresses the immune system.

    How it worksCortisol raises blood sugar and damps inflammation. That helps in the short term, but when stress lasts it lowers lymphocyte activity and resistance to infection.

    Exam tipThis is the slow HPA axis; the fast route is the sympathetic-adrenal medullary system releasing adrenaline. Link it to Hans Selye's general adaptation syndrome.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (d) → (c) → (a) → (b)Starts with immune suppression, which is the end result of the chain; CRF must come first to start the hormonal cascade.
    • (3) (a) → (c) → (d) → (b)Puts cortisol before ACTH, but the adrenal cortex releases cortisol only after ACTH from the pituitary reaches it.
    • (4) (b) → (c) → (a) → (d)Starts with ACTH, but the pituitary releases ACTH only after the hypothalamus sends CRF.

  30. Imaginal disputation by using the ability of a client to imagine is a method of:

    • 1REBT
    • 2Existential Confrontation
    • 3Gestalt therapy Confrontation
    • 4Mindfulness
    ✓ Answer: (1) REBT
    Albert Ellis, rational emotive behaviour therapy
    The concept

    Why this oneImaginal disputation is an REBT technique: the client imagines a troubling event and, while still imagining it, disputes the irrational belief behind the upset.

    How it worksIn rational emotive imagery the client pictures the worst case, feels the unhealthy emotion, then changes the self-talk so it becomes a healthy one (concern instead of anxiety).

    Exam tipAlbert Ellis's ABCDE: Activating event, Belief, Consequence, Disputing, Effect. Disputing can be cognitive, imaginal or behavioural.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Existential ConfrontationExistential therapists (Irvin Yalom, Rollo May) confront clients with death, freedom, isolation and meaninglessness; they do not use imagery to dispute beliefs.
    • (3) Gestalt therapy ConfrontationGestalt therapy (Fritz Perls) confronts avoidance in the here and now with the empty chair and dialogue; it does not dispute beliefs.
    • (4) MindfulnessMindfulness trains non-judgemental awareness of thoughts as they are, rather than arguing with or disputing them.

  31. Using Hull's theory, Taylor conceptualized that anxiety is an emotionally based drive and therefore high anxious subjects on Taylor's Manifest Anxiety Scale (MAS) should be conditioned faster than low anxious subjects. The study verified the hypothesis. This provides evidence for:

    • 1Concurrent validity of MAS
    • 2Construct validity of MAS
    • 3Convergent validity of MAS
    • 4Lack of validity for MAS
    ✓ Answer: (2) Construct validity of MAS
    Janet Taylor's MAS; construct validation (Anne Anastasi)
    The concept

    Why this oneThe MAS was built on Clark Hull's drive theory. Showing that high scorers condition faster, as the theory predicts, supports the scale as a measure of the construct anxiety-as-drive.

    What it isConstruct validity is how far a test measures a theoretical trait. It is shown by deriving predictions from the theory and testing them, often experimentally.

    Exam tipConcurrent validity = correlation with a criterion measured at the same time. Convergent validity = high correlation with other measures of the same trait (Campbell and Fiske).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Concurrent validity of MASCorrelating the MAS with a criterion measured at the same time, such as clinicians' ratings; no theory-based experimental prediction is involved.
    • (3) Convergent validity of MASShown by high correlations with other anxiety tests; this study tested a prediction from drive theory, not overlap with other tests.
    • (4) Lack of validity for MASThe prediction was confirmed, so the result supports the scale; lack of validity would follow only if high and low scorers conditioned alike.

  32. During the 1960s, Hoffman and others criticised the college entrance examination and other nationally administered educational tests, and argued that:

    • (a) These favour shrewd and nimble witted
    • (b) These are over concerned with the expression of skill than the quality of thought behind the answer
    • (c) These favour subtle, creative and more profound persons
    • (d) These carry a bad effect in recognition of merit.

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1only (a) is correct
    • 2(a) and (d) are correct
    • 3only (c) is correct
    • 4(c) and (d) are correct
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a) and (d) are correct
    Banesh Hoffman, The Tyranny of Testing (1962)
    The concept

    Why this oneBanesh Hoffman argued that multiple-choice tests favour the shrewd and nimble-witted (a) and have a bad effect on education and the recognition of merit (d).

    Why others false(b) twists his point: he said the tests score only the chosen answer, ignore the quality of thought behind it and neglect skill in expression. (c) is the reverse: he said they penalise the subtle, creative, more profound person.

    ContextHis attack on the multiple-choice format fed the 1960s debate on standardised testing that Anne Anastasi reviews.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) only (a) is correct(a) is true, but (d) is also one of Hoffman's charges: that such tests harm education and the recognition of merit.
    • (3) only (c) is correct(c) inverts Hoffman: he said the tests penalise the subtle, creative and profound candidate, not favour them.
    • (4) (c) and (d) are correct(d) is true but (c) is false; Hoffman held that creative, profound thinkers are the ones these tests penalise.

  33. Arrange in sequence the scoring categories for 'Motive to Avoid Success', as given below.

    • (a) Non-contingent negative consequences
    • (b) Interpersonal engagement
    • (c) Relief
    • (d) Absence of Instrumental conditioning
    • (e) Absence of others

    Which of the sequences given below is correct?

    • 1(a) → (b) → (d) → (c) → (e)
    • 2(b) → (a) → (c) → (e) → (d)
    • 3(a) → (b) → (c) → (d) → (e)
    • 4(c) → (d) → (e) → (b) → (a)
    ✓ Best answer: (3) (a) → (b) → (c) → (d) → (e)
    Matina Horner, motive to avoid success (fear of success)
    The concept

    Why this oneThe revised, empirically derived scoring system for the motive to avoid success lists its categories in the order given: non-contingent negative consequences, interpersonal engagement, relief, absence of instrumental activity, absence of others.

    What it isMatina Horner found that many women, writing stories to the cue 'Anne finds herself at the top of her medical school class', imagined negative consequences of success. She called this fear of success.

    How it is scoredLike David McClelland's n Ach, it is scored by coding imagery in TAT-type stories against set categories.

    Not certainThis comes from a niche scoring manual not covered in standard textbooks; option (1), which swaps relief and absence of instrumental activity, is the runner-up.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) → (b) → (d) → (c) → (e)Swaps relief and absence of instrumental activity; in the scoring system relief is listed before absence of instrumental activity.
    • (2) (b) → (a) → (c) → (e) → (d)Starts with interpersonal engagement, but non-contingent negative consequences heads the list of categories.
    • (4) (c) → (d) → (e) → (b) → (a)Runs almost backwards, ending with non-contingent negative consequences, which is the first category listed.

  34. Match the following diseases with their corresponding descriptions.

    • List I: (a) Parkinson's Disease; (b) Huntington's Disease; (c) Alzheimer's Disease; (d) Korsakoff Syndrome
    • List II: (i) Movement disorder associated with cognitive deficits; (ii) Progressive motor disorder associated with severe dementia; (iii) Progressive decline in memory starting with selective forgetting; (iv) A form of dementia caused by deficiency of vitamin Thiamine

    Choose the correct option from those given below:

    • 1(a)-(ii), (b)-(i), (c)-(iv), (d)-(iii)
    • 2(a)-(i), (b)-(iv), (c)-(iii), (d)-(ii)
    • 3(a)-(i), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(iv)
    • 4(a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a)-(i), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(iv)
    Neurodegenerative and amnesic disorders (John Pinel)
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Parkinson's = (i) movement disorder with cognitive deficits; (b) Huntington's = (ii) progressive motor disorder with severe dementia; (c) Alzheimer's = (iii) progressive memory decline starting with selective forgetting; (d) Korsakoff = (iv) thiamine deficiency dementia.

    Key factsParkinson's: loss of dopamine neurons in the substantia nigra, with tremor, rigidity and slowness. Huntington's: a dominant gene, chorea, ending in severe dementia.

    KorsakoffSeen in chronic alcoholism: thiamine (vitamin B1) loss damages the mammillary bodies and medial thalamus, causing amnesia with confabulation.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(ii), (b)-(i), (c)-(iv), (d)-(iii)Swaps Parkinson's and Huntington's and puts thiamine with Alzheimer's; thiamine deficiency causes Korsakoff syndrome, not Alzheimer's.
    • (2) (a)-(i), (b)-(iv), (c)-(iii), (d)-(ii)Gives Huntington's the thiamine link and Korsakoff the motor disorder; Huntington's is genetic chorea, Korsakoff is alcohol-related thiamine loss.
    • (4) (a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)Pairs Alzheimer's with thiamine and Korsakoff with a movement disorder; both are wrong, and Huntington's is a motor disorder with dementia.

  35. Arrange the Ego Strengths in correct sequence according to Erikson's stages of development.

    • 1Hope → purpose → competence → fidelity → love → care → wisdom → will
    • 2Will → hope → purpose → competence → fidelity → care → wisdom → love
    • 3Hope → will → purpose → competence → fidelity → love → care → wisdom
    • 4Purpose → hope → will → fidelity → competence → care → wisdom → love
    ✓ Answer: (3) Hope → will → purpose → competence → fidelity → love → care → wisdom
    Erik Erikson, psychosocial stages and basic virtues
    The concept

    Correct orderHope (trust vs mistrust) → will (autonomy vs shame) → purpose (initiative vs guilt) → competence (industry vs inferiority) → fidelity (identity vs role confusion) → love (intimacy vs isolation) → care (generativity vs stagnation) → wisdom (integrity vs despair).

    What they areErik Erikson called each ego strength the basic virtue that emerges when a stage crisis is resolved well.

    Exam tipMemory aid: 'Hope Will Purposely Compete For Love, Care and Wisdom'.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Hope → purpose → competence → fidelity → love → care → wisdom → willPuts will last, but will (autonomy vs shame and doubt) is the second virtue, just after hope; wisdom is the final one.
    • (2) Will → hope → purpose → competence → fidelity → care → wisdom → loveStarts with will and ends with love; hope comes first, and love (intimacy) comes before care and wisdom.
    • (4) Purpose → hope → will → fidelity → competence → care → wisdom → loveStarts with purpose and puts love last; the sequence begins with hope in infancy and ends with wisdom in old age.

  36. The Generational Digital or Gen D factor:

    • (a) pertains to reversal of power hierarchy in terms of digital technology
    • (b) pertains to imparting knowledge downward to the younger generation
    • (c) calls for psychoeducation of parents and caregivers on digital technology usage
    • (d) calls for the adolescent psychoeducation on digital technology usage

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1Only (a)
    • 2Only (b)
    • 3(a) and (c)
    • 4(b) and (d)
    ✓ Best answer: (3) (a) and (c)
    Generational digital divide (Gen D factor)
    The concept

    Why this oneThe Gen D factor describes a reversal of the usual hierarchy: the young know digital technology better than their parents (a). So the remedy targets the adults: psychoeducation of parents and caregivers (c).

    Why others false(b) is the traditional direction of teaching; with digital technology, knowledge flows upward from child to parent. (d) misses the point, since the gap lies with the adults who must guide and monitor use.

    Not certainGen D is a niche term from writing on digital media, not a standard textbook; if (c) is read as a separate recommendation rather than part of the idea, option (1) is the runner-up.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Only (a)(a) is right, but the reversed hierarchy is exactly why parents and caregivers need psychoeducation, so (c) also belongs.
    • (2) Only (b)Knowledge passing down to the young is the traditional pattern; the Gen D factor describes the reverse, so (b) is false.
    • (4) (b) and (d)Both parts miss the reversal: knowledge goes upward, and the adults, who lag behind, are the ones who need psychoeducation.

  37. Involvement of the frontal lobe in intelligence can be understood in terms of which of the following sequences?

    • 1Frontal lobe → Focusing attention internally → Working memory → General reasoning
    • 2Frontal lobe → Focusing attention internally → Response selection → General reasoning
    • 3Frontal lobe → Short term memory → Working memory → General reasoning
    • 4Frontal lobe → Response selection → Working memory → General reasoning
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Frontal lobe → Focusing attention internally → Working memory → General reasoning
    Michael Kane and Randall Engle, executive attention
    The concept

    Why this oneThe prefrontal cortex lets a person hold attention on internal representations and block distraction. This controlled attention sets working memory capacity, which in turn predicts general (fluid) reasoning.

    How it worksMichael Kane and Randall Engle argue that working memory capacity is largely executive attention; John Duncan found lateral frontal cortex active across high-g tasks.

    Exam tipShort-term memory is passive storage; working memory adds active control. That is why working memory tasks correlate more strongly with g.

    Not certainThe chain comes from research papers rather than one standard textbook; option (4), with response selection as the frontal step, is the runner-up because the frontal lobe also selects responses.

    Old keyThis page used to show (4) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Frontal lobe → Focusing attention internally → Response selection → General reasoningResponse selection is a frontal output function, but the route to reasoning runs through working memory, which this chain leaves out.
    • (3) Frontal lobe → Short term memory → Working memory → General reasoningShort-term memory is passive storage, not the frontal contribution; the frontal lobe adds attentional control, which turns storage into working memory.
    • (4) Frontal lobe → Response selection → Working memory → General reasoningResponse selection is a frontal function, but the link from frontal lobe to working memory is internally focused attention, not picking responses.

  38. Which of the following are the two major symptoms of Alzheimer's disease?

    • 1Neurofibrillary tangles and Amyloid plaques
    • 2Collateral sprouting and cerebral ischemia
    • 3Amyloid plaques and cerebral ischemia
    • 4Neurofibrillary tangles and collateral sprouting
