    ✓ Answer: (1) Neurofibrillary tangles and Amyloid plaques
    Alois Alzheimer; plaques and tangles
    The concept

    Why this oneThe two defining brain signs of Alzheimer's disease are amyloid plaques (clumps of beta-amyloid between neurons) and neurofibrillary tangles (twisted tau threads inside neurons).

    How it worksPlaques and tangles first build up in the medial temporal lobe (entorhinal cortex, hippocampus), which is why memory goes first. Acetylcholine neurons of the basal forebrain are also lost.

    Exam tipAlois Alzheimer first described plaques and tangles; traditionally a definite diagnosis needs them to be found at autopsy.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Collateral sprouting and cerebral ischemiaCollateral sprouting is regrowth of axon branches after injury, and cerebral ischaemia is loss of blood supply (stroke); neither is an Alzheimer's hallmark.
    • (3) Amyloid plaques and cerebral ischemiaPlaques are a hallmark, but ischaemia (blocked blood flow) causes stroke and vascular dementia, not Alzheimer's disease.
    • (4) Neurofibrillary tangles and collateral sproutingTangles are a hallmark, but collateral sprouting is a recovery process after neural damage, not a sign of Alzheimer's.

  39. Which of the following are the advantages of fMRI over PET scan?

    • (a) Nothing has to be injected into the body
    • (b) Provides both structural and functional information of the same image
    • (c) Produces three-dimensional images of activity over the entire brain

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1Only (a) and (b)
    • 2Only (a) and (c)
    • 3Only (b) and (c)
    • 4(a), (b) and (c)
    ✓ Answer: (4) (a), (b) and (c)
    John Pinel, Biopsychology: fMRI versus PET
    The concept

    Why this oneJohn Pinel lists the advantages of fMRI over PET: nothing is injected, it gives structural and functional information in the same image, its spatial resolution is better, and it can produce 3-D images of activity over the whole brain. So (a), (b) and (c) are all true.

    How it worksfMRI tracks the BOLD signal, the rise in oxygenated blood in active areas. PET needs a radioactive tracer injected into the blood.

    Exam tipPET keeps one strength: with labelled ligands it can image specific chemicals and receptors.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Only (a) and (b)(a) and (b) are right, but (c) is also an fMRI advantage Pinel lists: 3-D images of activity across the whole brain.
    • (2) Only (a) and (c)(a) and (c) are right, but (b) is too: fMRI shows structure and function in the same image, while PET shows only activity.
    • (3) Only (b) and (c)(b) and (c) are right, but (a) is the most cited advantage: fMRI needs no injected radioactive tracer.

  40. Feminist theory helped in:

    • 1dealing with gender inequality
    • 2understanding sexual preferences
    • 3human growth and development
    • 4gender issues
    ✓ Best answer: (1) dealing with gender inequality
    Feminist theory and feminist therapy (Gerald Corey)
    The concept

    Why this oneFeminist theory began as a challenge to gender inequality: unequal power, rights and opportunities for women. In therapy its slogan is 'the personal is political'.

    How it worksFeminist therapists such as Jean Baker Miller look at how gender-role socialisation and oppression shape distress, and aim for an egalitarian therapist-client relationship.

    Exam tipGerald Corey lists empowerment, valuing women's experience and social change among its goals.

    Not certainOption (4) 'gender issues' is broad and also true; (1) is chosen because challenging gender inequality is the defining aim of feminist theory.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) understanding sexual preferencesSexual preferences are studied in sexology and research on sexual orientation; feminist theory centres on power and gender, not sexual preference.
    • (3) human growth and developmentHuman development is the field of developmental psychology (Jean Piaget, Erik Erikson); feminists such as Carol Gilligan criticised such theories for male bias but did not build them.
    • (4) gender issuesVery broad; feminist theory does deal with gender issues, but its specific contribution is exposing and challenging gender inequality.

  41. "Value of behaviour to the organism, its evolutionary history, and its causation in terms of stimulus, hormonal and neurological events" has been an area of study of which of the following?

    • 1Evolutionary psychology
    • 2Psychoanalysis
    • 3Ethology
    • 4Instinct Psychology
    ✓ Answer: (3) Ethology
    Niko Tinbergen, four questions of ethology
    The concept

    Why this oneEthology, the biological study of animal behaviour in natural settings, asks about a behaviour's survival value, its evolutionary history and its immediate causes (stimuli, hormones, nerves).

    How it worksNiko Tinbergen's four questions: causation (mechanism), development, function (survival value) and evolution. Konrad Lorenz and Karl von Frisch shared the 1973 Nobel Prize with him.

    Exam tipClassic ethology terms: fixed action pattern, sign stimulus, imprinting.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Evolutionary psychologyEvolutionary psychology (David Buss, Leda Cosmides) explains human mental mechanisms as adaptations; it does not study hormonal and neural causes of animal behaviour.
    • (2) PsychoanalysisSigmund Freud's psychoanalysis studies unconscious conflict and childhood experience, not evolutionary history or hormonal causes of behaviour.
    • (4) Instinct PsychologyWilliam McDougall's instinct psychology listed inborn instincts as the motives of behaviour; it did not study evolutionary history or neural causation.

  42. Which part of the brain is responsible for transferring information from Short Term Memory to Long Term Memory?

    • 1Amygdala
    • 2Hypothalamus
    • 3Hippocampus
    • 4Cerebellum
    ✓ Answer: (3) Hippocampus
    Hippocampal consolidation; patient H.M. (Brenda Milner)
    The concept

    Why this oneThe hippocampus consolidates new information, moving it from short-term into long-term memory.

    EvidencePatient H.M. (Henry Molaison) lost both hippocampi in epilepsy surgery. His short-term memory and old memories stayed, but he could not form new long-term declarative memories (anterograde amnesia).

    Exam tipAmygdala = emotional memory; cerebellum = motor skills and conditioned reflexes; hypothalamus = drives such as hunger and thirst.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) AmygdalaThe amygdala adds emotional weight to memories, especially fear; it does not transfer general information into long-term memory.
    • (2) HypothalamusThe hypothalamus regulates hunger, thirst, temperature, sex and the pituitary; it is not the memory consolidation centre.
    • (4) CerebellumThe cerebellum handles motor coordination, procedural skills and eyeblink conditioning, not consolidation of declarative facts.

  43. For becoming a good basketball player, one must use:

    • 1Algorithms
    • 2Heuristics
    • 3Concepts
    • 4Prototypes
    ✓ Answer: (2) Heuristics
    Heuristics versus algorithms in problem solving
    The concept

    Why this oneBasketball needs split-second decisions in a changing game, so a player relies on heuristics: quick rules of thumb learned from experience, such as 'pass when double-teamed'.

    What it isAn algorithm is a step-by-step procedure that guarantees a solution but is slow. A heuristic is a shortcut that is fast and usually works, though it can fail.

    Exam tipGerd Gigerenzer's gaze heuristic (keep the angle of gaze to the ball constant) explains how fielders catch a ball without computing its path.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) AlgorithmsAlgorithms are exhaustive step-by-step procedures that guarantee an answer, as in maths; they are far too slow for fast, changing game situations.
    • (3) ConceptsConcepts are mental categories that group objects and ideas; they organise knowledge but are not a strategy for split-second play.
    • (4) PrototypesPrototypes are the most typical examples of a concept (Eleanor Rosch); they help with categorising, not quick decisions in a game.

  44. Which of the following correctly describe Nafs (Self or ego) in Sufism?

    • (a) It can be viewed along a continuum
    • (b) It is not meant to please God
    • (c) It refers to negative traits only
    • (d) It has a potential to function from the grossest to the highest level

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a) and (c) are correct
    • 2(b) and (c) are correct
    • 3(b) and (d) are correct
    • 4(a) and (d) are correct
    ✓ Answer: (4) (a) and (d) are correct
    Sufi psychology: stages of the nafs
    The concept

    Why this oneSufis see the nafs as a continuum (a) that can rise from the grossest, self-centred level to the highest, purified level (d).

    StagesFrom nafs al-ammara (the commanding self that urges evil) → nafs al-lawwama (the self-reproaching self) → nafs al-mutma'inna (the self at peace), and on to the self that is content with God and pleasing to God.

    Why others false(b) is false: the higher nafs is pleasing to God. (c) is false: only the lowest level is negative, and the nafs can be refined.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) and (c) are correct(a) is right but (c) is not; only the lowest level, nafs al-ammara, is negative, while higher levels are calm and pure.
    • (2) (b) and (c) are correctBoth are false: the purified nafs is described as pleasing to God, and the nafs is not limited to negative traits.
    • (3) (b) and (d) are correct(d) is right but (b) is not; the aim of Sufi practice is a nafs that is content with God and pleasing to Him.

  45. Match the waves with the corresponding frequencies.

    • List I: (a) Alpha waves; (b) Beta waves; (c) Theta waves; (d) Delta waves
    • List II: (i) 4–7 Hz; (ii) 14–30 Hz; (iii) 8–13 Hz; (iv) 0–3 Hz

    Choose the correct option from those given below:

    • 1(a)-(iii), (b)-(ii), (c)-(i), (d)-(iv)
    • 2(a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(i), (d)-(iv)
    • 3(a)-(iii), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)
    • 4(a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a)-(iii), (b)-(ii), (c)-(i), (d)-(iv)
    EEG frequency bands (Hans Berger)
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Alpha = (iii) 8 to 13 Hz; (b) Beta = (ii) 14 to 30 Hz; (c) Theta = (i) 4 to 7 Hz; (d) Delta = (iv) 0 to 3 Hz.

    What they meanBeta = alert, active thinking; alpha = relaxed wakefulness with eyes closed; theta = drowsiness and light sleep; delta = deep slow-wave sleep.

    Exam tipFrequency falls as arousal falls: beta, then alpha, then theta, then delta.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(i), (d)-(iv)Swaps alpha and beta; beta is the faster 14 to 30 Hz rhythm of alert thinking, alpha the 8 to 13 Hz relaxed rhythm.
    • (3) (a)-(iii), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)Alpha and beta are right, but theta and delta are swapped; delta is the slowest wave (0 to 3 Hz), seen in deep sleep.
    • (4) (a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)Swaps both pairs: alpha and beta are reversed, and so are theta and delta.

  46. Which of the following is the most common anti-obesity hormone?

    • 1Leptin
    • 2Orexin
    • 3Insulin
    • 4Ghrelin
    ✓ Answer: (1) Leptin
    Leptin, the fat-cell satiety hormone
    The concept

    Why this oneLeptin is released by fat cells in proportion to body fat. It acts on the hypothalamus to cut food intake and raise energy use, so it is called the anti-obesity hormone.

    Evidenceob/ob mice cannot make leptin and become very obese; leptin injections make them eat less and lose weight.

    Exam tipMost obese people have high leptin but are leptin resistant, so leptin does not cure common obesity. Ghrelin from the stomach is the hunger hormone.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) OrexinOrexin (hypocretin) from the lateral hypothalamus promotes wakefulness and food seeking; its loss causes narcolepsy, and it does not fight obesity.
    • (3) InsulinInsulin from the pancreas lowers blood glucose and promotes fat storage; it also signals satiety, but it is not the main anti-obesity hormone.
    • (4) GhrelinGhrelin is released by an empty stomach and stimulates hunger; it is the hunger hormone, the opposite of an anti-obesity signal.

  47. Arrange the following in sequence as per Atkinson and Shiffrin's model of memory when the information is processed.

    • (a) Short term memory
    • (b) Elaborative rehearsal
    • (c) Long term memory
    • (d) Attention (in Sensory memory)
    • (e) Sensory memory
    • 1(d) → (e) → (a) → (b) → (c)
    • 2(e) → (a) → (d) → (b) → (c)
    • 3(e) → (d) → (a) → (b) → (c)
    • 4(d) → (e) → (a) → (b) → (c)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (e) → (d) → (a) → (b) → (c)
    Richard Atkinson and Richard Shiffrin, multi-store model (1968)
    The concept

    Correct order(e) Sensory memory holds all input briefly → (d) attention selects part of it → (a) short-term memory → (b) elaborative rehearsal links it to meaning → (c) long-term memory.

    How it worksUnattended sensory traces fade within about a second (iconic) or a few seconds (echoic). STM holds about 7 ± 2 items for around 20 seconds unless rehearsed.

    Exam tipMaintenance rehearsal keeps an item in STM; elaborative rehearsal (deep processing, Fergus Craik and Robert Lockhart) gets it into LTM.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (d) → (e) → (a) → (b) → (c)Puts attention before sensory memory; information must enter sensory memory before attention can select any of it.
    • (2) (e) → (a) → (d) → (b) → (c)Puts attention after short-term memory; attention is the gate that moves information from sensory memory into STM.
    • (4) (d) → (e) → (a) → (b) → (c)Same order as option (1): attention cannot come before the sensory register it selects from.

  48. Which of the following is the explanation of the Premack principle?

    • 1A primary reinforcement is thought of as being related to an organism's survival
    • 2A secondary reinforcer acquires reinforcing value because of pairing with a primary reinforcer
    • 3All responses are potential reinforcers
    • 4A more frequently occurring activity can be used to reinforce an activity that occurs less often
    ✓ Answer: (4) A more frequently occurring activity can be used to reinforce an activity that occurs less often
    David Premack, Premack principle
    The concept

    Why this oneDavid Premack showed that a high-probability behaviour can reinforce a low-probability one: 'finish your homework, then you can play'.

    How it worksReinforcers are treated as activities, not things. Whatever a person prefers to do can be made contingent on a less preferred activity.

    Exam tipAlso called Grandma's rule. Its refinement, response deprivation theory, says any activity restricted below its usual level can act as a reinforcer.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A primary reinforcement is thought of as being related to an organism's survivalDescribes a primary reinforcer such as food or water, which satisfies a biological need without any learning.
    • (2) A secondary reinforcer acquires reinforcing value because of pairing with a primary reinforcerDescribes a secondary (conditioned) reinforcer such as money or praise, which gains value from pairing with primary reinforcers.
    • (3) All responses are potential reinforcersOverstates the idea: an activity reinforces only a less probable activity, so reinforcing power depends on relative probability, not on simply being a response.

  49. Arrange in sequence the network of auditory pathways.

    • (a) Auditory Nerve
    • (b) Cochlear Nuclei
    • (c) Superior Olives
    • (d) Lateral Lemniscus
    • (e) Inferior Colliculi
    • (f) Medial Geniculate Nuclei
    • (g) Primary Auditory Cortex

    Choose the correct sequence:

    • 1(a) → (c) → (b) → (d) → (e) → (g) → (f)
    • 2(a) → (b) → (c) → (d) → (e) → (f) → (g)
    • 3(b) → (a) → (d) → (c) → (g) → (e) → (f)
    • 4(f) → (g) → (e) → (d) → (c) → (b) → (a)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a) → (b) → (c) → (d) → (e) → (f) → (g)
    Ascending auditory pathway (Pinel, Carlson)
    The concept

    Correct order(a) Auditory nerve → (b) cochlear nuclei (medulla) → (c) superior olives → (d) lateral lemniscus → (e) inferior colliculi (midbrain) → (f) medial geniculate nuclei (thalamus) → (g) primary auditory cortex (temporal lobe).

    How it worksThe superior olive is the first point where input from both ears meets, so it compares timing and loudness between the ears to locate sounds.

    Exam tipMedial geniculate = hearing; lateral geniculate = vision.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) → (c) → (b) → (d) → (e) → (g) → (f)Puts superior olives before cochlear nuclei and cortex before the medial geniculate; auditory nerve fibres end in the cochlear nuclei first, and the thalamus relays to cortex.
    • (3) (b) → (a) → (d) → (c) → (g) → (e) → (f)Starts with the cochlear nuclei and puts cortex before the colliculi; the auditory nerve comes first, and the cortex is the final stop.
    • (4) (f) → (g) → (e) → (d) → (c) → (b) → (a)Lists the pathway backwards, from thalamus to nerve; hearing signals ascend from the auditory nerve to the cortex.

  50. Escalation of commitment involves the following sequence of steps.

    • (a) Occurrence of loss
    • (b) Self-Justification
    • (c) Strengthening by external factor
    • (d) Rational expectation of positive outcome

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(d) → (a) → (c) → (b)
    • 2(d) → (a) → (b) → (c)
    • 3(a) → (b) → (c) → (d)
    • 4(a) → (b) → (d) → (c)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (d) → (a) → (b) → (c)
    Barry Staw, escalation of commitment (Greenberg and Baron)
    The concept

    Correct order(d) A project starts with a rational expectation of a positive outcome → (a) losses occur → (b) the decision maker carries on out of self-justification, not wanting to admit an error → (c) external factors (political pressure, public commitment) strengthen commitment further.

    What it isEscalation of commitment is the tendency to keep investing in a failing course of action, 'throwing good money after bad' (Barry Staw).

    Exam tipRemedies: set limits in advance, let different people make the first and later decisions, and review progress regularly.

    Old keyThis page used to mark this question as withdrawn. That key does not line up with this paper, so it is answered here like the rest.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (d) → (a) → (c) → (b)Puts external pressure before self-justification; the first reaction to early losses is the personal need to justify the decision, with outside forces added later.
    • (3) (a) → (b) → (c) → (d)Starts with the loss; escalation begins with a sound decision based on a rational expectation of success, before any loss occurs.
    • (4) (a) → (b) → (d) → (c)Starts with the loss and puts the rational expectation in the middle; the positive expectation comes first and leads to the initial decision.

  51. Client: "I really don't have much to say (tapping foot)." Counsellor: "Do you know you are tapping your foot?" Client: "No. I guess I am feeling some impatience (pause). Even more, some confused." Here the counselor is:

    • 1Trying to create awareness in the client
    • 2Being a little too harsh
    • 3Making an effort to understand the client's behaviour at a deeper level
    • 4Trying to create both awareness and deeper understanding
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Trying to create awareness in the client
    Fritz Perls, Gestalt awareness technique
    The concept

    Why this oneThe counsellor simply points to a nonverbal act the client has not noticed (foot tapping). Bringing an unnoticed behaviour into focus is awareness work.

    How it worksIn Gestalt therapy (Fritz Perls), awareness of what one is doing right now is itself healing. The counsellor reflects the body cue and the client discovers the feeling behind it (impatience, confusion).

    Exam tipGestalt asks 'what' and 'how' and stays in the here and now; it does not interpret causes or ask 'why'.

    Not certainThe client's reply does reach a deeper grasp of his feelings, so (4) is defensible, but the counsellor's own move only points out the behaviour, which is awareness work.

    Old keyThis page used to show (4) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Being a little too harshThe question is gentle and non-judgemental; it neither blames nor attacks the client, so it is not harsh.
    • (3) Making an effort to understand the client's behaviour at a deeper levelProbing the deeper meaning or causes of behaviour is interpretation, as in psychoanalysis; this counsellor does not interpret, the client finds the feeling himself.
    • (4) Trying to create both awareness and deeper understandingThe deeper understanding comes from the client, not from any interpretation by the counsellor; the counsellor's own move is only to raise awareness.

  52. The Phi-Phenomenon is best seen between which of the following time intervals?

    • 150 to 350 milliseconds
    • 2110 to 320 milliseconds
    • 330 to 200 milliseconds
    • 4215 to 375 milliseconds
    ✓ Answer: (3) 30 to 200 milliseconds
    Max Wertheimer, apparent motion (1912)
    The concept

    Why this oneWhen two lights flash one after the other, movement is seen between them when the gap is about 30 to 200 ms, best at about 60 ms.

    How it worksGap under about 30 ms: the lights look simultaneous. Gap over about 200 ms: they look successive, two separate flashes. In between, one light seems to move: the phi phenomenon.

    Exam tipPhi is apparent movement, the basis of films and neon signs. Max Wertheimer's study of it, with Wolfgang Köhler and Kurt Koffka, started Gestalt psychology.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 50 to 350 millisecondsStarts above the 30 ms threshold and runs well past 200 ms, where the two flashes are seen as separate (succession), not as movement.
    • (2) 110 to 320 millisecondsLeaves out the optimal gap of about 60 ms and runs past 200 ms, where the flashes look successive.
    • (4) 215 to 375 millisecondsLies almost wholly above 200 ms, where the two lights are seen one after the other with no movement.

  53. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Assertion (A): Philosophically the word pragmatism emphasizes result rather than methods. Reason (R): A pragmatic view of science accepts a methodological approach to knowledge. In the light of the above two statements, choose the correct option:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true, but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false, and (R) is true
    ✓ Best answer: (3) (A) is true, but (R) is false
    Charles Peirce, William James, John Dewey: pragmatism
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. Pragmatism (William James, John Dewey) judges an idea by its practical consequences: what works is true. Results matter more than the route taken.

    ReasonFalse as stated. A pragmatic view of science does not commit to one fixed method; it is method-pluralist and uses whatever approach answers the question.

    Exam tipPragmatism is the usual philosophy behind mixed-methods research: the research question, not the method, comes first.

    Not certainIf R is read as pragmatists such as Charles Peirce valuing the method of science, R is true but does not explain A, giving (2).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)R is not true as stated, and a stress on a fixed method could not explain a philosophy that puts results ahead of methods.
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)Needs R to be true; pragmatism does not tie knowledge to one methodological approach, so R fails.
    • (4) (A) is false, and (R) is trueA is the standard definition of pragmatism (truth judged by practical results), so A cannot be false.

  54. Double standards of mental health with regard to adult females:

    • (a) depicted adult female behaviour as less socially desirable and healthy
    • (b) had set up barriers against the advancement of women in non-traditional roles
    • (c) pertain to the changing dual personality of women in the current scenario
    • (d) pertain to the identification of the general standards of healthy behaviour with men only

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1Only (d) is correct
    • 2Only (c) is correct
    • 3Only (a) and (b) are correct
    • 4(a), (b) and (c) are correct
    ✓ Answer: (3) Only (a) and (b) are correct
    Inge Broverman and colleagues (1970)
    The concept

    Why this oneIn the study by Inge Broverman and colleagues, clinicians rated a healthy woman as less healthy and less socially desirable than a healthy adult, so (a) is true. Such a double standard also discourages women from non-traditional roles, so (b) is true.

    What they foundClinicians' picture of a healthy adult (sex unspecified) matched their picture of a healthy man. A healthy woman was seen as more submissive, less independent and more emotional.

    Why not (c), (d)(c) 'dual personality' is not part of the concept. The adult standard did match the male one, but (d) alone leaves out the core finding (a), so the best set is (a) and (b).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Only (d) is correctThe adult standard did align with the male one, but (d) alone misses the core finding that female behaviour was rated less healthy and desirable.
    • (2) Only (c) is correct(c) is not part of the concept; the double standard is about clinicians' stereotyped judgements, not a changing dual personality of women.
    • (4) (a), (b) and (c) are correctIncludes (c), which is false; the double standard concerns stereotyped clinical judgements, not a dual personality of women.

  55. Consider the three types of investigation in psychological research written in alphabetical order:

    • (a) Controlled laboratory experiments;
    • (b) Ex post facto studies; and
    • (c) Field experiment

    Arrange them in descending order in terms of their ability to control secondary variance usually.

    • 1(a), (c), (b)
    • 2(a), (b), (c)
    • 3(c), (a), (b)
    • 4(b), (c), (a)
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a), (c), (b)
    Fred Kerlinger, control of extraneous variance
    The concept

    Why this oneThe laboratory experiment controls extraneous (secondary) variance best, the field experiment next, and the ex post facto study least.

    Correct order(a) laboratory experiment: variables manipulated, setting controlled, random assignment → (c) field experiment: manipulation and randomisation in a real setting with less control → (b) ex post facto: no manipulation, no random assignment.

    Exam tipFred Kerlinger's MAXMINCON: maximise systematic variance, minimise error variance, control extraneous variance. Control falls as realism rises.

    Old keyThis page used to show (4) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (a), (b), (c)Puts ex post facto second, but it has no manipulation or random assignment, so it controls extraneous variance least, below the field experiment.
    • (3) (c), (a), (b)A field experiment runs in a natural setting with many uncontrolled influences, so it cannot beat the laboratory experiment.
    • (4) (b), (c), (a)This is the ascending order (least to most control), the reverse of what is asked.

  56. There are many mnemonic devices to help a person memorize lists of words and vocabulary items. Identify the correct device series.

    • (a) Categorical Clustering
    • (b) Interactive Images
    • (c) Recognition
    • (d) Pegword System
    • (e) Method of Loci

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a), (b) and (c)
    • 2(b), (c) and (d)
    • 3(c), (d) and (e)
    • 4(a), (b), (d) and (e)
    ✓ Answer: (4) (a), (b), (d) and (e)
    Robert Sternberg, mnemonic devices in cognitive psychology
    The concept

    Why this oneCategorical clustering, interactive images, the pegword system and the method of loci are all mnemonic devices; recognition is a way of retrieving, not a device.

    What each doesClustering groups items by category; interactive images link items in one vivid scene; pegwords hook items onto rhymes (one is a bun); loci place items along a familiar route.

    Exam tipOther devices: acronyms, acrostics and the keyword system (for foreign vocabulary). Recognition and recall are memory measures.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (b) and (c)Includes recognition, which is a retrieval task (picking the right item from choices), not a technique for encoding lists.
    • (2) (b), (c) and (d)Includes recognition, a memory test rather than a mnemonic, and leaves out clustering and the method of loci.
    • (3) (c), (d) and (e)Includes recognition, which is a way of testing memory, and leaves out clustering and interactive images.

  57. If you move a book from your desk to a shelf, you know the form of the book is not changing. It may be because of which of the following?

    • 1Linear perspective
    • 2Relative size
    • 3Shape constancy
    • 4Size constancy
    ✓ Best answer: (3) Shape constancy
    Perceptual constancy: shape constancy
    The concept

    Why this oneAs you carry the book its image on the retina changes from a rectangle to a trapezoid, yet you still see a rectangle. Keeping form stable is shape constancy.

    What it isPerceptual constancy lets us see objects as stable despite changing retinal images: shape, size, brightness and colour constancy.

    Exam tipShape: form stays the same (an opening door). Size: an object does not seem to shrink as it moves away (a friend walking off).

    Not certainMoving the book also changes its distance, so size constancy (4) can be argued, but the stem asks about its form, which is shape.

    Old keyThis page used to show (4) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are not the answer
    • (1) Linear perspectiveA monocular depth cue: parallel lines seem to meet in the distance; it signals depth, not a stable form.
    • (2) Relative sizeA depth cue: the smaller of two similar objects looks farther away; it does not keep form constant.
    • (4) Size constancyKeeps an object's perceived size stable as distance changes; the stem asks about form, which is shape constancy.

  58. Arrange the following stages of moral development in sequence of their emergence as an individual grows up.

    • (a) Morality of interpersonal cooperation
    • (b) Universal ethical principle orientation
    • (c) Social order maintaining orientation
    • (d) Instrumental Purpose Orientation

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(d) → (a) → (b) → (c)
    • 2(d) → (a) → (c) → (b)
    • 3(a) → (b) → (d) → (c)
    • 4(b) → (c) → (d) → (a)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (d) → (a) → (c) → (b)
    Lawrence Kohlberg, stages of moral development
    The concept

    Why this oneLawrence Kohlberg's stages run from self-interest to approval to law and order to universal principles: (d) → (a) → (c) → (b).

    Correct orderStage 2 instrumental purpose (preconventional) → Stage 3 interpersonal cooperation, good boy/nice girl → Stage 4 social order maintaining, law and order (both conventional) → Stage 6 universal ethical principles (postconventional).

    Exam tipStage 1 is punishment and obedience; Stage 5 is social contract. Three levels, two stages each.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (d) → (a) → (b) → (c)Puts universal ethical principles (Stage 6) before social order maintaining (Stage 4), reversing the last two steps.
    • (3) (a) → (b) → (d) → (c)Starts with Stage 3 and puts Stage 6 second; instrumental purpose (Stage 2) must come first.
    • (4) (b) → (c) → (d) → (a)Almost reversed: universal ethical principles is the highest stage, reached last if at all.

  59. Beta endorphin is mobilized from the Pituitary Gland during stress in approximately the same quantities as:

    • 1Adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)
    • 2Corticotrophin releasing factor (CRF)
    • 3Epinephrine
    • 4Dopamine
    ✓ Answer: (1) Adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)
    Pro-opiomelanocortin (POMC) co-release
    The concept

    Why this oneBeta endorphin and ACTH are cut from the same precursor molecule, pro-opiomelanocortin (POMC), in the anterior pituitary, so stress releases them together in roughly equal amounts.

    How it worksStress → hypothalamus releases CRF → anterior pituitary splits POMC → ACTH (drives cortisol from the adrenal cortex) plus beta endorphin (pain relief).

    Exam tipThis is the basis of stress-induced analgesia: the body's own opioids blunt pain during emergencies.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Corticotrophin releasing factor (CRF)Made by the hypothalamus, not the pituitary; it triggers the release of ACTH and beta endorphin rather than being released with them.
    • (3) EpinephrineReleased by the adrenal medulla under sympathetic control, not split from POMC in the pituitary.
    • (4) DopamineA neurotransmitter of reward and movement pathways; it is not split from POMC alongside beta endorphin.

  60. Which of the following are the elements of the Language Acquisition Process?

    • (a) Phonemes
    • (b) Morphemes
    • (c) Syntax
    • (d) Pragmatics

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a), (c) and (d) only
    • 2(b), (c) and (d) only
    • 3(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 4(a), (b) and (d) only
    ✓ Best answer: (3) (a), (b) and (c) only
    Structure of language: phonemes, morphemes, syntax
    The concept

    Why this oneA child acquires language by mastering its structural units: phonemes (basic sounds), morphemes (smallest units of meaning) and syntax (rules for ordering words).

    How it buildsSounds (cooing, babbling) → words made of morphemes → word combinations governed by syntax (telegraphic speech) → full sentences.

    PragmaticsPragmatics is the social use of language (turn taking, politeness); it is about using language in context rather than one of its structural building blocks.

    Not certainAll four belong to language development; texts that count pragmatics as an element could support (1), (2) or (4), but the three structural units are the usual set.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (c) and (d) onlyDrops morphemes, the smallest units of meaning, and adds pragmatics, which is the social use of language rather than a structural unit.
    • (2) (b), (c) and (d) onlyDrops phonemes, the basic sound units that babbling and first words are built from, and adds pragmatics instead.
    • (4) (a), (b) and (d) onlyDrops syntax, the grammar rules for combining words, which is central to acquiring language, and adds pragmatics.

  61. The core emphasis of developmental psychology is represented by which of these sets of pairs?

    • 1Individual v/s shared and stability v/s change
    • 2Young v/s old and stability v/s change
    • 3Individual v/s shared and young v/s old
    • 4Cross-sectional v/s longitudinal and self-esteem v/s self-doubt
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Individual v/s shared and stability v/s change
    Lifespan developmental psychology, core issues
    The concept

    Why this oneDevelopmental psychology asks how people change and how they stay stable over the lifespan, and which patterns are shared by everyone versus individual to each person.

    How it worksShared (normative) pattern: most babies walk around one year. Individual differences: timing and style vary with genes and experience. Stability versus change: does a shy toddler stay shy?

    Exam tipOther core issues: nature versus nurture, continuity versus discontinuity (stages), critical versus sensitive periods.

    Not certainBoth (1) and (2) contain stability versus change; (2) works only if the age span is treated as a core issue, which standard texts do not do.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Young v/s old and stability v/s changeThe age range the field covers is not a theoretical issue; only stability versus change fits here.
    • (3) Individual v/s shared and young v/s oldYoung versus old describes age groups, not a core issue, and the pair leaves out stability versus change.
    • (4) Cross-sectional v/s longitudinal and self-esteem v/s self-doubtResearch designs (cross-sectional, longitudinal) plus a personality theme; neither pair is the field's core emphasis.

  62. Which of the following schools of Buddhism focuses on collective freedom from suffering and feeling the ways of enlightenment?

    • 1Nirvana
    • 2Theravada Buddhism
    • 3Mahayana Buddhism
    • 4Vajrayana Buddhism
    ✓ Answer: (3) Mahayana Buddhism
    Mahayana Buddhism, bodhisattva ideal
    The concept

    Why this oneMahayana ('great vehicle') stresses liberation for all beings. Its ideal is the bodhisattva, who delays final nirvana to help others reach enlightenment.

    ContrastTheravada stresses individual liberation by one's own effort; its ideal is the arhat. Vajrayana is the tantric school of Tibet.

    Exam tipCollective, compassion-based liberation → Mahayana; individual path → Theravada; ritual, mantra and tantra → Vajrayana.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) NirvanaThis is the goal itself, the end of craving and suffering, not a school of Buddhism.
    • (2) Theravada BuddhismThe older school of Sri Lanka and South-East Asia; it stresses individual liberation through one's own effort (the arhat ideal).
    • (4) Vajrayana BuddhismThe tantric school of Tibet using mantras, rituals and a guru; it grew out of Mahayana but is known for esoteric practice.

  63. The three psychologists who illustrated the diversity of approaches to functional psychology at Columbia were:

    • 1Harvey Carr, James McKeen Cattell and E.L. Thorndike
    • 2James Angell, Harvey Carr and James McKeen Cattell
    • 3E.L. Thorndike, R.S. Woodworth and James Angell
    • 4James McKeen Cattell, E.L. Thorndike and R.S. Woodworth
    ✓ Answer: (4) James McKeen Cattell, E.L. Thorndike and R.S. Woodworth
    James McKeen Cattell, E. L. Thorndike, Robert S. Woodworth
    The concept

    Why this oneAt Columbia University functionalism was loose and practical: James McKeen Cattell (mental tests), Edward Thorndike (law of effect) and Robert Woodworth (dynamic psychology).

    Chicago vs ColumbiaThe formal Chicago school of functionalism was led by John Dewey, James Angell and Harvey Carr.

    Exam tipRobert Woodworth added the organism to S-R, giving S-O-R, and stressed motivation in his dynamic psychology.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Harvey Carr, James McKeen Cattell and E.L. ThorndikeIncludes Harvey Carr, who led functionalism at Chicago after James Angell, not at Columbia.
    • (2) James Angell, Harvey Carr and James McKeen CattellPairs Cattell with two Chicago men: James Angell and Harvey Carr led the Chicago school of functionalism.
    • (3) E.L. Thorndike, R.S. Woodworth and James AngellIncludes James Angell, who made functionalism a formal school at the University of Chicago, not Columbia.

  64. Match the following types of memories with their features.

    • List I: (a) Non-declarative memory; (b) Echoic memory; (c) Semantic memory; (d) Episodic memory
    • List II: (i) Type of declarative memory containing general knowledge of language and information; (ii) Type of declarative memory containing personal information not readily available to others; (iii) Type of LTM for skills, procedures, habits etc.; (iv) Auditory sensation lasting only for 2 to 4 seconds

    Choose the correct option from those given below:

    • 1(a)-(i), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(iv)
    • 2(a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)
    • 3(a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(i), (d)-(ii)
    • 4(a)-(iv), (b)-(iii), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(i), (d)-(ii)
    Endel Tulving (episodic, semantic); Ulric Neisser (echoic)
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Non-declarative = (iii) LTM for skills, procedures, habits; (b) Echoic = (iv) auditory sensation lasting 2 to 4 seconds; (c) Semantic = (i) general knowledge of language and facts; (d) Episodic = (ii) personal events not readily available to others.

    How it worksDeclarative (explicit) memory splits into semantic (facts) and episodic (personal events), as Endel Tulving proposed. Non-declarative (implicit) memory covers skills, priming and conditioning.

    Exam tipThe iconic (visual) store lasts under a second; the echoic (auditory) store lasts a few seconds. Ulric Neisser named both.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(i), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(iv)Gives non-declarative memory the semantic definition and echoic memory the episodic one; non-declarative holds skills, echoic is brief auditory sensation.
    • (2) (a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)Pairs non-declarative memory with personal events and echoic with skills; non-declarative holds skills, and echoic is brief auditory sensation.
    • (4) (a)-(iv), (b)-(iii), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)Pairs non-declarative with auditory sensation and semantic with personal events; non-declarative holds skills, semantic holds general knowledge.

  65. Match the following elements of the Immune System with their respective functions.

    • List I: (a) Neutrophils; (b) Immunoglobulins; (c) NK (Natural Killer) cells; (d) CD4
    • List II: (i) Yield Marshalls; (ii) Cytotoxic action; (iii) Phagocytic action; (iv) Antibody action

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a)-(iv), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(i)
    • 2(a)-(iii), (b)-(i), (c)-(ii), (d)-(iv)
    • 3(a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    • 4(a)-(ii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(i), (d)-(iii)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    Psychoneuroimmunology: immune cell functions
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Neutrophils = (iii) phagocytic action; (b) Immunoglobulins = (iv) antibody action; (c) NK cells = (ii) cytotoxic action; (d) CD4 = (i) marshals that direct the response.

    How it worksNeutrophils engulf microbes; immunoglobulins are antibodies made by B cells; natural killer cells kill virus-infected and tumour cells; CD4 helper T cells coordinate (marshal) other immune cells.

    Exam tipHIV destroys CD4 cells, so the defence loses its coordinator. Stress also lowers NK cell activity.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(iv), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(i)Gives neutrophils antibody action and NK cells phagocytosis; antibodies are immunoglobulins and NK cells kill by cytotoxicity.
    • (2) (a)-(iii), (b)-(i), (c)-(ii), (d)-(iv)Calls immunoglobulins marshals and CD4 cells antibodies; it is the reverse: CD4 helper cells marshal, immunoglobulins are antibodies.
    • (4) (a)-(ii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(i), (d)-(iii)Gives neutrophils cytotoxicity and NK cells the marshal role; neutrophils are phagocytes and CD4 cells do the coordinating.

  66. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Assertion (A): Culture encompasses lifestyles, values, practices and goals which provide distinct identities to people and community. Reason (R): Culture always acts as a constraint in the growth of a community. In the light of the above two statements, choose the correct option:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true, but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false, but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (3) (A) is true, but (R) is false
    Cultural psychology: culture as a shared way of life
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. Culture is a shared design for living (values, beliefs, practices, goals) that gives a group its distinct identity.

    ReasonFalse. Culture can limit some choices, but it also enables growth by passing on knowledge, skills and support; 'always a constraint' is wrong.

    Exam tipAbsolute words such as always or never in a Reason often signal that it is false.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)R is false, since culture also enables growth, and a false Reason cannot explain the Assertion.
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)Needs R to be true; culture is not always a constraint, it also supports and enables a community's growth.
    • (4) (A) is false, but (R) is trueA is a standard definition of culture, so it is true; R is the false statement.

  67. Eleven rural, eleven semi-urban and eleven urban students were thoroughly interviewed and ranked (using a pooled sample of N = 33) for their communication skills. Which one of the following statistical techniques would be appropriate to explore the groupwise difference in communication?

    • 1Friedman's test
    • 2Kruskal-Wallis H test
    • 3Rank difference correlation
    • 4One-way ANOVA for independent samples
    ✓ Answer: (2) Kruskal-Wallis H test
    Kruskal-Wallis one-way analysis of variance by ranks
    The concept

    Why this oneThree independent groups (rural, semi-urban, urban) with ranked (ordinal) data call for the Kruskal-Wallis H test, the non-parametric version of one-way ANOVA.

    How it worksRank all 33 students together, sum the ranks in each group, then H = [12 / N(N + 1)] × Σ(R2/n) - 3(N + 1), tested against chi-square with k - 1 = 2 df.

    Exam tipTwo independent groups → Mann-Whitney U; related samples → Wilcoxon (two) or Friedman (three or more).

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Friedman's testFor three or more related (repeated or matched) samples; these are three separate groups of students.
    • (3) Rank difference correlationSpearman's rho measures the relationship between two sets of ranks, not differences between groups.
    • (4) One-way ANOVA for independent samplesNeeds interval scores with roughly normal distributions; ranked data call for its non-parametric version, Kruskal-Wallis.

  68. Which of the following norms suggest to people how they ought to behave?

    • 1Social responsibility norms
    • 2Reciprocity norms
    • 3Prescriptive norms
    • 4Proscriptive norms
    ✓ Answer: (3) Prescriptive norms
    Social norms: prescriptive versus proscriptive
    The concept

    Why this onePrescriptive norms tell people what they ought to do (help a friend, say thank you).

    ContrastProscriptive norms tell people what they ought not to do (do not steal). Together they make up a group's rules of conduct.

    Exam tipDescriptive norms describe what most people actually do; injunctive norms state what is approved or disapproved (Robert Cialdini).

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Social responsibility normsA specific helping norm: help those who depend on us; it is one example of a rule, not the general class of 'ought' norms.
    • (2) Reciprocity normsA specific norm (Alvin Gouldner) that we should help, and not harm, those who have helped us; it is one example, not the category.
    • (4) Proscriptive normsThese say what people should not do, forbidding behaviour rather than suggesting how one ought to behave.

  69. Which of the following are motivators for helping others?

    • (a) Attitudes
    • (b) Empathy
    • (c) Personal distress
    • (d) Norms and values

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a), (b) and (c)
    • 2(a), (b) and (d)
    • 3(a), (c) and (d)
    • 4(b), (c) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (4) (b), (c) and (d)
    C. Daniel Batson, empathy versus personal distress
    The concept

    Why this onePeople help out of empathy (concern for the victim), personal distress (to relieve their own upset) and norms and values (social responsibility, reciprocity, moral values). Attitudes are not a standard helping motive.

    How it worksC. Daniel Batson's empathy-altruism view: empathic concern gives an altruistic motive. Personal distress gives an egoistic motive: help to reduce one's own arousal, as in Jane Piliavin's arousal cost-reward model.

    Exam tipWhen escape is easy, distressed people often leave instead of helping; empathic people help anyway.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (b) and (c)Includes attitudes and drops norms and values, which are classic motives for helping (social responsibility, reciprocity).
    • (2) (a), (b) and (d)Includes attitudes and leaves out personal distress, which Batson showed is a real (egoistic) motive for helping.
    • (3) (a), (c) and (d)Includes attitudes and leaves out empathy, the central altruistic motive for helping.

  70. Suler's (2004) concept of the "Online Disinhibition Effect" is characterised by:

    • (a) Anonymity
    • (b) Accessibility
    • (c) Hierarchies negation
    • (d) Easy escape from consequences

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a) and (b) only
    • 2(a), (b) and (c)
    • 3(a), (c) and (d)
    • 4(a), (b) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a), (c) and (d)
    John Suler, online disinhibition effect (2004)
    The concept

    Why this oneAccessibility is not one of John Suler's factors. Anonymity, the levelling of status and authority (hierarchies negated) and the easy escape from consequences are, so (a), (c) and (d).

    Suler's six factorsDissociative anonymity, invisibility, asynchronicity (post and leave, an 'emotional hit and run'), solipsistic introjection, dissociative imagination, and minimisation of status and authority.

    Exam tipDisinhibition can be benign (sharing feelings, kindness) or toxic (rude language, threats, flaming).

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) and (b) onlyIncludes accessibility, which is not one of Suler's factors, and leaves out the levelling of status and the escape from consequences.
    • (2) (a), (b) and (c)Includes accessibility, which is not a Suler factor, and leaves out the easy escape from consequences that anonymity and asynchronicity allow.
    • (4) (a), (b) and (d)Includes accessibility, which is not a Suler factor, and leaves out the minimisation of status and authority.

  71. A teacher had marks for 50 students in his class. He computed their mean and standard deviation. Considering that the evaluation was too strict, he gave five grace marks to each student. This would:

    • (a) Increase mean by five
    • (b) Alter standard deviation in an unpredicted way
    • (c) Change the rank order of the students
    • (d) Change the skewness of the distribution

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a) only
    • 2(a) and (b) only
    • 3(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 4(a), (c) and (d) only
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a) only
    Effect of adding a constant to scores
    The concept

    Why this oneAdding the same 5 marks to every score shifts the whole distribution up by 5. The mean rises by 5; nothing else changes.

    How it worksSD uses deviations from the mean: (X + 5) - (M + 5) = X - M, so the SD is unchanged. Everyone moves up together, so rank order and skewness (shape) stay the same.

    Exam tipAdd or subtract a constant: mean changes, SD does not. Multiply by a constant: both mean and SD are multiplied by it.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (a) and (b) onlySD does not change at all, let alone unpredictably, because every deviation from the mean stays the same.
    • (3) (a), (b) and (c) onlySD and rank order are both unchanged; adding the same amount to everyone keeps the gaps and the order.
    • (4) (a), (c) and (d) onlyRank order and skewness stay the same; the shape of the distribution only slides along the scale.

  72. In quota sampling, the probability of each element of the population being included in the sample:

    • 1is equal to the sampling fraction
    • 2can be specified in advance
    • 3cannot be specified in advance
    • 4is 1/k where K is the number of strata
    ✓ Answer: (3) cannot be specified in advance
    Non-probability sampling: quota sampling
    The concept

    Why this oneQuota sampling is non-probability sampling: interviewers fill set quotas (for example 50 men, 50 women) with whoever they choose, so no one can state each element's chance of selection.

    How it differsStratified random sampling also uses subgroups, but picks randomly within each, so probabilities are known. Quota sampling drops that random step.

    Exam tipKnown selection probability: simple random, stratified, cluster, systematic. Unknown: quota, purposive, accidental, snowball.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) is equal to the sampling fractionAn equal chance equal to the sampling fraction (n/N) holds for simple random sampling, not quota sampling.
    • (2) can be specified in advanceProbabilities can be stated in advance only in probability sampling; quota sampling lets the interviewer choose who is included.
    • (4) is 1/k where K is the number of strataA fixed 1/k chance fits systematic sampling, where k is the sampling interval; quota sampling has no known selection probability.

  73. "Confrontation" in Gestalt therapies involves:

    • 1What and why questions
    • 2What and how questions
    • 3How and why questions
    • 4What, how and why questions
    ✓ Answer: (2) What and how questions
    Fritz Perls, Gestalt therapy
    The concept

    Why this oneGestalt confrontation challenges clients with what and how questions: 'What are you doing now? How are you avoiding?' These keep the client in present awareness.

    Why not 'why''Why' questions invite intellectual explanations, excuses and talk about the past, which Fritz Perls saw as a way of avoiding experience.

    Exam tipGestalt = here and now, awareness, unfinished business, empty chair; confrontation is done in a caring, inviting way.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) What and why questionsGestalt therapists avoid 'why' questions because they lead to intellectualising and explaining away feelings.
    • (3) How and why questionsIncludes 'why', which Gestalt avoids because it pulls the client into rationalising rather than present experience.
    • (4) What, how and why questionsAdds 'why', the one question type Gestalt confrontation deliberately avoids.

  74. In terms of psychoanalytic therapy, the means of keeping the unconscious conflict intact, thereby impeding any attempts to probe into the real sources of personality problems, is known as:

    • 1Repression
    • 2Suppression
    • 3Denial
    • 4Resistance
    ✓ Answer: (4) Resistance
    Sigmund Freud, resistance in psychoanalytic therapy
    The concept

    Why this oneIn psychoanalytic therapy, resistance is anything that works against therapy and keeps unconscious material out of awareness. It keeps the unconscious conflict intact and blocks probing into its real sources.

    How it worksResistance shows as missed sessions, lateness, silence, forgetting or changing the topic. The analyst interprets it, because it points to what the client most fears.

    Exam tipSigmund Freud treated resistance and transference as the main material of analysis; working through them is the core of the therapy.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) RepressionThe basic defence that pushes threatening material into the unconscious; resistance is how that material is guarded during therapy.
    • (2) SuppressionThe conscious, deliberate pushing aside of a thought, not an unconscious block that operates in therapy.
    • (3) DenialA general defence that refuses to accept a painful reality, not the therapy-specific term for blocking the probe.

  75. Jensen's level I and level II abilities can be best understood in terms of which of the following?

    • (a) Difficulty and complexity of tasks
    • (b) Socioeconomic differences
    • (c) Racial differences
    • (d) Geographical differences

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a), (c) and (d) only
    • 2(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 3(b), (c) and (d) only
    • 4(a), (b), (c) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a), (b) and (c) only
    Arthur Jensen, Level I and Level II abilities
    The concept

    Why this oneArthur Jensen split abilities by task complexity and linked them to socioeconomic and racial differences, so (a), (b) and (c). Geography played no part.

    What they areLevel I: simple associative learning and rote memory (digit span). Level II: abstract reasoning and problem solving, close to Charles Spearman's g.

    His claimJensen reported Level I scores to be fairly similar across social classes and racial groups, but Level II scores to differ. These claims remain heavily disputed.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (c) and (d) onlyIncludes geographical differences, which Jensen did not use, and leaves out the socioeconomic differences central to his Level I and II work.
    • (3) (b), (c) and (d) onlyLeaves out task complexity, the very basis of the Level I (rote) versus Level II (conceptual) split, and adds geography.
    • (4) (a), (b), (c) and (d)Adds geographical differences; Jensen's theory dealt with task complexity, social class and race, not region.

  76. Which part of the neuron is covered by the Myelin sheath?

    • 1Whole of Axon
    • 2Parts of Axon
    • 3Whole of Dendrite
    • 4Parts of Dendrites
    ✓ Answer: (2) Parts of Axon
    Myelination by Schwann cells and oligodendrocytes
    The concept

    Why this oneMyelin wraps the axon in segments, not end to end. Between the segments are bare gaps called nodes of Ranvier, and the axon hillock and terminal buttons are also uncovered.

    How it worksMyelin is a fatty insulating layer made by Schwann cells in the peripheral nervous system and oligodendrocytes in the central nervous system. The impulse jumps from node to node (saltatory conduction), which makes it much faster.

    Exam tipMyelin covers the axon only, and only in parts. Dendrites receive input and are not myelinated. Loss of myelin in the CNS causes multiple sclerosis.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Whole of AxonMyelin is interrupted at the nodes of Ranvier and absent at the axon hillock and terminals, so the axon is never covered completely.
    • (3) Whole of DendriteDendrites are the receiving branches of the neuron; they carry graded potentials and are not myelinated.
    • (4) Parts of DendritesNo part of a dendrite carries myelin; myelin insulates axons only, to speed up the action potential.

  77. Which of the following are the main biases in the attribution process?

    • (a) Fundamental attribution error
    • (b) Actor-observer effect
    • (c) Situational influence
    • (d) Self-serving bias

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a), (b) and (c)
    • 2(a), (b) and (d)
    • 3(b), (c) and (d)
    • 4(a), (c) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a), (b) and (d)
    Lee Ross; Edward Jones and Richard Nisbett
    The concept

    Why this oneThe three classic attribution biases are the fundamental attribution error, the actor-observer effect and the self-serving bias, so (a), (b) and (d) are right.

    FAE and actor-observerFundamental attribution error (Lee Ross, 1977): over-weighting personality and under-weighting the situation when explaining others' behaviour. Actor-observer effect (Edward Jones and Richard Nisbett): we blame the situation for our own acts but dispositions for others' acts.

    Self-serving biasSelf-serving bias: we credit our successes to internal causes (ability, effort) and blame our failures on external ones (luck, a hard test).

    Why not (c)Situational influence is a type of cause (an external attribution), not a bias. The biases are errors in how we weigh such causes.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (b) and (c)Includes situational influence, which is a kind of external cause people attribute behaviour to, not a bias, and drops the self-serving bias.
    • (3) (b), (c) and (d)Leaves out the fundamental attribution error, the best known attribution bias, and adds situational influence, which is a cause, not a bias.
    • (4) (a), (c) and (d)Leaves out the actor-observer effect and counts situational influence, an external cause rather than an error, as a bias.

  78. Which of the following are true as per Brentano's concept of Phenomenology?

    • (a) Outer objects can be perceived only
    • (b) Mental experiences can be perceived only
    • (c) Feelings and willing be combined in one category
    • (d) Outer objects can be both perceived and experienced

    Choose the best option:

    • 1(c) and (d) are correct
    • 2(a) and (c) are correct
    • 3(b), (c) and (d) are correct
    • 4(a) and (b) are correct
    ✓ Best answer: (3) (b), (c) and (d) are correct
    Franz Brentano, act psychology (1874)
    The concept

    Why this oneFranz Brentano held that mental acts can only be perceived inwardly, never observed as objects, while outer (physical) objects can be both perceived and observed. He also merged feeling and willing into one class. So (b), (c) and (d).

    Act psychologyIn Psychology from an Empirical Standpoint (1874), Brentano said psychology studies mental acts, not contents. Every act is intentional: it is directed at an object (I see something, I judge something).

    Three classesHe sorted acts into ideating (presenting), judging, and loving and hating; the last class joins feeling and willing, which is why (c) is true.

    Exam tipBrentano = act psychology, intentionality, inner perception; his student Edmund Husserl founded phenomenology. Wilhelm Wundt = content psychology.

    Not certainThe statements loosely paraphrase Franz Brentano's inner versus outer perception, with 'experienced' read as 'observed'; if 'outer objects can be perceived only' is taken as true, (2) is the runner-up.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (c) and (d) are correctGets (c) and (d) but omits (b): for Brentano, mental experiences are known only through inner perception and cannot be observed like outer objects.
    • (2) (a) and (c) are correctPairs (c) with (a), but Brentano did not limit outer objects to mere perception; unlike mental acts, they can also be observed.
    • (4) (a) and (b) are correctLeaves out (c), the surest Brentano claim: he merged feeling and willing into a single class of loving and hating.

  79. In the context of marginalization, it is suggested to adopt the 'Social Empowerment Model' while dealing with LGBTs because:

    • (a) it increases this group's collective and personal self-advocacy
    • (b) they learn that they can lead "fulfilling and satisfying" lives
    • (c) concerns about LGBT are social concerns and not the concern for the individual
    • (d) they realise that being gay or lesbian is not a pathological condition

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a), (b) and (c) are correct
    • 2(a) and (d) are correct
    • 3(b) and (c) are correct
    • 4(a), (b) and (d) are correct
    ✓ Best answer: (4) (a), (b) and (d) are correct
    Social empowerment model, LGBT-affirmative counselling
    The concept

    Why this oneThe social empowerment model builds personal and collective self-advocacy (a), helps clients see that they can lead fulfilling lives (b), and rejects the idea that being gay or lesbian is a pathology (d).

    Why not (c)Statement (c) is too absolute. The model links individual distress to social oppression, but it still works with the person; it does not say the individual's concerns do not matter.

    BackgroundMarginalised groups face stigma and discrimination. Empowerment approaches move the focus from 'fixing' the client to building power, skills and community support, in line with the 1973 removal of homosexuality from the DSM.

    Not certainThis comes from a niche counselling source on the social empowerment model; (2) is the runner-up if (b) is treated as a by-product rather than a reason.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (b) and (c) are correctIncludes (c), which wrongly says LGBT concerns are only social and not the individual's; the model works at both levels. It also drops (d).
    • (2) (a) and (d) are correct(a) and (d) are right but incomplete: the model also aims to show clients they can lead fulfilling and satisfying lives.
    • (3) (b) and (c) are correctIncludes the false statement (c) and misses (a), self-advocacy, which is the core aim of any empowerment model.

  80. The phenomenon where bystanders assume that nothing is wrong in an emergency because no one looks concerned is called:

    • 1Diffusion of responsibility
    • 2Pluralistic ignorance
    • 3Prosocial behaviour
    • 4Bystander effect
    ✓ Answer: (2) Pluralistic ignorance
    Bibb Latané and John Darley, bystander intervention
    The concept

    Why this onePluralistic ignorance is when each bystander looks at the others, sees calm faces, and wrongly concludes there is no emergency. Everyone hides their own worry, so the whole group misreads the situation.

    Where it fitsIn Bibb Latané and John Darley's model, a bystander must notice the event, interpret it as an emergency, take responsibility, decide how to help, and act. Pluralistic ignorance blocks the interpretation step.

    Classic studyIn their smoke-filled room study, students alone reported the smoke far more often than students sitting with passive confederates who ignored it.

    Exam tipPluralistic ignorance = 'Is it an emergency?' Diffusion of responsibility = 'Is it my job to help?'

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Diffusion of responsibilityThe feeling that someone else will help because many are present; it blocks taking responsibility, not interpreting the event as an emergency.
    • (3) Prosocial behaviourAny voluntary act that benefits others, such as helping; it is the outcome bystander research studies, not this error of interpretation.
    • (4) Bystander effectThe overall finding that people are less likely to help when others are present; pluralistic ignorance is one of its causes.

  81. Match the following types of aggression with their description.

    • List I: (a) Hostile Aggression; (b) Emotional Aggression; (c) Instrumental Aggression; (d) Thanatos
    • List II: (i) An instinctual drive involving aggressive action towards death; (ii) Injury in which the goal is to obtain something of value; (iii) An act stemming from feelings of anger and aimed at inflicting pain; (iv) Injury or harm that is carried out for the explicit goal of hurting someone

    Choose the correct option from those given below:

    • 1(a)-(i), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(iv)
    • 2(a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)
    • 3(a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(i), (d)-(ii)
    • 4(a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    ✓ Answer: (4) (a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(ii), (d)-(i)
    Types of aggression; Sigmund Freud's Thanatos
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Hostile aggression = (iii) act stemming from anger and aimed at inflicting pain; (b) Emotional aggression = (iv) harm carried out with the explicit goal of hurting someone; (c) Instrumental aggression = (ii) injury used to obtain something of value; (d) Thanatos = (i) instinctual drive towards death.

    How to solve(c)-(ii) and (d)-(i) are unmistakable, and only option 4 has both. Hostile and emotional aggression are near-synonyms: in both, hurting the victim is the goal itself.

    Key contrastInstrumental aggression uses harm as a means to another goal, such as money or status. Sigmund Freud proposed Thanatos, the death instinct, whose energy turned outward becomes aggression.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(i), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(iv)Pairs Thanatos with deliberate hurting and instrumental aggression with anger; instrumental aggression aims at a reward, and Thanatos is Freud's death drive.
    • (2) (a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)Gives hostile aggression the 'obtain something of value' description, which defines instrumental aggression, and gives instrumental aggression the goal of hurting.
    • (3) (a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(i), (d)-(ii)Gets (a) and (b) right but swaps the last two: Thanatos is the death drive (i), and instrumental aggression seeks something of value (ii).

  82. In a newly developed adjustment inventory using a 'Yes/No' format, there were some positively worded items ('Yes' response indicating adjustment) and some negatively worded items ('No' response indicating adjustment). Test developers follow this practice to offset the effects of:

    • 1Acquiescence
    • 2Faking
    • 3Random responding
    • 4Socially desirable responding
    ✓ Answer: (1) Acquiescence
    Acquiescence response set; balanced keying
    The concept

    Why this oneAcquiescence is the tendency to say 'Yes' or agree regardless of content. Mixing positively and negatively keyed items (balanced keying) means a yes-sayer gains on some items and loses on others, so the bias cancels out.

    What it isAcquiescence is a response set: a style of answering that distorts scores whatever the item content. It is common in Yes/No and agree/disagree formats.

    Exam tipAcquiescence → balanced keying. Social desirability → forced-choice items matched on desirability, or a lie scale. Faking → validity scales (e.g. MMPI L, F, K).

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) FakingDeliberately distorting answers to look good or bad; it is detected with validity scales or reduced with forced-choice formats, not balanced keying.
    • (3) Random respondingAnswering without reading the items; it is detected by infrequency or consistency scales, not offset by reversing item wording.
    • (4) Socially desirable respondingAnswering to appear favourable; reversed items do not stop it because the desirable answer stays obvious. Forced-choice formats and lie scales are used instead.

  83. Which of the following statements are correct?

    • (a) Dilthey opposed reductionism
    • (b) Dilthey opposed psychophysical parallelism
    • (c) Dilthey rejected the positivistic approaches to experimental psychology
    • (d) Dilthey rejected the idea of description

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a), (b) and (d) are correct
    • 2(a), (c) and (d) are correct
    • 3(b) and (c) are correct
    • 4(a), (b) and (c) are correct
    ✓ Answer: (4) (a), (b) and (c) are correct
    Wilhelm Dilthey, descriptive psychology (1894)
    The concept

    Why this oneWilhelm Dilthey opposed reducing mental life to elements or physiology (a), rejected the hypotheses of explanatory psychology such as psychophysical parallelism (b), and rejected positivist experimental psychology as the model for the human sciences (c).

    Why (d) is falseDilthey did not reject description; he called for a descriptive and analytic psychology that describes lived experience as a connected whole (1894).

    Key ideaHe split the Naturwissenschaften (natural sciences, which explain) from the Geisteswissenschaften (human sciences, which understand, Verstehen): 'Nature we explain; mental life we understand.'

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (b) and (d) are correctIncludes (d), but Dilthey championed description: his alternative to experimental psychology was a descriptive and analytic psychology of lived experience.
    • (2) (a), (c) and (d) are correctIncludes (d), which is false because description was Dilthey's favoured method, and drops (b), his rejection of psychophysical parallelism.
    • (3) (b) and (c) are correctBoth statements are true, but the set is incomplete: opposition to reductionism (a) is central to Dilthey's case for the human sciences.

  84. Creativity has been conceptualised in terms of which of the following?

    • (a) As person
    • (b) As potential
    • (c) As process
    • (d) As perseverance

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 2(b), (c) and (d) only
    • 3(a), (c) and (d) only
    • 4(a), (b), (c) and (d)
    ✓ Best answer: (1) (a), (b) and (c) only
    Mel Rhodes' four Ps; creativity as potential
    The concept

    Why this oneCreativity is studied as a person (traits of creative people), as a process (stages of creative thinking) and as a potential (a capacity everyone has to some degree). Perseverance helps creative work but is not a way of defining creativity.

    Four PsMel Rhodes grouped definitions of creativity into four Ps: person, process, product and press (the environment).

    ProcessGraham Wallas described the creative process in four stages: preparation, incubation, illumination and verification.

    PotentialCreativity as potential links to J. P. Guilford's divergent thinking: tests of fluency, flexibility and originality measure creative capacity, not achievement.

    Not certainLists differ by source; if perseverance is counted as a way of conceptualising creativity (as a persistent trait), (4) is the runner-up.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (b), (c) and (d) onlyDrops person, one of the most standard views of creativity, and adds perseverance, a helpful trait of creative people rather than a conceptualisation.
    • (3) (a), (c) and (d) onlyDrops potential, a standard view (everyone has some creative capacity), and adds perseverance, which supports creative work but does not define creativity.
    • (4) (a), (b), (c) and (d)Includes perseverance; persistence is one trait of creative people, but standard frameworks define creativity as person, process, product, press or potential.

  85. According to social impact theory, the effect of majority on minority rests on three of the following.

    • (a) Majority's strength
    • (b) Minority's will
    • (c) Immediacy
    • (d) Number of persons

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a) (b) (c)
    • 2(a) (c) (d)
    • 3(b) (c) (d)
    • 4(b) (d) (a)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a) (c) (d)
    Bibb Latané, social impact theory (1981)
    The concept

    Why this oneIn Bibb Latané's social impact theory, influence depends on the strength (status, power) of the source, its immediacy (closeness in space and time) and the number of people. So (a), (c) and (d).

    How it worksImpact = f(S × I × N). Adding sources raises impact, but each extra person adds less than the one before (diminishing returns).

    Exam tipThe minority's will is not part of the formula. Minority influence on a majority is explained by Serge Moscovici's idea of consistency.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) (b) (c)Includes the minority's will, which is not a factor in social impact theory, and leaves out the number of people, a core factor.
    • (3) (b) (c) (d)Leaves out the majority's strength (status, power) and adds the minority's will, which the theory does not include.
    • (4) (b) (d) (a)Includes the minority's will and leaves out immediacy, the closeness of the source in space and time.

  86. Given below are two statements, Assertion (A) and Reason (R). Assertion (A): High sensation seekers are inclined to get involved in a variety of thrill-seeking activities. Reason (R): Engagement in thrill-seeking behaviour triggers the release of epinephrine. In the light of the above two statements, choose the correct option:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true, but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false, but (R) is true
    ✓ Best answer: (2) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    Marvin Zuckerman, sensation seeking
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. Marvin Zuckerman defined sensation seeking as the need for varied, novel and intense experiences; high scorers seek thrills such as skydiving and fast driving.
