    ReasonTrue. Thrilling, risky activity activates the sympathetic nervous system, and the adrenal medulla releases epinephrine (adrenaline).

    Why R failsEpinephrine is released in anyone doing something scary, high or low sensation seeker. Zuckerman linked the trait to an optimal level of arousal and brain chemistry such as low MAO and dopamine, so R does not explain A.

    Not certainIf the setter had the popular 'adrenaline rush' account in mind, (1) would be marked; Marvin Zuckerman's theory explains the trait through arousal needs, dopamine and MAO, not epinephrine release.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)Both are true, but adrenaline release happens in everyone during thrills; the trait itself is explained by arousal needs and dopamine and MAO differences.
    • (3) (A) is true, but (R) is falseR is true: thrilling or frightening activity triggers sympathetic arousal and epinephrine release from the adrenal medulla.
    • (4) (A) is false, but (R) is trueA is true: seeking varied, intense and risky experiences is exactly how Zuckerman defined high sensation seeking.

  87. Match the following leadership styles with their characteristics.

    • List 1: (a) Directive; (b) Supportive; (c) Participative; (d) Achievement oriented
    • List 2: (i) Leader consults subordinates thereby permitting them to participate in decision making; (ii) Leader sets challenging goals and seeks improvement in performance; (iii) Leader provides specific guidance by establishing work schedules and rules; (iv) Leader establishes good relations with subordinates and satisfies their needs

    Choose the correct option from those given below:

    • 1(a)-(i), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(iv)
    • 2(a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)
    • 3(a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(i), (d)-(ii)
    • 4(a)-(iv), (b)-(i), (c)-(ii), (d)-(iii)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a)-(iii), (b)-(iv), (c)-(i), (d)-(ii)
    Robert House, path-goal theory of leadership (1971)
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Directive = (iii) specific guidance, schedules and rules; (b) Supportive = (iv) good relations and attention to needs; (c) Participative = (i) consults subordinates in decisions; (d) Achievement-oriented = (ii) sets challenging goals and seeks improvement.

    What it isIn Robert House's path-goal theory, the leader clears the path to goals and raises followers' belief that effort leads to rewards. The best style depends on the followers and the task.

    Exam tipPath-goal theory draws on Victor Vroom's expectancy theory. Directive suits ambiguous tasks; supportive suits stressful or boring tasks.

    Old keyThis page used to show (4) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(i), (b)-(ii), (c)-(iii), (d)-(iv)Gives directive leadership consultation and participative leadership rule-setting; consulting subordinates is participative, and setting schedules and rules is directive.
    • (2) (a)-(ii), (b)-(iii), (c)-(iv), (d)-(i)Shifts the pairs: directive leaders give specific guidance, not challenging goals, and achievement-oriented leaders set challenging goals rather than consult.
    • (4) (a)-(iv), (b)-(i), (c)-(ii), (d)-(iii)Gives directive leadership the relationship role and participative leadership goal-setting; building good relations is supportive, and consulting is participative.

  88. Dr. X, as a therapist, felt a sense of complete understanding towards a father client whose children behaved exactly the way his own children used to behave. This is:

    • 1Empathy
    • 2Jumping to conclusion
    • 3Warmth and genuineness
    • 4Sympathy
    ✓ Best answer: (4) Sympathy
    Carl Rogers, empathy versus sympathy
    The concept

    Why this oneDr X's understanding comes from his own past, not from the client's frame of reference. Feeling with a client because 'I have been through the same' is sympathy, which shades into over-identification.

    EmpathyCarl Rogers defined empathy as sensing the client's private world 'as if' it were your own, without losing the 'as if' quality. An empathic therapist never assumes complete understanding.

    Why it mattersSympathy can lead the therapist to project his own experience onto the client and miss what is unique in this father's situation. This links to countertransference.

    Not certainMany would read 'complete understanding' as empathy (1); but understanding drawn from the therapist's own matching life, not the client's frame of reference, is sympathy or over-identification.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) EmpathyUnderstanding the client's world from the client's frame of reference while staying separate; claiming complete understanding through one's own experience goes beyond it.
    • (2) Jumping to conclusionA cognitive error of judging on too little evidence; the item describes a felt emotional closeness, not a hasty inference.
    • (3) Warmth and genuinenessWarmth (unconditional positive regard) and genuineness (congruence) are Rogers' other core conditions; they concern acceptance and honesty, not a feeling of shared experience.

  89. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Assertion (A): MRI provides images of brain activity rather than brain images. Reason (R): Results in MRI are obtained from the measurement of waves that hydrogen atoms emit when they are activated by radio-frequency waves in a magnetic field. In the light of the above two statements, choose the correct option:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true, but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false, but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (4) (A) is false, but (R) is true
    Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) versus fMRI
    The concept

    AssertionFalse. Standard MRI gives high-resolution pictures of brain structure. Images of brain activity come from fMRI (or PET).

    ReasonTrue. In MRI a strong magnetic field lines up hydrogen atoms; a radio-frequency pulse knocks them out of line, and the waves they give off as they realign are measured to build the image.

    Exam tipStructure: CT, MRI. Activity: PET, fMRI (the BOLD signal, based on oxygenated blood flow), EEG.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)A is false: plain MRI shows brain structure; it is functional MRI that tracks activity through changes in blood oxygen.
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)A is false, so this cannot be right: structural MRI images anatomy, not activity.
    • (3) (A) is true, but (R) is falseThe reverse is true: A is false (MRI shows structure) and R is true (hydrogen atoms emit the measured waves).

  90. Read the following paragraph and answer questions 91 to 95. The experimental psychologist wanted to explore whether noise adversely affects reaction time (RT). She also wanted to explore whether the stress level would moderate the effect of noise. She varied noise at three levels: low (30 decibels), moderate (60 decibels) and high (90 decibels). She varied stress at two levels: low and high. From the initial pool of college students, subjects were randomly assigned to different groups. However, a few subjects could not turn up to the laboratory due to vacation. In all, two hundred and ninety-seven subjects participated in the experiment. Each subject, in each group, was tested for simple RT, disjunctive RT, and choice RT. The theory and preliminary analyses indicated the three types of RT were moderately correlated. The covariance matrices, showing the covariances among the three types of RT, were found to be quite similar for all the groups. The multivariate analysis of variance (MANOVA) results were computed to explore the group differences in means. As the most common practice is, four test criteria (Pillai's criterion, Wilks' criterion, Hotelling's criterion, and Roy's criterion) were obtained. Among the different results obtained in this work, one interesting finding was that stress moderated the effect of noise level on reaction time.
    Q.91 Which of the following test criteria is more appropriate than the others?

    • 1Roy's criterion
    • 2Wilks' criterion
    • 3Hotelling's criterion
    • 4Pillai's criterion
    ✓ Best answer: (4) Pillai's criterion
    Pillai's trace, robust MANOVA criterion
    The concept

    Why this oneDropouts left 297 subjects in 6 cells, and 297 ÷ 6 = 49.5, so the cell sizes are unequal. Pillai's criterion (Pillai's trace) is the most robust of the four to unequal n and assumption violations, so it is the safest choice.

    The four criteriaAll four test whether group centroids differ on the combined DVs. With 1 df (stress, 2 levels) they give the same F; noise and the interaction have 2 df, so the four can differ.

    When to use whichWilks' lambda: the usual default. Roy's largest root: most powerful when groups differ on one dimension, least robust. Pillai's trace: advised by Barbara Tabachnick and Linda Fidell when cells are unequal, samples small or homogeneity doubtful.

    Not certainWilks' criterion (2) is the most widely reported default and is fine once covariance matrices are homogeneous; Pillai's is preferred here only because the dropouts make the cells unequal.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Roy's criterionRoy's largest root uses only the first discriminant dimension; powerful when groups differ on one dimension but the least robust, so a poor choice with unequal cells.
    • (2) Wilks' criterionWilks' lambda is the most commonly reported criterion, but it is less robust than Pillai's trace when cell sizes are unequal, as here after the dropouts.
    • (3) Hotelling's criterionHotelling's trace (Hotelling-Lawley) behaves much like Wilks' lambda and is less robust than Pillai's trace to unequal cell sizes.

  91. The design employed in the above study can best be labelled as:

    • 1Randomized six group design with a single factor
    • 2Balanced factorial design
    • 3Unbalanced factorial design
    • 4Unbalanced factorial design with a covariate
    ✓ Answer: (3) Unbalanced factorial design
    Factorial design with unequal cell sizes
    The concept

    Why this oneTwo IVs are crossed, noise (3 levels) × stress (2 levels), giving a 3 × 2 factorial design with 6 cells. With 297 subjects, 297 ÷ 6 = 49.5, so the cells cannot be equal: the design is unbalanced.

    Balanced vs unbalancedBalanced means every cell has the same n. Dropouts (here, students away on vacation) make cell sizes unequal, which complicates the sums of squares and calls for robust tests.

    Why no covariateA covariate is a variable measured before treatment and statistically controlled (ANCOVA). The three RT measures are dependent variables, not covariates.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Randomized six group design with a single factorThere are six groups, but they come from two crossed factors (noise and stress), so it is factorial; a single-factor design could not test the noise × stress interaction.
    • (2) Balanced factorial designBalanced needs equal n in every cell; 297 subjects cannot be split equally into 6 cells (49.5 each), and dropouts are reported.
    • (4) Unbalanced factorial design with a covariateNo covariate was measured or controlled; simple, disjunctive and choice RT are the dependent variables analysed by MANOVA.

  92. Which one of the following conclusions can be confidently drawn from the above study?

    • 1Main effect of noise is significant
    • 2Main effect of stress is significant
    • 3Noise × Stress interaction is significant
    • 4The main effects of noise, stress and interaction effect are significant
    ✓ Answer: (3) Noise × Stress interaction is significant
    Interaction (moderation) in a factorial design
    The concept

    Why this oneThe passage says stress moderated the effect of noise on RT. Moderation means the effect of one IV changes across levels of another, which is a significant noise × stress interaction.

    What it isA main effect is the overall effect of one factor averaged over the other; an interaction exists when the lines for one factor are not parallel across levels of the other.

    Exam tipA significant interaction says nothing about whether the main effects are significant. The passage reports only the interaction, so that is all we can conclude.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Main effect of noise is significantThe passage never reports the main effect of noise; with a significant interaction the main effect may or may not be significant, and would need qualifying.
    • (2) Main effect of stress is significantNo result for the main effect of stress is given; stress is said only to moderate the noise effect, which is an interaction.
    • (4) The main effects of noise, stress and interaction effect are significantOnly the interaction is reported; claiming all three effects are significant goes beyond the evidence in the passage.

  93. The above information suggests that:

    • 1the assumption of homogeneity of covariance matrices is not satisfied
    • 2the assumption of homogeneity of covariance matrices is satisfied
    • 3covariances among Dependent Variables (DV) are zero
    • 4the assumption of homogeneity of variances is not tenable
    ✓ Answer: (2) the assumption of homogeneity of covariance matrices is satisfied
    Homogeneity of covariance matrices (Box's M test)
    The concept

    Why this oneThe passage says the covariance matrices of the three RTs were quite similar for all the groups. That is exactly the MANOVA assumption of homogeneity of covariance matrices, so it is satisfied.

    What it isIt is the multivariate version of ANOVA's equal-variance assumption: each group should have the same variance on each DV and the same covariances between DVs. It is usually checked with Box's M test.

    Exam tipBox's M is very sensitive in large samples, so a strict level such as .001 is often used. If it is violated and cells are unequal, report Pillai's criterion.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) the assumption of homogeneity of covariance matrices is not satisfiedThe opposite: covariance matrices that are quite similar across groups mean the homogeneity assumption holds, not fails.
    • (3) covariances among Dependent Variables (DV) are zeroThe three RTs were moderately correlated, so their covariances are clearly not zero.
    • (4) the assumption of homogeneity of variances is not tenableSimilar covariance matrices also have similar variances on their diagonals, so equal variances are tenable too.

  94. Which one of the following reasons best justifies the application of MANOVA in the present work?

    • 1There are several groups and more than one Independent Variable (IV)
    • 2More than one Dependent Variable (DV) has been employed
    • 3More than one Dependent Variable with moderate intercorrelations has been employed
    • 4The moderating effect of stress on the effect of noise on RT can be demonstrated only through MANOVA
    ✓ Answer: (3) More than one Dependent Variable with moderate intercorrelations has been employed
    Rationale for MANOVA: moderately correlated DVs
    The concept

    Why this oneMANOVA is best justified when there are several DVs that are moderately correlated, as the three RTs are here. It tests group differences on their combined pattern and controls the Type I error of many separate ANOVAs.

    Why moderateIf the DVs are uncorrelated, separate ANOVAs do the job. If they are very highly correlated, they are redundant (multicollinearity) and MANOVA loses power.

    Exam tipNumber of IVs decides whether a design is factorial; number of DVs decides whether the analysis is multivariate. ANOVA: one DV. MANOVA: two or more DVs.

    Old keyThis page used to show (4) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) There are several groups and more than one Independent Variable (IV)Several groups and more than one IV justify a factorial design, which a factorial ANOVA handles with a single DV.
    • (2) More than one Dependent Variable (DV) has been employedMore than one DV is needed but not enough: uncorrelated DVs could be analysed by separate ANOVAs, so the moderate correlation is the real reason.
    • (4) The moderating effect of stress on the effect of noise on RT can be demonstrated only through MANOVAModeration is the interaction term and can be tested by a factorial ANOVA on each DV; MANOVA is not the only way.

  95. Read the following paragraph and answer questions 96 to 100. A leading business school in the city had a view that its students must attain total proficiency in English language usage. As an initial step, the school authorities decided to develop a brief language usage test. The project incharge wrote 30 items. Each item had five alternative answers, one of which was correct. He administered these 30 items to a sample of 250 students. For item analysis, item-remainder correlations were computed. The item analysis retained 25 items. The twenty-five item version was administered to a fresh sample of 300 students. Three reliability coefficients were computed: split-half reliability coefficient, Kuder-Richardson (K-R) reliability coefficient, and Cronbach alpha. The Cronbach alpha was found to be 0.7. The test-retest reliability was also computed with a time-interval of eight weeks in two administrations, using the same sample. The test-retest correlation was found to be 0.26, significant at the .001 level. The school authorities, pleased with the efforts of the project incharge, asked him to add similar items and develop a 75-item version.
    Q.96 The above information provides evidence for:

    • (a) Unsatisfactory test-retest reliability
    • (b) Satisfactory test-retest reliability
    • (c) Acceptable internal consistency reliability
    • (d) Unacceptable internal consistency reliability

    Choose the correct option:

    • 1(a) and (c)
    • 2(a) and (d)
    • 3(b) and (c)
    • 4(b) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a) and (c)
    Reliability benchmarks (Jum Nunnally's 0.70 rule)
    The concept

    Why this oneA test-retest r of 0.26 is far too low, so (a). Cronbach's α of 0.70 meets the usual minimum for a new test, so (c). Hence (a) and (c).

    WorkingShared variance = r² = 0.26² ≈ 0.07, so only about 7% of score variance is stable over eight weeks. Significance at .001 comes from the large N: t = 0.26 × √298 ÷ √(1 - 0.0676) ≈ 4.6, which only shows r is not zero.

    BenchmarksStatistical significance is not adequacy. A reliability of about 0.70 is acceptable for early research (Jum Nunnally), while good ability tests usually show test-retest coefficients of 0.80 or more.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (a) and (d)(a) is right, but α = 0.70 is at the accepted minimum for a new test, so internal consistency is acceptable, not unacceptable.
    • (3) (b) and (c)r = 0.26 explains only about 7% of variance; its significance reflects the large sample of 300, not satisfactory stability.
    • (4) (b) and (d)Both parts are reversed: test-retest r = 0.26 is unsatisfactory, and α = 0.70 is acceptable.

  96. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Assertion (A): The computation of K-R reliability is questionable in the above study. Reason (R): K-R reliability is suitable for tests with dichotomous items. In the light of the above two statements, choose the correct option:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true, but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false, but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (4) (A) is false, but (R) is true
    G. Frederic Kuder and Marion Richardson (1937), KR-20
    The concept

    AssertionFalse. Each item has five options but only one is correct, so it is scored right = 1, wrong = 0. Every item is therefore dichotomous, and K-R is exactly the right formula.

    ReasonTrue. KR-20 (and KR-21) were built for items scored 0/1: KR-20 = (k ÷ (k - 1)) × (1 - Σpq ÷ sX²), where pq is each item's variance.

    Exam tipThe number of answer options does not decide this; the scoring does. Rating-scale or partial-credit items need Cronbach's alpha instead.

    Old keyThis page used to show (2) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)A is false: five-option items scored right or wrong are dichotomous, so using K-R here is fully appropriate.
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)A is false: the number of answer choices does not matter; scoring each item as correct (1) or incorrect (0) makes it dichotomous.
    • (3) (A) is true, but (R) is falseThe reverse: R is true (K-R suits dichotomously scored items) and A is false.

  97. If the project incharge developed a 75-item version by adding similar items, what would be the expected Cronbach alpha?

    • 10.700
    • 20.824
    • 30.875
    • 40.931
    ✓ Answer: (3) 0.875
    Spearman-Brown prophecy formula
    The concept

    Why this oneGoing from 25 to 75 similar items lengthens the test by a factor of n = 75 ÷ 25 = 3. The Spearman-Brown prophecy formula predicts the new reliability.

    Workingrnew = nr ÷ (1 + (n - 1)r) = (3 × 0.7) ÷ (1 + 2 × 0.7) = 2.1 ÷ 2.4 = 0.875.

    Exam tipLonger tests are more reliable, with diminishing returns, as long as the added items are parallel. Halving the test (n = 0.5) would drop 0.7 to 0.35 ÷ 0.65 ≈ 0.54.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 0.700Leaves α unchanged; adding parallel items raises reliability, so tripling the length must push it above 0.70.
    • (2) 0.824This is what doubling gives: (2 × 0.7) ÷ (1 + 0.7) = 1.4 ÷ 1.7 ≈ 0.824. The test is tripled, not doubled.
    • (4) 0.931Overshoots: reaching 0.931 would need a test nearly six times as long (about 145 items); tripling gives 2.1 ÷ 2.4 = 0.875.

  98. Which one of the following correlations is most suitable for computing item-remainder correlations?

    • 1Tetrachoric Correlations
    • 2Phi Coefficient
    • 3Contingency Coefficient
    • 4Point biserial Correlation
    ✓ Answer: (4) Point biserial Correlation
    Point-biserial correlation in item analysis
    The concept

    Why this oneAn item-remainder correlation relates each item, scored 0/1 (a true dichotomy: right or wrong), with the total of the remaining items, a continuous score. Dichotomous with continuous → point-biserial r.

    Formularpb = ((Mp - Mq) ÷ st) × √(pq), where Mp and Mq are the mean remainder scores of those who passed and failed the item.

    Why 'remainder'The item is removed from the total first, so it is not correlated with itself. This stops the item-total correlation from being inflated, which matters most in short tests.

    Old keyThis page used to show (3) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Tetrachoric CorrelationsTetrachoric r is for two variables both artificially dichotomised from underlying normal continua; the remainder score here is continuous.
    • (2) Phi CoefficientPhi is for two true dichotomies, such as pass/fail on two items; it suits item-item, not item-remainder, correlation.
    • (3) Contingency CoefficientThe contingency coefficient C is for two categorical variables in a chi-square table; it does not fit a continuous remainder score.

  99. Consider the three reliability coefficients computed in the above study: (a) split-half reliability coefficient; (b) K-R reliability coefficient; and (c) Cronbach alpha. Which one of the following is true in the above context?

    • 1All the three reliability coefficients would be the same
    • 2All the three reliability coefficients would be different
    • 3(b) and (c) would be the same, but (a) can be different
    • 4(a) and (b) would be the same, but (b) can be different
    ✓ Answer: (3) (b) and (c) would be the same, but (a) can be different
    Lee Cronbach (1951): alpha equals KR-20 for 0/1 items
    The concept

    Why this oneFor items scored 0/1, each item's variance is pq, so Cronbach's α reduces exactly to KR-20; both equal 0.70 here. A split-half coefficient depends on how the test is split, so it can differ.

    Workingα = (k ÷ (k - 1)) × (1 - Σsi² ÷ sX²). Put si² = piqi for dichotomous items and you get KR-20: (k ÷ (k - 1)) × (1 - Σpq ÷ sX²).

    Split-halfSplit-half correlates two halves (e.g. odd vs even items) and steps it up with Spearman-Brown. Lee Cronbach showed α is the mean of all possible split-half coefficients, so any single split may be higher or lower.

    Exam tipKR-20 = α for dichotomous items. KR-21 assumes all items are equally difficult and gives a value equal to or lower than KR-20.

    Old keyThis page used to show (1) here, from a key that does not line up with this paper. See below for why it is wrong.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) All the three reliability coefficients would be the sameSplit-half reliability depends on the particular split and can differ; only KR-20 and α are mathematically identical for 0/1 items.
    • (2) All the three reliability coefficients would be differentK-R (KR-20) and Cronbach's α cannot differ here: with dichotomous items α reduces exactly to KR-20.
    • (4) (a) and (b) would be the same, but (b) can be differentGarbled and wrong: the split-half coefficient is the one that can differ, while K-R and α must be equal for 0/1 items.

4. June 2019 NET paper (answers worked out)

This is the UGC NET Psychology June 2019 question paper, reproduced verbatim from the source document. No official answer key was ever found for it, so every answer below is worked out from the standard textbooks and checked against a second, separate solve. Each question shows the answer and who it comes from, the concept in short chunks, and one line on why each other option is wrong. The paper is heavy on Assertion-Reason items, Match List I with List II items, and two passage-based statistics clusters (Q91 to 95 and Q96 to 100), each preceded by its passage.

Trap

These answers are worked out, not an official key. Best answer marks the 20 questions that are ambiguous, come from a niche source or look miscopied; read the "Not certain" line there, it names the runner-up option.

  1. Mr A approached the therapist for anger management. After Mr A narrated an incident in which he experienced anger, which of the following is the most appropriate way of further questioning at the initial level?

    • 1Could you please tell me why did you get angry?
    • 2Could you please explain what made you angry?
    • 3Please help me understand whom did you get angry at
    • 4Please explain how did you feel when you were angry
    ✓ Best answer: (2) Could you please explain what made you angry?
    Counselling skills: open, non-judgemental questioning
    The concept

    Why this oneWhat made you angry is an open, non-blaming question about the trigger of the anger, the first thing anger work needs to map.

    How it worksAnger management (for example Raymond Novaco's stress inoculation approach) starts by finding the provoking situations, then the thoughts, body signals and actions that follow.

    Exam tipSkilled interviewers avoid why questions early on: they sound accusing and invite excuses or intellectualising.

    Not certainExploring feelings first, option (4), is also defensible in a client-centred frame, but anger is already the named feeling, so the trigger question comes first.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Could you please tell me why did you get angry?A why question; it sounds like an accusation, makes the client defensive and invites justifications rather than a description of the trigger.
    • (3) Please help me understand whom did you get angry atA narrow question about the target person only; it misses the situation, thoughts and events that sparked the anger.
    • (4) Please explain how did you feel when you were angryAsks about the feeling, but the client has already said it was anger; feelings work usually follows once the trigger is clear.

  2. Which of the following are true for bootstrapping?

    • (a) It involves a large number of sub-samples.
    • (b) It does not rely on assumptions about the population.
    • (c) It is applicable in case of nonmetric data only.
    • (d) It is based on sample data only.

    Select the correct option

    • 1Only (a) and (b)
    • 2Only (a), (b) and (c)
    • 3Only (a), (b) and (d)
    • 4Only (b), (c) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (3) Only (a), (b) and (d)
    Bradley Efron, bootstrap resampling
    The concept

    Why this oneStatements (a), (b) and (d) are true. The bootstrap draws thousands of resamples with replacement from the one observed sample to estimate a statistic's sampling distribution.

    How it worksThe sample stands in for the population, so no assumption such as normality is needed; the spread of the resampled statistics gives the standard error and confidence interval.

    Why (c) is falseIt works with any data, including metric (interval and ratio) data such as means, correlations and regression weights.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Only (a) and (b)Misses (d): the bootstrap is built only from the observed sample data, with no outside population values, so (d) is also true.
    • (2) Only (a), (b) and (c)Includes (c), which is false, since bootstrapping is widely used with metric data; it also leaves out the true (d).
    • (4) Only (b), (c) and (d)Drops the true (a), the many resamples that define the method, and adds the false (c).

  3. In Torrance Test of Creative Thinking (figural), picture construction activity is scored for which of the following?

    • (a) Fluency
    • (b) Flexibility
    • (c) Originality
    • (d) Elaboration

    Select the correct option

    • 1Only (a) and (b)
    • 2Only (c) and (d)
    • 3Only (b), (c) and (d)
    • 4(a), (b), (c) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (2) Only (c) and (d)
    E. Paul Torrance, TTCT Figural scoring
    The concept

    Why this oneIn picture construction the child makes only one picture from a given shape, so there is nothing to count for fluency or flexibility; it is scored for originality and elaboration.

    Test layoutE. Paul Torrance's figural test has three activities: picture construction, picture completion and lines or circles. The last two give many responses, so fluency can also be scored there.

    Exam tipFluency = number of ideas; flexibility = number of categories; originality = statistical rarity; elaboration = amount of added detail.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Only (a) and (b)Fluency and flexibility need many responses to count, but picture construction yields a single drawing, so neither is scored there.
    • (3) Only (b), (c) and (d)Flexibility counts the categories across many responses; with only one picture it cannot be scored in this activity.
    • (4) (a), (b), (c) and (d)All four are scored across the figural test as a whole, but not in the single-picture construction activity.

  4. Arrange the following in correct sequence of development of emotional expressions:

    • (a) Self-conscious emotions emerge but depend on monitoring and encouragement of adults
    • (b) Expression of happiness increases while interacting with familiar people
    • (c) Anger and happiness increase in frequency and intensity
    • (d) Self-conscious emotions are clearly linked to self-evaluation

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(b) (a) (c) (d)
    • 2(b) (c) (a) (d)
    • 3(b) (c) (d) (a)
    • 4(c) (b) (a) (d)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (b) (c) (a) (d)
    Laura Berk, milestones of emotional development
    The concept

    Correct order(b) first 6 months: happiness is expressed more with familiar people → (c) 7 to 12 months: anger (and fear) rise in frequency and intensity → (a) 18 months to 2 years: self-conscious emotions appear but depend on adult monitoring → (d) early childhood: they become tied to self-evaluation.

    Why this orderBasic emotions come first. Self-conscious emotions (shame, guilt, pride, embarrassment) need a sense of self, which appears in the second year; judging oneself by inner standards comes later.

    Exam tipSelf-conscious emotions are first guided by adults' reactions, then by the child's own standards.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (b) (a) (c) (d)Puts adult-dependent self-conscious emotions (a) before the rise in anger (c), but anger intensifies late in the first year, well before self-conscious emotions appear.
    • (3) (b) (c) (d) (a)Places self-evaluation (d) before the adult-dependent stage (a); children first rely on adults' reactions and only later judge themselves by their own standards.
    • (4) (c) (b) (a) (d)Starts with rising anger (c), but greater happiness with familiar people (b) appears earlier, in the first six months along with the social smile.

  5. Limbic system, basal ganglia and corpus striatum are parts of which section of the brain?

    • 1Midbrain
    • 2Hindbrain
    • 3Telencephalon
    • 4Diencephalon
    ✓ Answer: (3) Telencephalon
    Divisions of the brain: telencephalon
    The concept

    Why this oneThe telencephalon is the largest part of the forebrain. It contains the cerebral cortex, the limbic system and the basal ganglia, whose main part is the corpus striatum.

    Brain divisionsForebrain = telencephalon + diencephalon (thalamus, hypothalamus). Midbrain = mesencephalon (tectum, tegmentum). Hindbrain = metencephalon (pons, cerebellum) + myelencephalon (medulla).

    Exam tipLimbic system and basal ganglia → telencephalon; thalamus and hypothalamus → diencephalon.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) MidbrainThe midbrain (mesencephalon) holds the tectum (superior and inferior colliculi) and the tegmentum with the substantia nigra, not the limbic system or striatum.
    • (2) HindbrainThe hindbrain holds the medulla, pons and cerebellum, which control vital functions, posture and balance.
    • (4) DiencephalonThe diencephalon is the thalamus and hypothalamus, the other half of the forebrain; the basal ganglia do not sit there.

  6. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): Focus group involves an unstructured interview.

    Reason (R): Focus group involves interaction among participants moderated by the researcher, as a source of data.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    Focus group method (moderated group interview)
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. A focus group is an unstructured interview with a small group (often 6 to 10 people), run by a moderator in a natural, conversational way.

    ReasonTrue. The data come from the group interaction: people react to, build on and challenge each other, with the researcher acting as moderator.

    LinkR explains A: because the interaction itself is the data, the moderator keeps the format open instead of working through a fixed list of questions.

    Not certainMany methods texts call focus groups semi-structured because the moderator uses a topic guide, which would make A doubtful and favour (4).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)R is not a side fact; the reliance on free participant interaction is exactly why the focus group interview is left unstructured.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseR is true: interaction among participants, guided by a moderator, is the defining source of focus group data.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is trueA is true in the usual definition: the focus group is a nonstructured, natural group interview, not a fixed questionnaire.

  7. Psychometric tests of creativity like the Torrance Tests of Creative Thinking measure creativity in terms of:

    • 1Person
    • 2Product
    • 3Process
    • 4Persuasion
    ✓ Best answer: (3) Process
    E. Paul Torrance; Mel Rhodes' four Ps of creativity
    The concept

    Why this oneDivergent-thinking tests such as the TTCT treat creativity as a thinking process: E. Paul Torrance defined it as sensing problems or gaps, forming and testing ideas, and communicating the results.

    The four PsMel Rhodes sorted creativity into person (traits), process (thinking), product (outputs) and press (environment). Psychometric divergent-thinking tests fall under process.

    Exam tipFluency, flexibility, originality and elaboration are indices of the divergent-thinking process, scored from the responses.

    Not certainBecause TTCT scores are counted from the responses produced, some texts describe it as a product measure, which would make (2) the answer.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) PersonThe person approach studies traits of creative people, such as openness or life history, using personality inventories and biographical checklists, not divergent-thinking tasks.
    • (2) ProductThe product approach judges finished creative works, for example by expert ratings in Teresa Amabile's consensual assessment technique, rather than measuring thinking skills.
    • (4) PersuasionPersuasion is the P added by Dean Keith Simonton: creativity as convincing others that an idea is valuable; tests do not measure it.

  8. Which of the following is true about radioimmunological assays?

    • 1These are methods to measure hormones
    • 2These methods were discovered by Jay Tepperman
    • 3These are anatomical methods
    • 4These measure brain structures
    ✓ Answer: (1) These are methods to measure hormones
    Rosalyn Yalow and Solomon Berson, radioimmunoassay
    The concept

    Why this oneRadioimmunoassay (RIA) measures tiny amounts of hormones and other substances in blood or tissue, using antibodies and radioactively labelled hormone.

    How it worksLabelled and unlabelled hormone compete to bind a fixed amount of antibody; the more natural hormone in the sample, the less radioactivity is bound, which gives its concentration.

    WhoIt was developed by Rosalyn Yalow and Solomon Berson; Yalow received the 1977 Nobel Prize for it.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) These methods were discovered by Jay TeppermanRIA was developed by Rosalyn Yalow and Solomon Berson; Jay Tepperman did not discover it.
    • (3) These are anatomical methodsAnatomical methods (histology, staining, tract tracing) show structure; RIA is a biochemical method that measures chemical concentrations.
    • (4) These measure brain structuresBrain structure is shown by imaging (CT, MRI) or histology; RIA measures hormone levels, not structures.

  9. A competent and well-performing female middle-level manager did not consider herself suitable for the position of senior manager, thinking that females are not suitable for such high positions. This is a typical example of which of the following?

    • 1Glass-ceiling phenomenon
    • 2Low motivation to approach success
    • 3Gender discrimination
    • 4High motivation to avoid failure
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Glass-ceiling phenomenon
    Glass ceiling (gender barrier to top positions)
    The concept

    Why this oneThe glass ceiling is an invisible barrier, built on attitudes and stereotypes, that keeps capable women from top posts. Here the stereotype is held by the manager herself, so she rules herself out.

    How it worksGender stereotypes that link leadership with men act from outside (biased promotions) and from inside (women doubting they fit); both keep women below senior level.

    Exam tipGlass ceiling = women stuck below the top; glass cliff = women promoted into risky, failing posts.

    Not certainHer self-limiting belief is internal, so it can also be read as low motivation to approach success (2), close to Matina Horner's fear of success.

    Why the other options are not the answer
    • (2) Low motivation to approach successA low achievement motive (David McClelland, John Atkinson) would show across tasks; she is competent and performing well, and the block comes from a gender stereotype.
    • (3) Gender discriminationGender discrimination is unfair treatment by others, such as being passed over; here no one has blocked her, she applies the stereotype to herself.
    • (4) High motivation to avoid failureAtkinson's motive to avoid failure is a general fear of failing; her doubt rests on a belief about women, not on fear of failure.

  10. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): Tolman's purposive behaviourism was considered to be a major advancement in the field of Psychology.

    Reason (R): Pre-Tolman behaviourism excessively emphasized S-R relations and ignored cognitive processes.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    Edward Tolman, purposive behaviourism
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. Edward Tolman kept behaviourist methods but argued that behaviour is purposive (goal-directed) and guided by expectancies and cognitive maps.

    ReasonTrue. Earlier behaviourism, led by John B. Watson, reduced behaviour to stimulus-response links and excluded mental processes.

    LinkR explains A: Tolman's advance was bringing cognition back through intervening variables, filling the gap left by pure S-R theory. He is seen as a bridge to cognitive psychology.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)R is the very reason Tolman mattered: he added the cognitive variables that S-R behaviourism had left out.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseR is true: John B. Watson's behaviourism studied only observable stimulus-response links and rejected mental concepts.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is trueA is true: Tolman's latent-learning and cognitive-map studies are seen as a major step towards cognitive psychology.

  11. Which of the following are characteristics of post-trauma growth?

    • (a) Relationships are strengthened or enhanced
    • (b) Self-views become more positive
    • (c) Life philosophies become ambiguous
    • (d) Greater acceptance of personal limitations

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 2(a), (b) and (d) only
    • 3(b), (c) and (d)
    • 4(a), (c) and (d) only
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a), (b) and (d) only
    Richard Tedeschi and Lawrence Calhoun, post-traumatic growth
    The concept

    Why this oneStatements (a), (b) and (d) are true. After trauma many people report closer relationships, a stronger, more positive self-view, and greater acceptance of their own limits and vulnerability.

    Three areasGrowth shows in self-perception (strength plus accepting limits), relationships (closeness, compassion) and life philosophy (new priorities, more appreciation of life, spiritual change).

    Why (c) is falseLife philosophies become clearer, not ambiguous: people rethink their priorities and value life more.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (b) and (c) onlyIncludes (c), which is false, since philosophy of life becomes clearer and more meaningful; it also drops the true (d).
    • (3) (b), (c) and (d)Includes the false (c) and leaves out (a), the strengthening of relationships, a core area of growth.
    • (4) (a), (c) and (d) onlyIncludes the false (c) and leaves out (b), the stronger and more positive self-view people report.

  12. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Psychologist): (a) Binet and Simon; (b) Terman; (c) Thorndike; (d) Eysenck M W

    List-II (Definition): (i) The power of good response from the perspective of truth or fact; (ii) The capacity to judge, understand and reason well; (iii) The capacity to learn concepts and grasp their significance; (iv) Error-free transmission of information through the cortex

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)
    • 2(a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iii); (d)-(iv)
    • 3(a)-(ii); (b)-(iii); (c)-(i); (d)-(iv)
    • 4(a)-(iii); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iv); (d)-(i)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a)-(ii); (b)-(iii); (c)-(i); (d)-(iv)
    Classic definitions of intelligence: Binet, Terman, Thorndike, Eysenck
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Alfred Binet and Théodore Simon = (ii) to judge, understand and reason well; (b) Lewis Terman = (iii) capacity to learn concepts and grasp their significance; (c) Edward Thorndike = (i) power of good responses from the point of view of truth or fact; (d) Eysenck = (iv) error-free transmission of information through the cortex.

    How to rememberBinet: judgement is the core of intelligence. Terman: abstract thinking. Thorndike: good responses (a connectionist view). Eysenck: a biological, neural-efficiency view.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)Gives Binet and Simon the biological definition, which is Eysenck's, and Eysenck their judgement definition; Terman and Thorndike are right.
    • (2) (a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iii); (d)-(iv)Gives Binet and Simon Thorndike's 'good responses' definition and Terman the judgement definition; only Eysenck is paired correctly.
    • (4) (a)-(iii); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iv); (d)-(i)Every pair is wrong: for example Thorndike gets Eysenck's neural definition and Eysenck gets Thorndike's 'good responses'.

  13. Skills that are operated automatically and without thinking are noted in which memory?

    • 1Implicit memory
    • 2Explicit memory
    • 3Flashbulb memory
    • 4Autobiographical memory
    ✓ Answer: (1) Implicit memory
    Implicit versus explicit memory (Daniel Schacter)
    The concept

    Why this oneImplicit memory shows itself in performance without conscious recollection. Skills that run automatically, such as typing or cycling, are its procedural part.

    How it worksImplicit (non-declarative) memory covers procedural skills, priming and classical conditioning; it relies on the basal ganglia and cerebellum more than the hippocampus.

    Exam tipExplicit = 'knowing that' (can be put into words); implicit = 'knowing how' (shown by doing).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Explicit memoryExplicit (declarative) memory is conscious recall of facts and events, split by Endel Tulving into semantic and episodic memory.
    • (3) Flashbulb memoryFlashbulb memory (Roger Brown and James Kulik) is a vivid, detailed memory of the moment one heard shocking news.
    • (4) Autobiographical memoryAutobiographical memory is the conscious record of one's own life events, a kind of explicit, episodic memory.

  14. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): According to classical theory of test scores, total variance is equal to true variance plus error variance.

    Reason (R): Classical theory of test scores assumes a significant but low positive correlation between true scores and error scores.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (3) (A) is true but (R) is false
    Classical test theory (true score model)
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. In classical test theory X = T + E, so total variance = true variance + error variance (σ2X = σ2T + σ2E).

    ReasonFalse. The model assumes errors are random, so the correlation between true scores and error scores is zero, not low positive.

    Why it mattersThat zero correlation lets the variances add with no covariance term; reliability is then σ2T / σ2X.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)R is false: classical theory assumes true and error scores are uncorrelated (r = 0), so it cannot explain A.
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)R is not true; any correlation between true and error scores would add a covariance term and break the simple variance sum.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is trueA is true: total variance splitting into true plus error variance is the basic equation of classical test theory.

  15. What is the term given to 'autonomy, or being self-determining, independent and able to resist social pressures to think and act in certain ways'?

    • 1Emotional well-being
    • 2Social well-being
    • 3Psychological well-being
    • 4Physical well-being
    ✓ Answer: (3) Psychological well-being
    Carol Ryff, psychological well-being model (1989)
    The concept

    Why this oneAutonomy (self-determination, independence, resisting social pressure, judging oneself by personal standards) is one of the six dimensions of psychological well-being in Carol Ryff's model.

    Six dimensionsSelf-acceptance, positive relations with others, autonomy, environmental mastery, purpose in life and personal growth.

    Exam tipRyff's model is eudaimonic (functioning well); Ed Diener's subjective well-being is hedonic (feeling good).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Emotional well-beingEmotional well-being is the hedonic side: positive feelings, few negative feelings and life satisfaction, as in subjective well-being.
    • (2) Social well-beingSocial well-being (Corey Keyes) covers functioning in society: social integration, contribution, acceptance, actualisation and coherence.
    • (4) Physical well-beingPhysical well-being is bodily health and fitness, not a psychological quality such as autonomy.

  16. Which of the following are the behaviours identified by Maslow that lead to self-actualization?

    • (a) Growth choices
    • (b) Use of ego defence
    • (c) Self-awareness
    • (d) Concentration

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 2(a), (c) and (d) only
    • 3(c) and (b) only
    • 4(a), (b) and (d) only
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a), (c) and (d) only
    Abraham Maslow, behaviours leading to self-actualisation
    The concept

    Why this oneAbraham Maslow listed behaviours that lead to self-actualisation; (a) making growth choices, (c) self-awareness and (d) full concentration (experiencing life with total absorption) are among them.

    Why (b) is falseMaslow said we must identify and give up ego defences, not use them, because defences distort reality and block growth.

    Other behavioursBeing honest and taking responsibility, trusting one's own judgement, developing one's potential and having peak experiences.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (b) and (c) onlyIncludes (b), but Maslow asked people to give up their ego defences; it also leaves out concentration.
    • (3) (c) and (b) onlySelf-awareness fits but use of ego defence does not; the pair also misses growth choices and concentration.
    • (4) (a), (b) and (d) onlyIncludes the use of ego defence, which Maslow saw as a barrier to growth, and leaves out self-awareness.

  17. The vicious circles that maintain depression, loneliness and shyness can be broken by social skill training, by positive experience that alters self-perception, by changing negative thought patterns. This is the description of which social-psychological approach to treatment?

    • 1Social skill training
    • 2Explanatory style therapy
    • 3Internal attribution
    • 4Social influence
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Social skill training
    David Myers, breaking vicious cycles in treatment
    The concept

    Why this oneThe description is David Myers' 'breaking vicious cycles' approach, and social skills training is its main tool: practised skills bring social success, and that success changes how people see themselves.

    How it worksDepressed, lonely and shy people act in ways that bring rejection, which confirms their negative self-image. Social skills training and explanatory style therapy (changing negative attributions) break this loop.

    Exam tipMyers' approaches: changing behaviour to change inner states, breaking vicious cycles, keeping gains through internal attributions, and therapy as social influence.

    Not certainChanging negative thought patterns is the core of explanatory style therapy (2), the other technique in the same 'breaking vicious cycles' approach, so (2) is the runner-up.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Explanatory style therapyExplanatory style therapy covers only the thinking strand: it retrains self-blaming, stable, global explanations of failure.
    • (3) Internal attributionInternal attribution is how change is maintained: people keep improvements when they credit them to their own effort rather than to the treatment.
    • (4) Social influenceSocial influence is the view that therapy works partly through persuasion by a credible therapist, not the vicious-cycle approach described.

  18. Which of the following is a step to transformative change in which an unpredictable and often affective experience occurs?

    • 1Integration
    • 2Preparation
    • 3Inspiration
    • 4Organic inquiry
    ✓ Answer: (3) Inspiration
    Organic inquiry, transpersonal research method (Jennifer Clements)
    The concept

    Why this oneIn organic inquiry, change happens in three steps; the middle step, inspiration, is the unpredictable, often emotional experience in which new insight breaks through.

    Correct orderPreparation (immersing in the topic) → inspiration (the unplanned, affective breakthrough) → integration (making sense of it and letting it change the person).

    What it isOrganic inquiry is a qualitative, transpersonal research approach in which the personal transformation of researcher and participants is part of the findings.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) IntegrationIntegration is the final step, when the insight is understood and absorbed into daily life; it is reflective rather than sudden.
    • (2) PreparationPreparation is the first, deliberate step of getting ready and immersing in the topic, before any unplanned experience occurs.
    • (4) Organic inquiryOrganic inquiry is the name of the whole research method, not one of its steps.

  19. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): Clients must be allowed complete freedom to discuss in their natural manner to communicate respect and acceptance.

    Reason (R): Completely suspending judgement and expressing positive regard are Rogers' core therapeutic conditions.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    Carl Rogers, client-centred therapy core conditions
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. In client-centred therapy the client leads and talks in their own way; letting them do so shows respect and acceptance.

    ReasonTrue in substance. Suspending judgement while valuing the client is unconditional positive regard, one of Carl Rogers' core conditions with empathy and congruence.

    LinkR explains A: the freedom given to the client is how the therapist expresses non-judgemental, unconditional positive regard.

    Not certainR names only part of Rogers' three core conditions (it leaves out congruence and empathy), so some would treat it as incomplete and prefer (2).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)R is the basis of A: allowing free expression is how the therapist puts non-judgemental positive regard into practice.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseR is true: unconditional positive regard means accepting the client without judgement, and it is one of Rogers' core conditions.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is trueA is true: client-centred therapy is non-directive, so the client is free to explore in their own way.

  20. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Concept): (a) Reward power; (b) Coercive power; (c) Referent power; (d) Legitimate power

    List-II (Description): (i) Potential to deliver threats and punishment to others; (ii) Authority of a person by virtue of certain characteristics; (iii) Positive reinforcement to produce change; (iv) Power to influence others

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a)-(iii); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iv); (d)-(i)
    • 2(a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(ii); (d)-(i)
    • 3(a)-(iii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(ii)
    • 4(a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iv); (d)-(iii)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a)-(iii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(ii)
    John French and Bertram Raven, bases of social power (1959)
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Reward power = (iii) positive reinforcement to produce change; (b) Coercive power = (i) threats and punishment; (c) Referent power = (iv) power to influence others through liking and identification; (d) Legitimate power = (ii) authority held by virtue of certain characteristics such as role or position.

    What it isJohn French and Bertram Raven named five bases of power: reward, coercive, legitimate, referent and expert; Raven later added informational power.

    Exam tipReward and coercion work through consequences; legitimate power through accepted authority; referent power through people wanting to be like the leader.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(iii); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iv); (d)-(i)Gives coercive power the authority description and legitimate power the threats description; coercion is about punishment, legitimacy about recognised authority.
    • (2) (a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(ii); (d)-(i)Shifts every pair: reward power is giving positive reinforcement, not general influence, and coercive power is threats, not reinforcement.
    • (4) (a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iv); (d)-(iii)Gives reward power the punishment description; rewards are positive reinforcement, while threats and punishment define coercive power.

  21. Which of the following explains the theory's ability to organize and explain several otherwise disjointed observations?

    • 1Heuristic function
    • 2Synthesizing function
    • 3Principle of verification
    • 4Scientific function
    ✓ Answer: (2) Synthesizing function
    B. R. Hergenhahn, functions of scientific theory
    The concept

    Why this oneA theory's synthesising function is its power to organise and explain many observations that would otherwise stand apart, tying them into one framework.

    Two functionsB. R. Hergenhahn describes two functions of a scientific theory: the synthesising function (organising known facts) and the heuristic function (generating new research).

    Exam tipSynthesising looks back, organising old facts; heuristic looks forward, prompting new studies.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Heuristic functionThe heuristic function is a theory's ability to generate new research and hypotheses, guiding future observation rather than organising existing ones.
    • (3) Principle of verificationThe principle of verification, from logical positivism, says a statement is meaningful only if it can be checked by observation.
    • (4) Scientific functionNot a named function of theory; the two standard functions are synthesising and heuristic.

  22. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): According to Rogers, all people are capable of growth and personal development.

    Reason (R): Positive change is a natural and expected progression.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    Carl Rogers, actualising tendency
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. Carl Rogers held that every person has the capacity for growth, self-direction and personal development.

    ReasonTrue. Rogers saw positive change as natural because of the actualising tendency, an inborn drive to develop one's potential.

    LinkR explains A: people can grow because growth is the natural, expected course once the right conditions (genuineness, unconditional positive regard, empathy) are present.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)R is the reason behind A: the actualising tendency makes positive change natural, which is why everyone is seen as capable of growth.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseR is true: for Rogers, growth is the natural direction of the organism, blocked only by conditions of worth.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is trueA is true: Rogers' view of human nature is optimistic, holding that everyone can grow and develop.

  23. Jung identified eight personality types on the basis of which of the following?

    • 1Two attitudes and four functions
    • 2Four attitudes and two functions
    • 3Four attitudes and four functions
    • 4Two attitudes and six functions
    ✓ Answer: (1) Two attitudes and four functions
    Carl Jung, psychological types
    The concept

    Why this oneCarl Jung crossed two attitudes (extraversion, introversion) with four functions (thinking, feeling, sensing, intuiting), giving 2 × 4 = 8 types.

    How it worksThinking and feeling are rational (judging) functions; sensing and intuiting are irrational (perceiving). An extraverted thinking type, for example, turns logical judgement outward.

    Exam tipThe Myers-Briggs Type Indicator built on Jung's types by adding a judging-perceiving dimension, giving 16 types.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Four attitudes and two functionsReverses the numbers: Jung described only two attitudes (extraversion, introversion) and four functions.
    • (3) Four attitudes and four functionsFour by four would give 16 types, the Myers-Briggs number; Jung's model has only two attitudes.
    • (4) Two attitudes and six functionsJung named four functions, not six; two attitudes by six functions would give 12 types, not eight.

  24. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): If all the assumptions are fulfilled, addition of any predictor in the Multiple Regression Analysis will increase the value of the coefficient of determination.

    Reason (R): Correlation of the criterion variable with the predictor variables can be positive or negative.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is false
    ✓ Best answer: (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    Multiple regression: coefficient of determination (R squared)
    The concept

    AssertionTrue in the usual sense. Adding a predictor can never lower R2; it rises by the new predictor's squared semipartial correlation, which cannot be negative.

    ReasonTrue. A predictor can relate to the criterion positively or negatively; the sign only sets the sign of its regression weight.

    LinkR does not explain A: R2 grows because explained variance cannot shrink when a variable is added, whatever the sign. This is why adjusted R2 is used to penalise extra predictors.

    Not certainStrictly, R squared stays unchanged when a new predictor adds no unique variance, so A could be judged false, favouring (4), whose printed wording looks like a misprint.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)The sign of the correlation does not drive the rise in R squared; an added predictor adds non-negative explained variance whether it correlates positively or negatively.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseR is true: criterion-predictor correlations can be positive or negative, and both kinds contribute to prediction.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is falseA is true in the usual sense, since R squared never falls when a predictor is added, and R is true, not false.

  25. A 30-year-old man identified as Mr K C suffered a serious motorcycle accident. Mr K C remembers many things but cannot remember everything. Mr K C seems to have which memory loss?

    • 1Semantic
    • 2Episodic
    • 3Procedural
    • 4Working memory
    ✓ Answer: (2) Episodic
    Endel Tulving, patient K.C. (episodic amnesia)
    The concept

    Why this oneK.C., studied by Endel Tulving, lost his episodic memory after a motorcycle accident: he could recall no personal events from his life, yet kept his general knowledge.

    What it isEpisodic memory stores personally experienced events tied to a time and place ('my last birthday'); semantic memory stores facts and word meanings ('Paris is in France').

    Exam tipK.C. is the classic case showing that episodic and semantic memory are separate systems.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) SemanticK.C.'s semantic memory (facts, language, general knowledge) stayed largely intact; what he lost was memory for his own experiences.
    • (3) ProceduralProcedural memory (skills such as playing cards or riding a bicycle) was spared in K.C., as in most amnesic patients.
    • (4) Working memoryWorking memory, the short-term workspace for holding and handling information, was largely normal in K.C.; his loss was long-term recall of personal episodes.

  26. While conducting an experiment, the researcher manipulates the independent variable within a pre-decided range. Which of the following describes this type of research?

    • 1Quasi-experiment
    • 2Random effect model
    • 3Fixed effect model
    • 4Mixed-method approach
    ✓ Answer: (3) Fixed effect model
    Fixed versus random effects in ANOVA
    The concept

    Why this oneIn a fixed effect model the researcher deliberately chooses the levels of the independent variable in advance (for example 0, 10 and 20 mg), so conclusions apply only to those levels.

    Random effectsIn a random effect model the levels are drawn at random from a larger population of possible levels, so the results generalise to all levels.

    Exam tipLevels picked by the experimenter = fixed; levels sampled by chance = random; a design with both kinds of factor = mixed model.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Quasi-experimentLacks random assignment of participants, usually because it uses pre-existing groups; it says nothing about how the levels of the independent variable are chosen.
    • (2) Random effect modelHere the levels of a factor are a random sample from a wider population of levels, not a range the researcher fixes in advance.
    • (4) Mixed-method approachCombines quantitative and qualitative data collection in one study; it is not about choosing levels of an independent variable.

  27. Consideration of mental health needs of a person with cancer will have positive effects on:

    • (a) Quality of life
    • (b) Emotional well-being
    • (c) Positive biological changes
    • (d) Killing carcinogenic cells

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a) and (b) only
    • 2(c) and (d) only
    • 3(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 4(b), (c) and (d) only
    ✓ Best answer: (3) (a), (b) and (c) only
    Psycho-oncology and psychoneuroimmunology
    The concept

    Why this oneCaring for the mental health of a person with cancer improves quality of life and emotional well-being, and lowering stress can bring positive biological changes, such as healthier stress-hormone and immune functioning.

    Mind-body linkPsychoneuroimmunology studies how stress acts on the immune system through the HPA axis and cortisol; reducing stress and depression helps the body's regulatory systems.

    Why not (d)Psychological care does not directly kill cancer cells; that is the job of surgery, chemotherapy, radiotherapy and other medical treatment.

    Not certainEvidence that psychological care produces biological change is suggestive rather than settled, so a cautious reading gives (1), quality of life and emotional well-being only.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) and (b) onlyLeaves out (c): reducing stress and depression in people with cancer is linked to measurable bodily gains, such as healthier cortisol rhythms and immune function.
    • (2) (c) and (d) onlyLeaves out the clearest benefits, (a) and (b), and includes (d), which is false because psychological care does not destroy cancer cells.
    • (4) (b), (c) and (d) onlyIncludes (d), which is false, and leaves out (a), quality of life, the best-documented benefit of psychological care in cancer.

  28. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): In simple regression analysis, if the value of one regression coefficient (bxy) is less than 1.00, the value of the second regression coefficient (byx) has to be less than 1.00.

    Reason (R): The correlation between two variables x and y is equal to the geometric mean of the two regression coefficients (bxy and byx).

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (4) (A) is false but (R) is true
    Properties of regression coefficients
    The concept

    AssertionFalse. Because r2 = bxy × byx and r2 cannot exceed 1, both coefficients cannot be greater than 1; but if one is below 1 the other can still exceed 1 (0.5 × 1.6 gives r2 = 0.8).

    ReasonTrue. The correlation coefficient is the geometric mean of the two regression coefficients: r = √(bxy × byx), taking the common sign of the two.

    Exam tipIf one regression coefficient is more than 1, the other must be less than 1; the reverse does not hold.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)(A) is false, so (R) cannot explain it; the geometric-mean rule only forbids both coefficients from exceeding 1 at the same time.
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)(A) is false: one coefficient below 1 does not force the other below 1, since 0.5 and 1.6 give a valid r2 of 0.8.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseReverses the truth: (R) is the true statement, r = √(bxy × byx), and (A) is the false one.

  29. Which one of the following is the correct answer for the experiment conducted at the University of California at Santa Barbara, in which individuals running on a jogging path speeded up as they came upon a woman seated on the grass facing them rather than sitting with her back turned?

    • 1Distraction conflict
    • 2Presence of co-actor
    • 3Evaluation apprehension
    • 4Free riders
    ✓ Answer: (3) Evaluation apprehension
    Charles Worringham and David Messick, jogging-path study
    The concept

    Why this oneJoggers sped up only when the woman faced them and could watch them, not when her back was turned; her presence was the same, so the boost came from concern about being evaluated.

    What it isEvaluation apprehension (Nickolas Cottrell) holds that others arouse us because we worry about how they will judge us, and this arousal strengthens dominant, well-learned responses such as running.

    Exam tipRobert Zajonc = mere presence arousal; Cottrell = evaluation apprehension; Robert Baron = distraction-conflict.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Distraction conflictRobert Baron's distraction-conflict theory says an audience splits attention between task and observers; it does not explain why facing versus turning away made the difference.
    • (2) Presence of co-actorMeans someone doing the same task alongside you, as in Norman Triplett's racing cyclists; the woman was sitting on the grass, not jogging.
    • (4) Free ridersGroup members who slack off and benefit from others' effort, a feature of social loafing when individual output cannot be identified.

  30. Which one of the following is NOT a characteristic of attention?

    • 1Processing capacity and selectiveness
    • 2Automatic processing
    • 3Initial detection of stimuli
    • 4Control
    ✓ Answer: (3) Initial detection of stimuli
    Features of attention: capacity, selectivity, control, automaticity
    The concept

    Why this oneInitial detection of stimuli is the job of sensation, the receptors registering energy from the world; attention works on that input afterwards by selecting what gets further processing.

    Features of attentionAttention has limited processing capacity, so it must be selective; it can be steered under voluntary control; and well-practised tasks become automatic, needing little attention.

    Exam tipSensation = detecting; perception = interpreting; attention = selecting and focusing limited mental resources.

    Why the other options are not the answer
    • (1) Processing capacity and selectivenessFits: attention is a limited-capacity resource, so it must select some inputs and ignore others, as Donald Broadbent's filter model proposed.
    • (2) Automatic processingFits: practised tasks run with little attentional effort; Walter Schneider and Richard Shiffrin contrasted such automatic processing with effortful controlled processing.
    • (4) ControlFits: attention can be directed voluntarily (top-down) or captured by salient stimuli (bottom-up), so control of attention is a core feature.

  31. What is the theoretical approach called which attempts to explain completely the phenomenon of one field of study in terms of the dynamics of a more basic field?

    • 1Interactionism
    • 2Reductionism
    • 3Pragmatism
    • 4Compliant solution
    ✓ Answer: (2) Reductionism
    Reductionism, philosophy of science
    The concept

    Why this oneReductionism explains a phenomenon completely in terms of a more basic field, for example explaining depression entirely by neurotransmitters, or behaviour entirely by physiology.

    How it worksPsychology is reduced to biology, biology to chemistry, chemistry to physics; critics reply that whole systems show emergent properties their parts lack, the Gestalt point that the whole differs from the sum of its parts.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) InteractionismHolds that two factors, such as mind and body or person and situation, influence each other; it does not reduce one field to another.
    • (3) PragmatismThe philosophy of William James and John Dewey that judges ideas by their practical consequences and usefulness.
    • (4) Compliant solutionKaren Horney's compliant (self-effacing) solution is a neurotic trend of moving toward people to win love and approval; it is not a theory of explanation.

  32. According to Adler, which of the following is used by the neurotic to escape from problems?

    • 1Depreciation
    • 2Self-accusation
    • 3Distancing
    • 4Safeguarding strategies
    ✓ Best answer: (3) Distancing
    Alfred Adler, safeguarding through distance
    The concept

    Why this oneAlfred Adler said neurotics escape life's tasks (work, love, friendship) by keeping a distance from them. He called this withdrawal, or safeguarding through distance.

    Four formsMoving backward (retreating to a safer, younger way of living), standing still (not moving at all), hesitating (putting things off) and constructing obstacles (a small barrier to blame).

    The bigger pictureDistancing is one of three safeguarding tendencies that protect a fragile self-esteem: excuses ('Yes, but'), aggression (depreciation, accusation, self-accusation) and withdrawal.

    Not certainSome keys may pick (4), the umbrella term, since all three kinds are safeguarding strategies; but the one that is itself an escape from problems is distancing.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) DepreciationBelittling others and overvaluing oneself; an aggressive safeguarding tendency that attacks people, not one that escapes problems.
    • (2) Self-accusationUsing guilt and self-torment to hurt the people close to you; another aggressive safeguarding tendency, not a way of escaping.
    • (4) Safeguarding strategiesThe umbrella term for all three kinds (excuses, aggression, withdrawal); they protect self-esteem, and the one that escapes problems is distancing.

  33. Which among the following are the assumptions on which Binet developed his first test of intelligence?

    • (a) Mental competence increases over the childhood years
    • (b) A child's relative standing in mental development compared to his age group will remain constant as the child grows.
    • (c) A child's relative standing in mental development compared to his age group increases as the child grows
    • (d) Mental competence decreases over the childhood years

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a) and (b) only
    • 2(a) and (c) only
    • 3(b) and (c) only
    • 4(b) and (d) only
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a) and (b) only
    Alfred Binet and Theodore Simon, Binet-Simon scale
    The concept
