    Why this oneAlfred Binet assumed that mental competence grows through childhood, so tasks could be graded by the age at which most children pass them, and that a child's relative standing among age-mates stays roughly constant.

    Mental ageThese assumptions gave the idea of mental age: a child who passes the items typical of 10-year-olds has a mental age of 10, whatever the chronological age.

    Exam tipConstant relative standing later underpinned William Stern's ratio IQ (MA/CA × 100), which stays stable only if a child keeps the same position.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (a) and (c) onlyStatement (c) is false: if relative standing kept rising, mental age would not give a stable index of how a child compares with peers.
    • (3) (b) and (c) onlyLeaves out (a), the core assumption that mental competence increases with age, and includes the false statement (c).
    • (4) (b) and (d) onlyStatement (d) contradicts the age-scale idea: Binet's items are graded precisely because competence increases, not decreases, across childhood.

  34. On a Wechsler-type Adult Intelligence Scale, having normally distributed scores, a subject had a percentile rank of 84. His deviation IQ would be:

    • 184
    • 285
    • 3115
    • 4120
    ✓ Answer: (3) 115
    David Wechsler's deviation IQ, mean 100, SD 15
    The concept

    Why this oneOn a normal curve about 84% of scores fall below +1 SD (50% below the mean plus 34% between the mean and +1 SD). Wechsler scales use mean 100 and SD 15, so IQ = 100 + 15 = 115.

    Workingz = (IQ - 100) / 15. Percentile 84 gives z = +1, so IQ = 100 + (1 × 15) = 115.

    Exam tipPercentiles 16, 50, 84 and 98 match Wechsler IQs of about 85, 100, 115 and 130.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 84Confuses the percentile rank with the IQ; a percentile is the percentage of people scoring below a person, not a point on the IQ scale.
    • (2) 85That IQ is 1 SD below the mean, which corresponds to the 16th percentile, not the 84th.
    • (4) 120That IQ is about 1.33 SD above the mean, roughly the 91st percentile.

  35. According to self-determination theory, which of the following form the basis of intrinsic motivation?

    • 1Experience, Competence
    • 2Competence, Autonomy
    • 3Competence, Novelty Seeking
    • 4Novelty Seeking, Sensation Seeking
    ✓ Answer: (2) Competence, Autonomy
    Edward Deci and Richard Ryan, self-determination theory
    The concept

    Why this oneEdward Deci and Richard Ryan argue that intrinsic motivation rests on feeling competent (effective) and autonomous (self-directed); rewards that undermine either feeling reduce it.

    Three needsSelf-determination theory names three basic psychological needs: competence, autonomy and relatedness; relatedness supports intrinsic motivation, but competence with autonomy is its core.

    Exam tipControlling rewards lower intrinsic motivation (the overjustification effect); informative feedback that raises felt competence increases it.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Experience, CompetenceOnly half right: experience is not one of Deci and Ryan's basic needs, which are competence, autonomy and relatedness.
    • (3) Competence, Novelty SeekingOnly half right: novelty seeking is a temperament dimension in Robert Cloninger's model, not a basic need in self-determination theory.
    • (4) Novelty Seeking, Sensation SeekingBoth are personality traits, novelty seeking from Robert Cloninger and sensation seeking from Marvin Zuckerman; neither is a basic need in self-determination theory.

  36. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Concept): (a) Naturalistic intelligence; (b) Cognitive processing speed; (c) Meta-components; (d) Motivating oneself

    List-II (Description): (i) Skill critical and important for archaeologists and botanists; (ii) Ability to execute easy and highly overlearned cognitive tasks; (iii) Mental processes individuals use effectively to guide their problem-solving efforts; (iv) Delaying gratification and stifling impulsiveness

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iii); (d)-(iv)
    • 2(a)-(ii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(iii)
    • 3(a)-(iii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)
    • 4(a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(ii); (d)-(i)
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iii); (d)-(iv)
    Howard Gardner, Robert Sternberg, Daniel Goleman; CHC processing speed
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Naturalistic intelligence = (i) skill for archaeologists and botanists; (b) Cognitive processing speed = (ii) executing easy, overlearned tasks; (c) Meta-components = (iii) processes that guide problem-solving; (d) Motivating oneself = (iv) delaying gratification, stifling impulsiveness.

    SourcesNaturalistic intelligence (recognising and classifying living things) is Howard Gardner's eighth intelligence; processing speed (Gs) is a broad ability in the Cattell-Horn-Carroll model.

    More sourcesMeta-components are the executive planning and monitoring processes in Robert Sternberg's componential subtheory; motivating oneself is a domain of Daniel Goleman's emotional intelligence.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (a)-(ii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(iii)Swaps (a) and (b), and (c) and (d): fluent execution of overlearned tasks is processing speed, and delaying gratification belongs to motivating oneself.
    • (3) (a)-(iii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)Gives meta-components to the botanist's skill and motivating oneself to overlearned tasks; meta-components guide problem-solving and self-motivation means delaying gratification.
    • (4) (a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(ii); (d)-(i)Reverses every pair; for instance, delaying gratification belongs to motivating oneself, not to naturalistic intelligence.

  37. Which of the following is/are true about rhodopsin?

    • (a) It is a rod pigment that enables vision in low light
    • (b) It is a cone pigment that enables vision in bright light
    • (c) It is a G-protein coupled receptor
    • (d) It is a cone pigment and G-protein coupled receptor.

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a) only
    • 2(a), (c) and (d) only
    • 3(b) only
    • 4(a) and (c) only
    ✓ Answer: (4) (a) and (c) only
    Rhodopsin, the rod photopigment
    The concept

    Why this oneRhodopsin is the photopigment in rods, which give sensitive, colourless vision in dim light; it is also a G-protein coupled receptor, so (a) and (c) are true.

    How it worksLight changes the retinal (a vitamin A derivative) inside rhodopsin, which activates the G-protein transducin; this closes sodium channels and hyperpolarises the rod.

    Exam tipRods = rhodopsin, scotopic (night) vision; cones = three photopsins, photopic colour vision.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) onlyTrue but incomplete: rhodopsin is also a G-protein coupled receptor, so (c) is true as well.
    • (2) (a), (c) and (d) onlyIncludes (d), which is false: rhodopsin is found in rods, not cones; cones use their own opsins.
    • (3) (b) onlyStatement (b) is false: bright-light colour vision depends on cone pigments (photopsins), while rhodopsin is bleached in bright light.

  38. Mrs B has attained the level of 'self' in that she now takes genuine pleasure in praying, meditation and other spiritual activities. Which type of 'self' is this in Sufism?

    • 1Serene Self
    • 2Inspired Self
    • 3Pleased Self
    • 4Pure Self
    ✓ Answer: (2) Inspired Self
    Robert Frager, Sufi seven levels of the self (nafs)
    The concept

    Why this oneIn Sufism the Inspired Self (nafs mulhima) is the stage where a person first takes genuine pleasure in prayer, meditation and other spiritual practices, rather than doing them only out of duty.

    Correct orderSeven levels of nafs: Tyrannical (ammara), Regretful (lawwama), Inspired (mulhima), Serene (mutma'inna), Pleased (radiya), Pleasing to God (mardiyya), Pure (safiya).

    Exam tipRegretful = first insight into one's faults; Inspired = joy in spiritual practice; Serene = inner peace; Pure = the ego dissolved in union with God.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Serene SelfThe fourth level (nafs mutma'inna), after the Inspired Self; its mark is deep inner peace and contentment.
    • (3) Pleased SelfThe fifth level (nafs radiya), content with whatever God sends, hardship included, not simply delighting in spiritual practice.
    • (4) Pure SelfThe seventh and highest level (nafs safiya), where the ego has dissolved and the person lives in full unity with God.

  39. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Concept): (a) Mental representation; (b) Operations; (c) Adaptation; (d) Assimilation

    List-II (Explanation): (i) Building schemas through direct interaction with the environment; (ii) Using current schemas to interpret the external world; (iii) The ability to remember and copy the behaviour of models who are not present; (iv) Mental representation of actions that obey logical rules

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)
    • 2(a)-(iv); (b)-(ii); (c)-(i); (d)-(iii)
    • 3(a)-(iii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)
    • 4(a)-(ii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(iii)
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a)-(iii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)
    Jean Piaget's cognitive-developmental theory
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Mental representation = (iii) remembering and copying absent models (deferred imitation); (b) Operations = (iv) mental actions that obey logical rules; (c) Adaptation = (i) building schemas through direct interaction with the environment; (d) Assimilation = (ii) using current schemas to interpret the world.

    Key ideasJean Piaget saw adaptation as two complementary processes: assimilation (fitting the world into existing schemas) and accommodation (changing schemas to fit the world).

    Exam tipDeferred imitation appears at the end of the sensorimotor stage and shows the child can hold a mental representation; operations first appear in the concrete operational stage.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)Swaps (a) and (b): the logical-rules description belongs to operations, and copying absent models shows mental representation.
    • (2) (a)-(iv); (b)-(ii); (c)-(i); (d)-(iii)Only (c) is right; interpreting the world through current schemas is assimilation, not operations, and copying absent models is mental representation, not assimilation.
    • (4) (a)-(ii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(iii)Every pair is wrong; for example, interpreting the world with current schemas is assimilation, not mental representation.

  40. First make a small request; when the person complies, make another large request. This concept is based on which compliance technique?

    • 1Foot-in-the-door
    • 2Door-in-the-face
    • 3Lowball
    • 4That's-not-all
    ✓ Answer: (1) Foot-in-the-door
    Jonathan Freedman and Scott Fraser, foot-in-the-door
    The concept

    Why this oneThe foot-in-the-door technique starts with a small request; once the person agrees, a larger related request is more likely to be accepted.

    Why it worksAgreeing to the small request changes how people see themselves (self-perception: 'I am the helpful type') and creates pressure to stay consistent.

    Exam tipSmall then large = foot-in-the-door; refused large then smaller = door-in-the-face; good deal then added costs = lowball; extras added before a reply = that's-not-all.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Door-in-the-faceRobert Cialdini's technique of starting with a large request that is refused, then a smaller one that seems a concession and invites reciprocity.
    • (3) LowballWins agreement to an attractive deal, then raises the cost; people stick with their commitment even though the terms have worsened.
    • (4) That's-not-allJerry Burger's technique of sweetening the offer with a discount or extra item before the person has replied to the first offer.

  41. When acetylcholine links up on the post-synaptic side, it generates a depolarisation. What do you call it?

    • 1Inhibitory Post-synaptic Potential
    • 2Excitatory Post-synaptic Potential
    • 3End plate potential
    • 4End plate potential and Inhibitory Post-synaptic Potential
    ✓ Best answer: (3) End plate potential
    End plate potential at the neuromuscular junction
    The concept

    Why this oneWhen acetylcholine from a motor neuron binds nicotinic receptors on the muscle's motor end plate, the resulting depolarisation is called the end plate potential (EPP).

    How it worksACh opens ligand-gated channels that let sodium in; the EPP is large enough to trigger a muscle action potential almost every time, and acetylcholinesterase then breaks ACh down.

    Exam tipEPP = depolarisation at a muscle fibre; EPSP = small graded depolarisation at a neuron; IPSP = hyperpolarisation.

    Not certainAn end plate potential is the neuromuscular form of an excitatory postsynaptic potential, so (2) is defensible if the stem means any ACh synapse.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Inhibitory Post-synaptic PotentialA hyperpolarisation that makes the postsynaptic cell less likely to fire, so it cannot be the depolarisation described.
    • (2) Excitatory Post-synaptic PotentialThe general name for a small graded depolarisation at a neuron-to-neuron synapse; at the muscle, the depolarisation ACh produces is specifically the end plate potential.
    • (4) End plate potential and Inhibitory Post-synaptic PotentialPairs a depolarisation with an IPSP, which is a hyperpolarisation; one event cannot be both.

  42. The interaction of sensory impulses indicates the complexity of stimulation and therefore the difficulties in predicting behaviour. Behaviour is seldom a function of only one stimulus; rather it is a function of many stimuli. The above explanation is based on which learning theory?

    • 1Guthrie's theory
    • 2Pavlov's theory
    • 3Hull's theory
    • 4Tolman's theory
    ✓ Answer: (3) Hull's theory
    Clark Hull, afferent neural interaction postulate
    The concept

    Why this oneThe passage is Clark Hull's postulate on the interaction of sensory impulses (afferent neural interaction): stimuli arriving together modify one another, so behaviour reflects a pattern of many stimuli.

    Hull's systemHull built a hypothetico-deductive theory from formal postulates; its central ideas are drive reduction and habit strength, with reaction potential = habit strength × drive.

    Exam tipHull = postulates and drive reduction; Edwin Guthrie = one-trial contiguity learning; Edward Tolman = cognitive maps and latent learning.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Guthrie's theoryContiguity theory: a stimulus pattern present during a movement will, on recurrence, produce that movement, learned in one trial; Edwin Guthrie set out no such postulate.
    • (2) Pavlov's theoryClassical conditioning pairs a conditioned stimulus with an unconditioned stimulus; Ivan Pavlov built no system of formal postulates about interacting sensory impulses.
    • (4) Tolman's theoryPurposive behaviourism: learning through expectancies and cognitive maps, as Edward Tolman argued, not through interacting sensory impulses.

  43. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Psychologist): (a) Carol Ryff; (b) Seligman; (c) Viktor Frankl; (d) Suler

    List-II (Concept): (i) Psychological well-being; (ii) Signature strength; (iii) Post-traumatic growth; (iv) Online disinhibition effect

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iii); (d)-(iv)
    • 2(a)-(ii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(iii)
    • 3(a)-(iii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(ii)
    • 4(a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(ii); (d)-(i)
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iii); (d)-(iv)
    Carol Ryff, Martin Seligman, Viktor Frankl, John Suler
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Carol Ryff = (i) psychological well-being; (b) Martin Seligman = (ii) signature strengths; (c) Viktor Frankl = (iii) post-traumatic growth; (d) John Suler = (iv) online disinhibition effect.

    Key ideasRyff's model has six dimensions of psychological well-being, including autonomy, purpose in life and self-acceptance; Seligman's signature strengths are a person's most characteristic character strengths.

    More ideasFrankl, a concentration camp survivor, held that finding meaning in suffering lets people grow from trauma; Suler's online disinhibition effect explains why people say things online they would not say face to face.

    Exam tipThe term post-traumatic growth was coined by Richard Tedeschi and Lawrence Calhoun; Frankl's logotherapy is its forerunner.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (a)-(ii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(iii)Pairs Carol Ryff with signature strengths, which is Martin Seligman's idea; Ryff is known for the six-dimension model of psychological well-being.
    • (3) (a)-(iii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(ii)Pairs Martin Seligman with psychological well-being (Ryff's model) and Viktor Frankl with online disinhibition, which is John Suler's idea.
    • (4) (a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(ii); (d)-(i)Reverses every pair; for example, Carol Ryff did not study online disinhibition, which was described by John Suler.

  44. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Concept): (a) Big-C creativity; (b) Little-C creativity; (c) Mini-C creativity; (d) Pro-C creativity

    List-II (Description): (i) Creativity of eminence; (ii) Creativity of everyday life recognised by others; (iii) Subjective and personal form of day-to-day creativity; (iv) Professional creativity which has not attained the status of eminence

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iii); (d)-(iv)
    • 2(a)-(ii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(iii)
    • 3(a)-(iii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)
    • 4(a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(ii); (d)-(i)
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iii); (d)-(iv)
    James Kaufman and Ronald Beghetto, Four C model of creativity
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Big-C = (i) creativity of eminence; (b) Little-c = (ii) everyday creativity recognised by others; (c) Mini-c = (iii) subjective, personal creativity; (d) Pro-c = (iv) professional creativity not yet eminent.

    How it worksJames Kaufman and Ronald Beghetto expanded the old Big-C and little-c split into a ladder: mini-c (personal insight while learning) → little-c (everyday) → Pro-c (expert) → Big-C (legendary).

    Exam tipBig-C examples are Einstein or Picasso; a student's idea that is new only to the student is mini-c.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (a)-(ii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(iii)Swaps Big-C with little-c and mini-c with Pro-c; Big-C is reserved for eminent, field-changing work.
    • (3) (a)-(iii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)Gives Big-C the subjective personal form, which is mini-c, and mini-c the eminent form, which is Big-C.
    • (4) (a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(ii); (d)-(i)Reverses every pair; for example, Big-C is eminence, while professional creativity short of eminence is Pro-c.

  45. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Schools of Psychology): (a) Psychoanalysis; (b) Behaviourism; (c) Structuralism; (d) Transpersonal Psychology

    List-II (Concept): (i) Elements of conscious experience; (ii) Self-actualization; (iii) Unconscious processes; (iv) S-R relations

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a)-(iii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(ii)
    • 2(a)-(iii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)
    • 3(a)-(i); (b)-(iv); (c)-(ii); (d)-(iii)
    • 4(a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iv); (d)-(iii)
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a)-(iii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(ii)
    Sigmund Freud, John Watson, Edward Titchener, Abraham Maslow
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Psychoanalysis = (iii) unconscious processes; (b) Behaviourism = (iv) S-R relations; (c) Structuralism = (i) elements of conscious experience; (d) Transpersonal psychology = (ii) self-actualisation.

    Key ideasSigmund Freud's psychoanalysis stresses unconscious motives; John B. Watson's behaviourism studies observable stimulus-response links; Edward Titchener's structuralism used introspection to find the elements of consciousness.

    TranspersonalAbraham Maslow, a founder of transpersonal psychology (the 'fourth force'), extended self-actualisation to peak experiences and self-transcendence.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a)-(iii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(ii)Swaps behaviourism and structuralism: Watson rejected consciousness and studied S-R links, while Titchener analysed the elements of conscious experience.
    • (3) (a)-(i); (b)-(iv); (c)-(ii); (d)-(iii)Gets only behaviourism right; it gives psychoanalysis Titchener's elements of consciousness and transpersonal psychology Freud's unconscious processes.
    • (4) (a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iv); (d)-(iii)Every pair is wrong; for example, behaviourism concerns S-R relations, not self-actualisation, which belongs to humanistic and transpersonal psychology.

  46. Multiple sclerosis is:

    • (a) a demyelinating disorder
    • (b) an autoimmune disease
    • (c) always accompanied by amnesia and cognitive disorder
    • (d) known to lead to jerky movements

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a) and (b) only
    • 2(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 3(a), (b) and (d) only
    • 4(a) and (d) only
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a), (b) and (d) only
    Multiple sclerosis, autoimmune demyelinating disease
    The concept

    Why this oneMultiple sclerosis is an autoimmune disease in which the immune system attacks CNS myelin, leaving hardened scars (scleroses); damage to motor and cerebellar pathways causes tremor, ataxia and jerky movements.

    Why not (c)Memory, attention and processing-speed problems affect many patients but not all, and severe amnesia is uncommon, so 'always' makes (c) false.

    Exam tipJean-Martin Charcot's classic MS triad is nystagmus, intention tremor and scanning speech; symptoms often come and go in relapses and remissions.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) and (b) onlyTrue but incomplete: MS damage to motor and cerebellar pathways commonly produces tremor and ataxia, so (d) holds as well.
    • (2) (a), (b) and (c) onlyIncludes (c), which is false: cognitive difficulties occur in many people with MS, but not always, and amnesia is not a defining feature.
    • (4) (a) and (d) onlyLeaves out (b): MS is an autoimmune disease in which immune cells attack the myelin of the central nervous system.

  47. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): If children watch programmes with violent and aggressive content, they themselves take recourse to violent and aggressive behaviour.

    Reason (R): Children learn aggressive behaviour from media through observational learning, where the observed behaviour need not be positively reinforced.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    Albert Bandura, observational learning (Bobo doll studies)
    The concept

    AssertionTrue as a general finding: children who watch violent content tend to behave more aggressively, as Albert Bandura's Bobo doll studies and later media-violence research show.

    ReasonTrue, and it explains A: through observational learning children acquire behaviour simply by watching a model, with no reward needed for the child or the model.

    Exam tipBandura separates learning from performance: seeing the model rewarded or punished affects whether the child performs the act, not whether it is learned.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)(R) does explain (A): imitation through observational learning is the mechanism by which watched aggression becomes the child's own aggression.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is false(R) is true: Bandura showed children learn modelled aggression without any reinforcement for themselves or the model.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is true(A) is supported: children exposed to aggressive models, live or on screen, imitate the aggression, as the Bobo doll experiments showed.

  48. Who proved the observation that cyclists seem to ride faster when they race against other cyclists than when they race alone?

    • 1Zajonc
    • 2Allport
    • 3Baron
    • 4Norman Triplett
    ✓ Answer: (4) Norman Triplett
    Norman Triplett (1898), first social psychology experiment
    The concept

    Why this oneNorman Triplett noticed from cycling records that riders went faster when racing others than alone, and confirmed it in a lab study where children wound fishing reels faster in pairs.

    What it showsThis was the first demonstration of social facilitation: the presence of others, as co-actors or audience, improves performance on simple, well-learned tasks.

    Exam tipTriplett = first experiment in social psychology; Floyd Allport named social facilitation; Robert Zajonc explained it by arousal.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) ZajoncLater explained social facilitation by drive arousal: others' presence strengthens dominant responses, helping easy tasks and hurting hard ones.
    • (2) AllportNamed social facilitation and studied co-acting groups about two decades later; Floyd Allport did not make the cyclist observation.
    • (3) BaronProposed the distraction-conflict theory of social facilitation much later; Robert Baron did not make the original cyclist observation.

  49. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): The method of calculating IQ in terms of MA/CA x 100 as such does not work with adults.

    Reason (R): Intelligence does not increase linearly with age past childhood.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    William Stern's ratio IQ versus David Wechsler's deviation IQ
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. Ratio IQ (MA/CA × 100) breaks down for adults: a 40-year-old with a mental age of 20 would score 50 despite normal adult ability.

    ReasonTrue, and it explains A: mental age stops rising steadily in the mid to late teens while chronological age keeps increasing, so the ratio falls artificially.

    Exam tipDavid Wechsler solved this with the deviation IQ, comparing a person with same-age peers (mean 100, SD 15).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)(R) is the direct reason (A) holds: the formula assumes mental age rises in step with chronological age, which fails after childhood.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is false(R) is true: gains in mental age slow and level off after adolescence, so intelligence does not keep rising linearly with age.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is true(A) is true: dividing a levelled-off mental age by an ever-rising chronological age gives adults misleadingly low IQs.

  50. A borrowed his father's car during a college vacation and smashed it after a party A attended. Expected reactions of his father run through A's mind and generate several possibilities. It is an example of which heuristic?

    • 1Availability heuristic
    • 2Representativeness heuristic
    • 3Conjunction error
    • 4Simulation heuristic
    ✓ Answer: (4) Simulation heuristic
    Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky, simulation heuristic
    The concept

    Why this oneA mentally runs through possible scenarios of how his father might react; judging outcomes by how easily they can be mentally simulated is the simulation heuristic.

    What it isDaniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky proposed that people build mental 'what if' scenarios; this underlies counterfactual thinking, such as stronger regret when a better outcome is easy to imagine.

    Exam tipAvailability = how easily examples come to mind from memory; simulation = how easily a scenario can be constructed.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Availability heuristicJudges frequency or probability by how easily past examples come to mind, not by imagining possible future scenarios.
    • (2) Representativeness heuristicJudges probability by how closely something resembles a typical case or prototype, often ignoring base rates.
    • (3) Conjunction errorJudging two events together as more likely than one alone, as in the Linda problem; it is a reasoning error, not a heuristic.

  51. The cognitive approach to prejudice arises from which theory, that an individual's self-concept derives partly from membership in an in-group?

    • 1Realistic group conflict theory
    • 2Scapegoat theory
    • 3Frustration-aggression theory
    • 4Social identity theory
    ✓ Answer: (4) Social identity theory
    Henri Tajfel and John Turner, social identity theory (1979)
    The concept

    Why this oneSocial identity theory says part of our self-concept comes from the groups we belong to. To feel good about ourselves we favour our in-group and look down on out-groups, which is the cognitive root of prejudice.

    How it worksThree steps: social categorisation (sorting people into groups), social identification (taking the group into the self) and social comparison (rating our group above others).

    Exam tipSelf-esteem from group membership = social identity; competition for scarce resources = realistic conflict; displaced frustration = scapegoat.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Realistic group conflict theoryMuzafer Sherif's theory: prejudice grows from competition for scarce resources between groups, as in the Robbers Cave study, not from the self-concept.
    • (2) Scapegoat theoryScapegoating means frustrated people displace hostility onto weaker, blameless groups; it is a motivational account of prejudice, not a self-concept one.
    • (3) Frustration-aggression theoryJohn Dollard and colleagues (1939) linked blocked goals to aggression; it explains hostility, not prejudice rooted in group-based identity.

  52. Choose the correct answer for the leadership style: low task, low relationship style.

    • 1Participating style
    • 2Selling style
    • 3Delegating style
    • 4Telling style
    ✓ Answer: (3) Delegating style
    Paul Hersey and Ken Blanchard, situational leadership model
    The concept

    Why this oneIn the situational leadership model, a leader who gives little direction (low task) and little support (low relationship) is delegating: the followers run the job themselves.

    How it worksThe right style depends on follower readiness (maturity). Low readiness needs direction; high readiness needs little of either.

    Correct pairsS1 Telling = high task, low relationship; S2 Selling = high task, high relationship; S3 Participating = low task, high relationship; S4 Delegating = low task, low relationship.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Participating styleThis is S3: low task but high relationship; the leader shares decisions and supports followers who are able but unsure.
    • (2) Selling styleThis is S2: high task and high relationship; the leader still directs but explains decisions and coaches.
    • (4) Telling styleThis is S1: high task, low relationship; the leader gives clear instructions and supervises closely, for followers with low readiness.

  53. Which of the following is a degenerative disease accompanied by amnesia, caused by frontotemporal lobular degeneration, and depression?

    • 1Alzheimer's disease
    • 2Parkinson's disease
    • 3Pick's disease
    • 4Huntington's disease
    ✓ Answer: (3) Pick's disease
    Arnold Pick; frontotemporal lobar degeneration
    The concept

    Why this onePick's disease is a form of frontotemporal lobar degeneration: the frontal and temporal lobes shrink and neurons fill with Pick bodies (tau protein).

    What it looks likeOnset is usually in middle age. Personality change, disinhibition, apathy or depression-like withdrawal and language loss come first; memory loss follows.

    Exam tipFrontotemporal atrophy = Pick's; plaques and tangles with early memory loss = Alzheimer's; caudate loss with chorea = Huntington's; substantia nigra loss with tremor = Parkinson's.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Alzheimer's diseaseAlzheimer's begins with memory loss from amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles, starting in the hippocampus and temporoparietal cortex, not focal frontotemporal lobar degeneration.
    • (2) Parkinson's diseaseParkinson's is loss of dopamine neurons in the substantia nigra, causing resting tremor, rigidity and slow movement.
    • (4) Huntington's diseaseHuntington's is an inherited disorder with caudate and putamen loss, causing chorea, mood change and dementia.

  54. Positive emotions are expressed as a result of activation of which of the following?

    • 1Frontal areas of the right hemisphere
    • 2Pre-frontal areas of the right hemisphere
    • 3Frontal areas of the left hemisphere
    • 4Pre-frontal areas of the left hemisphere
    ✓ Best answer: (4) Pre-frontal areas of the left hemisphere
    Richard Davidson, frontal asymmetry (approach-withdrawal) model
    The concept

    Why this oneEEG and imaging studies show more activity in the left prefrontal cortex during positive, approach-related emotions such as joy and interest.

    How it worksIn Richard Davidson's approach-withdrawal model, the left prefrontal area drives moving towards rewards, while the right prefrontal area drives withdrawal, as in fear, disgust and sadness.

    Exam tipLeft prefrontal = positive mood and approach; right prefrontal = negative mood and withdrawal. People with more resting left-sided activity report more cheerful moods.

    Not certainSome introductory texts simply say the left frontal lobe, so (3) is the runner-up; Richard Davidson's work places the activity in the left prefrontal cortex.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Frontal areas of the right hemisphereThe right frontal areas are linked with negative, withdrawal-related emotions such as fear and sadness, not positive ones.
    • (2) Pre-frontal areas of the right hemisphereIn Davidson's model the right prefrontal cortex serves withdrawal emotions; greater right activity goes with negative affect.
    • (3) Frontal areas of the left hemisphereCorrect hemisphere but too broad: the asymmetry is located in the prefrontal part of the left frontal lobe, not the frontal lobe as a whole.

  55. Who has proposed a three-componential theory of wisdom explaining cognitive, affective and reflective wisdom?

    • 1Sternberg
    • 2Baltes
    • 3Chandler
    • 4Ardelt
    ✓ Answer: (4) Ardelt
    Monika Ardelt, three-dimensional model of wisdom
    The concept

    Why this oneMonika Ardelt defined wisdom as a blend of three personality qualities, cognitive, reflective and affective, measured by her Three-Dimensional Wisdom Scale.

    The three partsCognitive: a wish to know the truth and understand life deeply. Reflective: seeing events from many angles, including oneself, without blame. Affective: sympathy and compassion for others.

    Exam tipArdelt = three dimensions in the person; Baltes = Berlin wisdom paradigm (expert life knowledge); Sternberg = balance theory.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) SternbergRobert Sternberg proposed the balance theory of wisdom: using knowledge to balance intrapersonal, interpersonal and extrapersonal interests for the common good.
    • (2) BaltesPaul Baltes led the Berlin wisdom paradigm: wisdom as expert knowledge in the fundamental pragmatics of life, judged by five criteria.
    • (3) ChandlerMichael Chandler wrote about wisdom as a form of understanding beyond technical skill; the cognitive, reflective and affective model with its scale is Ardelt's.

  56. A 12-year-old boy started showing a gradual onset of jerky, uncontrolled movements in the limbs with clumsiness, frequent falling, slurred speech and drooling, caused by HTT mutation. What disorder is he suffering from?

    • 1Ataxia
    • 2Huntington's
    • 3Dystonia
    • 4Essential tremors
    ✓ Answer: (2) Huntington's
    Juvenile Huntington's disease, HTT gene on chromosome 4
    The concept

    Why this oneThe HTT gene makes the protein huntingtin. An expanded CAG repeat in it causes Huntington's disease; onset before 20 is called juvenile Huntington's.

    What it looks likeJerky, uncontrolled movements (chorea), clumsiness, falls, slurred speech and drooling, with later decline in thinking and mood. Juvenile cases often show more rigidity and seizures.

    How it worksIt is autosomal dominant: one faulty copy from either parent is enough. Neurons in the caudate and putamen die first.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) AtaxiaAtaxia is loss of coordination, usually from cerebellar damage; it is a symptom with many causes, not the disorder an HTT mutation produces.
    • (3) DystoniaDystonia is sustained, involuntary muscle contraction causing twisting postures; its genetic forms involve other genes such as DYT1, not HTT.
    • (4) Essential tremorsEssential tremor is a common, often familial action tremor of the hands and head, without chorea or an HTT mutation.

  57. In Cattell's theory, the Dynamic Lattice shows the relationship among which of the following?

    • 1Ergs, sentiments and ability traits
    • 2Ergs, attitudes and temperamental traits
    • 3Sentiments and ergs
    • 4Ergs, sentiments and attitudes
    ✓ Answer: (4) Ergs, sentiments and attitudes
    Raymond Cattell, dynamic lattice and subsidiation
    The concept

    Why this oneRaymond Cattell's dynamic lattice is a diagram of how attitudes serve sentiments, and sentiments in turn serve inborn ergs.

    What they areErgs are innate drives (hunger, sex, curiosity). Sentiments are learned clusters of attitudes around objects such as career, home or self. Attitudes are specific interests in a course of action.

    How it worksThe chain is called subsidiation: an attitude is subsidiary to a sentiment, which is subsidiary to ergs. Liking a class serves a career sentiment, which serves security and self-assertion ergs.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Ergs, sentiments and ability traitsAbility traits (how well a person copes) are a separate class of Cattell's traits; the lattice links only dynamic traits, so attitudes are missing.
    • (2) Ergs, attitudes and temperamental traitsTemperament traits (style of behaviour) are not part of the lattice, and sentiments, the link between ergs and attitudes, are left out.
    • (3) Sentiments and ergsIncomplete: the lattice starts from specific attitudes, which feed into sentiments and then into ergs.

  58. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Concept): (a) Savouring; (b) Hedonic adaptation; (c) Type-A behaviour; (d) Undoing hypothesis

    List-II (Description): (i) Applying conscious awareness to enjoyment experiences; (ii) Happiness due to circumstances tends to be temporary; (iii) Hurry syndrome; (iv) Positive emotions lead to a quick recovery from detrimental effects

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iii); (d)-(iv)
    • 2(a)-(ii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(iii)
    • 3(a)-(iii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(ii)
    • 4(a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(ii); (d)-(i)
    ✓ Answer: (1) (a)-(i); (b)-(ii); (c)-(iii); (d)-(iv)
    Bryant, Fredrickson, Friedman and Rosenman; positive and health psychology
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Savouring = (i) applying conscious awareness to enjoyment; (b) Hedonic adaptation = (ii) happiness from circumstances is temporary; (c) Type-A behaviour = (iii) hurry syndrome; (d) Undoing hypothesis = (iv) positive emotions speed recovery.

    First twoSavouring (Fred Bryant) is deliberately noticing and prolonging good experiences. Hedonic adaptation is the hedonic treadmill: after a raise or a loss we drift back to our usual happiness level.

    Last twoType A (Meyer Friedman and Ray Rosenman) is time urgency, competitiveness and hostility. Barbara Fredrickson's undoing hypothesis says positive emotions undo the cardiovascular after-effects of negative ones.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) (a)-(ii); (b)-(iv); (c)-(i); (d)-(iii)Every pair is shifted: savouring is not temporary happiness, Type A is not conscious enjoyment, and the undoing hypothesis is not hurry syndrome.
    • (3) (a)-(iii); (b)-(i); (c)-(iv); (d)-(ii)Savouring is not hurry syndrome and Type A is not about recovery; hurry syndrome is the hallmark of Type A behaviour.
    • (4) (a)-(iv); (b)-(iii); (c)-(ii); (d)-(i)Reverses the list: quick recovery from negative states is the undoing hypothesis, and conscious enjoyment is savouring.

  59. The major function of the parathyroid is to maintain the level of:

    • 1calcium in blood
    • 2proteins in blood
    • 3vitamin D in blood
    • 4gluco-corticoids in blood
    ✓ Answer: (1) calcium in blood
    Parathyroid hormone (parathormone), calcium balance
    The concept

    Why this oneThe four parathyroid glands behind the thyroid release parathyroid hormone (PTH), which raises blood calcium when it falls.

    How it worksPTH frees calcium from bone, makes the kidneys keep calcium, and activates vitamin D so the gut absorbs more. Calcitonin from the thyroid works the other way.

    Why it mattersNerves and muscles need steady calcium. Too little causes tetany (muscle spasms); too much causes fatigue, depression and confusion.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) proteins in bloodBlood proteins such as albumin are made by the liver; the parathyroid does not regulate them.
    • (3) vitamin D in bloodPTH does switch on active vitamin D in the kidney, but only as a means of raising calcium; calcium is the level it maintains.
    • (4) gluco-corticoids in bloodGlucocorticoids such as cortisol come from the adrenal cortex, under the control of ACTH from the pituitary.

  60. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): A 3- to 4-year-old will readily hand over a blanket to another individual who is rubbing hands and shivering a lot.

    Reason (R): In sensitive face-to-face communication, infants connect emotionally with their caregivers; these experiences are believed to be the foundation for empathy.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    Early development of empathy and prosocial behaviour
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. By 2 to 4 years children show empathy and act on it: they comfort, share and hand things to someone who looks upset or cold. This is prosocial behaviour.

    ReasonTrue. Warm face-to-face exchanges with caregivers teach babies to read and share another person's feelings, which is thought to lay the base for empathy.

    LinkR explains A: the emotional attunement built in infancy grows into empathy, and empathy is what moves the preschooler to help the shivering person.

    Not certainR describes the infant roots of empathy while A is a preschool act, so some would read R as true but not the explanation, which is (2).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)R does more than sit beside A: it gives the developmental source of the empathy that drives the helping act in A.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseR is true: sensitive early caregiving is widely seen as the root of empathy in developmental psychology.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is trueA is true: preschoolers readily share and comfort when they see clear signs of distress such as shivering.

  61. Repeated presentation of negative news in mass media may lead to an increase in the occurrence of such negative events because of:

    • 1Werther effect
    • 2Contagion effect
    • 3Frequency effect
    • 4Crespi effect
    ✓ Best answer: (2) Contagion effect
    Media contagion (copycat) effect
    The concept

    Why this oneThe contagion effect is the spread of a behaviour through exposure: heavy coverage of violence, riots, suicides or mass shootings is followed by a rise in similar acts.

    How it worksCoverage offers models to imitate and makes the act seem common, in line with Albert Bandura's social learning and Robert Cialdini's social proof.

    Exam tipContagion = any negative event copied after media exposure; Werther effect = copycat suicide only; Papageno effect = coping stories that reduce suicide.

    Not certainIf the item means copycat suicides after media reports, (1) Werther effect also fits, since it is the suicide form of the same contagion.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Werther effectDavid Phillips' term for the specific rise in suicides after publicised suicides; the stem speaks of negative events in general.
    • (3) Frequency effectA learning and memory idea: items met more often are recognised and recalled more easily; it does not explain copied events.
    • (4) Crespi effectLeo Crespi's finding that performance shifts sharply when the size of a reward is changed (contrast effect in rats), not media imitation.

  62. Marked impairments in establishing social interactions and living in their own world, repetitive behaviour patterns, and non-existent or poor language skills are three major characteristics of which disorder?

    • (a) Autism
    • (b) Obsessive-compulsive disorder
    • (c) Catatonic schizophrenia
    • (d) Apraxia

    Select the correct option:

    • 1Only (a)
    • 2Only (b) and (c)
    • 3Only (d)
    • 4Only (a) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (1) Only (a)
    Leo Kanner, early infantile autism (1943)
    The concept

    Why this onePoor social relating (living in their own world), repetitive, restricted behaviour and absent or delayed language are the classic triad of autism.

    What it isLeo Kanner described it in 1943. DSM-5 calls it autism spectrum disorder, with two domains: social communication deficits and restricted, repetitive behaviour.

    Statement checkOnly (a) has all three features from early childhood. OCD and catatonia share some repetitive behaviour, and apraxia affects movement planning, but none shows this developmental picture.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Only (b) and (c)OCD (b) has obsessions and compulsions with normal language; catatonic schizophrenia (c) shows stupor or posturing within psychosis, not a developmental triad.
    • (3) Only (d)Apraxia (d) is loss of skilled, purposeful movement despite intact strength, usually after brain damage; it is not a social or language disorder.
    • (4) Only (a) and (d)Autism (a) fits, but apraxia (d) is a motor planning disorder, so adding it makes the option wrong.

  63. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Psychologist): (a) Kelley; (b) Eysenck; (c) Cattell; (d) Hull

    List-II (Concept): (i) Constructive alternativism; (ii) Hedonic tone; (iii) Hypothetico-deductive spiral; (iv) Response hierarchies

    Select the correct option:

    • 1a-i; b-iii; c-ii; d-iv
    • 2a-ii; b-iv; c-i; d-iii
    • 3a-i; b-ii; c-iii; d-iv
    • 4a-ii; b-i; c-iv; d-iii
    ✓ Answer: (3) a-i; b-ii; c-iii; d-iv
    Kelly, Eysenck, Cattell and Hull; personality and learning theories
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) George Kelly = (i) constructive alternativism; (b) Hans Eysenck = (ii) hedonic tone; (c) Raymond Cattell = (iii) hypothetico-deductive spiral; (d) Clark Hull = (iv) response hierarchies.

    Kelly and EysenckConstructive alternativism: any event can be construed in many ways. Eysenck tied hedonic tone (how pleasant stimulation feels) to arousal: introverts reach their best level with less stimulation.

    Cattell and HullCattell's inductive-hypothetico-deductive spiral cycles between data, hypotheses and testing. Hull's habit-family hierarchy ranks responses by habit strength, so the strongest appears first.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) a-i; b-iii; c-ii; d-ivSwaps (b) and (c): the hypothetico-deductive spiral is Cattell's research method, and hedonic tone belongs to Eysenck's arousal theory.
    • (2) a-ii; b-iv; c-i; d-iiiKelly's idea is constructive alternativism, not hedonic tone; response hierarchies are Hull's and the spiral is Cattell's.
    • (4) a-ii; b-i; c-iv; d-iiiKelly's term is constructive alternativism and Eysenck's is hedonic tone; response hierarchies belong to Hull, not Cattell.

  64. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): Intelligent teachers select information that is relevant for their pedagogical purpose.

    Reason (R): Selective combination involves determining which elements in a set of information are relevant for a given purpose.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (3) (A) is true but (R) is false
    Robert Sternberg and Janet Davidson, three processes of insight
    The concept

    Why this oneA is true, but R gives the wrong label. Picking out the relevant information is selective encoding, not selective combination.

    The three processesRobert Sternberg's account: selective encoding = sifting relevant from irrelevant facts; selective combination = putting the chosen facts together into a whole; selective comparison = relating new information to old knowledge.

    Exam tipEncode = pick, combine = join, compare = link to the past. They are the knowledge-acquisition components of his triarchic theory.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)R is false as written, so it cannot explain A; the process in A is selective encoding.
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)R is not true: selective combination means joining relevant facts into a whole, not choosing which facts are relevant.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is trueA is true: choosing information that suits the teaching goal is a mark of skilled, intelligent teaching.

  65. Who gave the statement that the nature and frequency of human aggressive behaviour varies tremendously across human societies and is likely to occur in some more than others?

    • 1Berkowitz
    • 2Freud
    • 3Dollard and Miller
    • 4Lorenz
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Berkowitz
    Leonard Berkowitz; social psychologists' critique of instinct theories
    The concept

    Why this oneSocial psychologists such as Leonard Berkowitz argued that if aggression were an instinct it would look much the same everywhere. In fact its rate and form vary hugely between societies, which points to learning and situations.

    How it worksBerkowitz's cognitive neoassociation model: unpleasant events create negative feeling that primes aggressive thoughts, and cues in the setting, such as weapons, make aggression more likely.

    Exam tipInstinct views: Sigmund Freud (Thanatos), Konrad Lorenz (fighting instinct). Cross-cultural variation is the classic argument against them.

    Not certainThe line is the standard social-psychological critique of instinct theories rather than a famous quote; Leonard Berkowitz is the only option who rejected instinct views, with (3) a weak runner-up.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) FreudSigmund Freud saw aggression as an inborn death instinct (Thanatos) turned outward, so he expected it in all humans, not mainly in some societies.
    • (3) Dollard and MillerJohn Dollard, Neal Miller and colleagues said frustration always leads to aggression; they stressed a universal frustration link, not variation across societies.
    • (4) LorenzKonrad Lorenz, an ethologist, held that aggression is an innate fighting instinct that builds up and must be released, the view this statement argues against.

  66. Which of the following is NOT true?

    • 1At the time of birth, the brain is not fully lateralised; rather it is plastic
    • 2Though the left hemisphere is broadly associated with language development, yet if it is injured during the first few years, other regions take over language functions to attain normal language competence
    • 3Damage to the left frontal lobe areas usually yields language comprehension problems.
    • 4Broca's area supports grammatical processing and language production
    ✓ Answer: (3) Damage to the left frontal lobe areas usually yields language comprehension problems.
    Broca's and Wernicke's areas; lateralisation and plasticity
    The concept

    Why this oneIt is false because left frontal damage (Broca's area) mainly harms speech production. Comprehension problems come from left temporal damage (Wernicke's area).

    Broca vs WernickeBroca's aphasia: slow, effortful, telegraphic speech with fair understanding. Wernicke's aphasia: fluent but meaningless speech with poor understanding.

    PlasticityAt birth the hemispheres have begun to specialise but are not fixed. Children with early left-hemisphere injury usually reach near-normal language because other areas take over, showing plasticity.

    Why the other options are not the answer
    • (1) At the time of birth, the brain is not fully lateralised; rather it is plasticTrue: the newborn brain is only partly lateralised and highly plastic, which is why early injuries are recovered from better than later ones.
    • (2) Though the left hemisphere is broadly associated with language development, yet if it is injured during the first few years, other regions take over language functions to attain normal language competenceTrue: after early left-hemisphere damage, the right hemisphere or other left areas take over language, and children usually reach near-normal competence.
    • (4) Broca's area supports grammatical processing and language productionTrue: Broca's area in the left frontal lobe supports speech production and grammar, so damage gives halting, telegraphic speech.

  67. Fill in the blanks by choosing the right sequence. In the upside-down and reversed-image projection process on ______, the ______ bends the rays entering the eye and the light rays from the top of an object are projected onto ______ at the bottom of ______.

    Select the correct option:

    • 1receptors, lens, retina, retina
    • 2lens, retina, receptors, retina
    • 3retina, lens, receptors, retina
    • 4retina, lens, retina, receptors
    ✓ Answer: (3) retina, lens, receptors, retina
    Image formation on the retina
    The concept

    Correct orderRetina, lens, receptors, retina: the image is projected upside down and reversed on the retina; the lens bends the light; rays from the top of an object land on receptors at the bottom of the retina.

    How it worksThe cornea and lens are curved, so light rays cross as they pass through. Top becomes bottom and left becomes right on the retina.

    Why we see uprightThe brain interprets the inverted retinal image, so the world looks the right way up.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) receptors, lens, retina, retinaThe image is projected on the retina, not on receptors, and the last two blanks give 'retina at the bottom of the retina', which makes no sense.
    • (2) lens, retina, receptors, retinaThe lens bends light and the retina receives it; this order puts the lens where the image lands and the retina where the bending happens.
    • (4) retina, lens, retina, receptorsThe last two blanks are reversed: rays land on receptors at the bottom of the retina, not on the retina at the bottom of the receptors.

  68. Which of the following sequences of decision-making perspectives of prosocial behaviour is correct?

    • 1Perceived need - taking personal responsibility - weighing the cost and benefits - deciding how to help - help is given
    • 2Taking personal responsibility - deciding how to help - help is given - weighing the cost and benefits - perceived need
    • 3Weighing the cost and benefits - perceived need - deciding how to help - help is given - taking personal responsibility
    • 4Perceived need - weighing the cost and benefits - deciding how to help - taking personal responsibility - help is given
    ✓ Answer: (1) Perceived need - taking personal responsibility - weighing the cost and benefits - deciding how to help - help is given
    Bibb Latané and John Darley's decision model of helping
    The concept

    Correct orderPerceive a need → take personal responsibility → weigh costs and benefits → decide how to help → give help.

    How it worksHelp can fail at any step. If people miss the need, think someone else will act (diffusion of responsibility), or find the cost too high, no help is given.

    Exam tipIt builds on Bibb Latané and John Darley's bystander research: notice, interpret as an emergency, assume responsibility, know how to help, then act.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Taking personal responsibility - deciding how to help - help is given - weighing the cost and benefits - perceived needStarts with responsibility and ends with perceived need; a person cannot take responsibility for a need they have not yet noticed.
    • (3) Weighing the cost and benefits - perceived need - deciding how to help - help is given - taking personal responsibilityPuts cost-benefit weighing first and responsibility last; responsibility comes right after the need is perceived, and help comes last.
    • (4) Perceived need - weighing the cost and benefits - deciding how to help - taking personal responsibility - help is givenPlaces responsibility after deciding how to help; first one accepts responsibility, then weighs costs and chooses the form of help.

  69. When people are pressurized to admit guilt but privately continue to believe in their own innocence, this is the correct answer for the above statement:

    • 1Voluntary false confession
    • 2Coerced-compliant false confession
    • 3Coerced-internalized false confession
    • 4Character evidence
    ✓ Answer: (2) Coerced-compliant false confession
    Saul Kassin and Lawrence Wrightsman, types of false confession
    The concept

    Why this oneIn a coerced-compliant false confession the suspect gives in to pressure (to end questioning, avoid threats or gain a promise) but privately still knows they are innocent.

    The three typesVoluntary: confessing with no police pressure, for attention or to protect someone. Coerced-compliant: public compliance, private belief in innocence. Coerced-internalised: the suspect comes to believe they did it.

    Exam tipCompliance = says it, does not believe it. Internalisation = says it and believes it.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Voluntary false confessionA voluntary false confession is given without police pressure, for example to seek attention or protect the real offender.
    • (3) Coerced-internalized false confessionHere the suspect, worn down by tactics such as false evidence, comes to believe they are guilty; in the stem belief in innocence remains.
    • (4) Character evidenceCharacter evidence is testimony about a person's general traits or reputation, used to suggest how they acted; it is not a type of confession.

  70. Torrance used the term 'creatively handicapped' for whom?

    • 1Students very high in creativity but low in academic achievement
    • 2Students high in intelligence but low in creativity
    • 3Students average in both intelligence and creativity
    • 4Students with low levels of both intelligence and creativity
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Students very high in creativity but low in academic achievement
    E. Paul Torrance, studies of creative children
    The concept

    Why this oneE. Paul Torrance noted that highly creative children often do not fit school routines, so they underachieve and are seen as difficult. He called them creatively handicapped: their creativity becomes a handicap in a conformist classroom.

    What it meansSchools reward memory and convergent thinking, while these children excel at divergent thinking. Without support they may hide or give up their creativity.

    Exam tipTorrance also built the Torrance Tests of Creative Thinking (TTCT), scored for fluency, flexibility, originality and elaboration.

    Not certainThis is a niche Torrance term; the runner-up is (2), the high-IQ, low-creativity group, which some might read as lacking in creativity.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Students high in intelligence but low in creativityThe bright, conforming student who does well in school; Jacob Getzels and Philip Jackson contrasted this group with high-creative students.
    • (3) Students average in both intelligence and creativityStudents average on both measures fit ordinary school demands and are not singled out by any special label.
    • (4) Students with low levels of both intelligence and creativityStudents struggling on both counts; the term described creativity causing school problems, not a lack of creativity.

  71. Hold a pencil at arm's length and look at it first with one eye then with the other. There is a little difference between the two views relative to the background. Now bring the pencil just six inches away from your face and try the same thing. This time you will perceive a great difference between the two views. Which one is the correct answer for the above example?

    • 1Monocular cue
    • 2Binocular cue
    • 3Top-down processing
    • 4Bottom-up processing
    ✓ Answer: (2) Binocular cue
    Binocular (retinal) disparity
    The concept

    Why this oneEach eye sees the pencil from a slightly different angle. This binocular disparity needs both eyes, so it is a binocular cue to depth.

    How it worksThe closer the object, the bigger the difference between the two retinal images. The brain reads a large disparity as near and a small one as far.

    Exam tipBinocular cues: retinal disparity and convergence. Monocular cues: linear perspective, interposition, texture gradient, relative size, motion parallax.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Monocular cueDepth cues such as interposition or linear perspective that work with one eye; this demonstration depends on comparing the views of two eyes.
    • (3) Top-down processingUsing knowledge and expectation to interpret input; the demonstration shows a raw sensory cue from two eyes.
    • (4) Bottom-up processingBuilding perception up from sensory data; it is a general processing mode, not the specific depth cue shown here.

  72. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): Mental stress may cause cardiac ischemia.

    Reason (R): Stress unfavorably affects the balance between cardiac demand and supply.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Answer: (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    Mental stress-induced myocardial ischaemia
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. Myocardial ischaemia means the heart muscle gets too little blood and oxygen. Acute mental stress can trigger it, especially in people with coronary artery disease.

    ReasonTrue. Stress hormones raise heart rate and blood pressure, which increases the heart's oxygen demand, while coronary vessels may constrict and cut supply.

    LinkR explains A: ischaemia is exactly a mismatch in which demand exceeds supply.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)R is the mechanism behind A, not a separate fact: stress upsets demand and supply, and that mismatch is ischaemia.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseR is true: stress raises demand through heart rate and blood pressure and can reduce supply through coronary constriction.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is trueA is true: mental stress-induced ischaemia is well documented in coronary patients, often without chest pain.

  73. Which attention is shifted without moving the eyes, commonly referred to as seeing something out of the corner of one's eye?

    • 1Covert attention
    • 2Overt attention
    • 3Divided attention
    • 4Span of attention
    ✓ Answer: (1) Covert attention
    Michael Posner, covert orienting of attention
    The concept

    Why this oneCovert attention shifts focus to a location without moving the eyes, as when you notice something out of the corner of your eye.

    How it worksIn Michael Posner's cueing task, people respond faster to targets at a cued spot even with their eyes fixed on the centre, showing attention moved covertly.

    Exam tipCovert = attention moves, eyes stay; overt = eyes and head turn to the target.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Overt attentionOvert attention means moving the eyes or head to look straight at the object of interest.
    • (3) Divided attentionDivided attention is splitting attention between two or more tasks at once, such as driving while talking.
    • (4) Span of attentionSpan of attention is the number of items a person can grasp in a single brief exposure.

  74. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Type of skin receptors): (a) Meissner's corpuscles; (b) Pacinian corpuscles; (c) Krause's end bulbs; (d) Free nerve endings

    List-II (Sensory function): (i) cold; (ii) pain; (iii) touch; (iv) pressure

    Select the correct option:

    • 1a-ii; b-iv; c-iii; d-i
    • 2a-iii; b-iv; c-i; d-ii
    • 3a-iv; b-i; c-ii; d-iii
    • 4a-iv; b-ii; c-iii; d-i
    ✓ Answer: (2) a-iii; b-iv; c-i; d-ii
    Cutaneous receptors, classic skin-senses account
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Meissner's corpuscles = (iii) touch; (b) Pacinian corpuscles = (iv) pressure; (c) Krause's end bulbs = (i) cold; (d) Free nerve endings = (ii) pain.

    Touch and pressureMeissner's corpuscles lie just under the skin surface and sense light touch. Pacinian corpuscles are deep, onion-like and respond to pressure and vibration.

    Cold and painClassic texts link Krause's end bulbs with cold and Ruffini endings with warmth. Free nerve endings carry pain and temperature.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) a-ii; b-iv; c-iii; d-iMeissner's corpuscles sense light touch, not pain; pain is carried by free nerve endings, and Krause's end bulbs are linked with cold.
    • (3) a-iv; b-i; c-ii; d-iiiPacinian corpuscles respond to pressure and vibration, not cold, and Meissner's corpuscles serve touch, not pressure.
    • (4) a-iv; b-ii; c-iii; d-iPacinian corpuscles do not carry pain, and free nerve endings are chiefly pain receptors, not cold ones; Meissner's serve touch.

  75. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): According to Freud, painful memories are the target of repression.

    Reason (R): Repression can be primal, i.e. innately part of the Id, or proper, i.e. an unacceptable derivative of original Id impulses.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Best answer: (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    Sigmund Freud, primal repression and repression proper
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. For Sigmund Freud, repression pushes threatening, painful memories, wishes and impulses out of consciousness into the unconscious.

    ReasonTrue. Freud split it into primal repression, which keeps id impulses from ever reaching awareness, and repression proper, which pushes out later ideas and memories linked to those impulses.

    LinkR lists two kinds of repression; it does not say why painful memories are its target. The reason is anxiety: repression protects the ego from it.

    Not certainIf R is read as saying why painful material is pushed out, some would choose (1), but R only classifies the kinds of repression.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)R only classifies repression into two kinds; the reason painful memories are repressed is that they arouse anxiety, which R does not mention.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseR is true: Freud did distinguish primal repression from repression proper, the after-pressure on derivatives of the original impulse.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is trueA is true: Freud saw repression as the basic defence that keeps painful and threatening memories out of awareness.

  76. The mechanistic view of motivation can be characterised by which of the following?

    • (a) S-O-R
    • (b) Direction is determined by S-R bonds
    • (c) Probability of habits depends on prior reinforcement contingencies
    • (d) Persistence of behaviour

    Select the correct option:

    • 1Only (a), (b) and (c)
    • 2Only (a), (b) and (d)
    • 3Only (b), (c) and (d)
    • 4(a), (b), (c) and (d)
    ✓ Best answer: (3) Only (b), (c) and (d)
    Clark Hull's drive theory, mechanistic view of motivation
    The concept

    Why this oneThe mechanistic view treats motivated behaviour as automatic: S-R bonds (habits) set its direction, past reinforcement sets how likely each habit is, and behaviour persists while the sustaining stimuli last.

    What it isIn Clark Hull's drive theory, a need creates drive, which energises whatever habits the situation calls up. No conscious intention or goal is assumed.

    Why not S-O-RS-O-R places the organism's inner states, expectancies and goals between stimulus and response. That mediating role is the mark of the cognitive view, as in Edward Tolman's purposive behaviourism.

    Exam tipMechanistic = automatic push (drive, habit, reinforcement); cognitive = conscious pull (expectancy, value, goals).

    Not certainClark Hull's theory has intervening organism variables and is sometimes written as S-O-R, which would give (1); but S-O-R usually marks the cognitive, purposive view.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Only (a), (b) and (c)Brings in (a), the S-O-R formula with mediating goals and expectancies, which belongs to the cognitive view, and drops (d), the mechanistic account of persistence.
    • (2) Only (a), (b) and (d)Brings in (a), the cognitive S-O-R formula, and drops (c), although habit strength built by past reinforcement is central to Hull's mechanistic theory.
    • (4) (a), (b), (c) and (d)Cannot be right because (a), with its mediating organism variables such as goals and expectancies, describes the cognitive view rather than the mechanistic one.

  77. Which of the following endeavours to revalue the unique qualities associated with womanhood and femininity?

    • 1Cultural feminism
    • 2Liberal feminism
    • 3Radical feminism
    • 4Socialist feminism
    ✓ Answer: (1) Cultural feminism
    Cultural (difference) feminism
    The concept

    Why this oneCultural feminism holds that women have distinct qualities, such as care, nurturance and cooperation, that society undervalues. It seeks to revalue these qualities rather than erase gender differences.

    How it differsIt is a form of difference feminism: the goal is not to make women like men but to honour a female way of being and relating.

    Exam tipLiberal = equal rights and access; radical = patriarchy is the root; socialist = capitalism plus patriarchy; cultural = celebrate femininity.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Liberal feminismLiberal feminism seeks equal legal rights, education and opportunity for women within existing institutions, stressing sameness with men, not special feminine qualities.
    • (3) Radical feminismRadical feminism sees patriarchy and male control of women's bodies and sexuality as the root of oppression and calls for overturning the gender system.
    • (4) Socialist feminismSocialist feminism links women's oppression to both capitalism and patriarchy, focusing on class and unpaid domestic labour.

  78. The Mayer-Salovey-Caruso Emotional Intelligence Test (MSCEIT) measures which of the following?

    • (a) Perceiving emotions
    • (b) Using emotions to facilitate thought
    • (c) Understanding emotions
    • (d) Self-motivation

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a) and (b) only
    • 2(c) and (d) only
    • 3(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 4(b), (c) and (d) only
    ✓ Answer: (3) (a), (b) and (c) only
    John Mayer, Peter Salovey, David Caruso: four-branch ability model
    The concept

    Why this oneThe MSCEIT is an ability test built on the four-branch model of John Mayer and Peter Salovey. Perceiving, using and understanding emotions are three of its branches; self-motivation is not.

    The four branches(1) Perceiving emotions in faces and pictures; (2) using emotions to facilitate thought; (3) understanding how emotions blend and change; (4) managing emotions in self and others.

    Exam tipSelf-motivation comes from Daniel Goleman's mixed model (self-awareness, self-regulation, motivation, empathy, social skills), not from the ability model.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) and (b) onlyLeaves out (c) understanding emotions, which is the third branch of the four-branch ability model that the MSCEIT measures.
    • (2) (c) and (d) onlyIncludes (d) self-motivation, which belongs to Daniel Goleman's mixed model, and leaves out perceiving emotions, the first MSCEIT branch.
    • (4) (b), (c) and (d) onlyIncludes (d) self-motivation from Goleman's model and drops (a) perceiving emotions, the most basic MSCEIT branch.

  79. The quotation, "People believe good things should happen to good people and bad things to bad people" represents:

    • 1Blank-out hypothesis
    • 2Just-world hypothesis
    • 3Karma hypothesis
    • 4Retribution hypothesis
    ✓ Answer: (2) Just-world hypothesis
    Melvin Lerner, belief in a just world
    The concept

    Why this oneThe just-world hypothesis of Melvin Lerner says people need to believe the world is fair, so good people are rewarded and bad people get what they deserve.

    Why it mattersTo protect this belief, people often blame the victim, for example assuming a victim of crime or illness must have done something to deserve it.

    Exam tipJust-world belief → victim derogation; it is a motivated belief that keeps the world feeling predictable and safe.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Blank-out hypothesisNot a recognised theory of fairness beliefs; the idea that good things happen to good people is Lerner's just-world belief.
    • (3) Karma hypothesisKarma is a religious and philosophical doctrine of moral cause and effect across lives, not the social psychology term for this belief.
    • (4) Retribution hypothesisRetribution means deserved punishment for wrongdoing; it is not the named social psychology hypothesis about believing the world is fair.

  80. An observer watches a video in which the speaker's lips form the sound 'ga-ga' while the sound track provides speech that is normally perceived as 'ba-ba'. The conjunction of these conflicting sources produces the perception of 'da-da', which integrates this information and results in which illusion?

    • 1Cross-modal illusion
    • 2Size illusion
    • 3Muller-Lyer illusion
    • 4Visual illusion
    ✓ Answer: (1) Cross-modal illusion
    Harry McGurk and John MacDonald, McGurk effect (1976)
    The concept

    Why this oneSeeing lips say 'ga' while hearing 'ba' makes people hear 'da'. This is the McGurk effect, a cross-modal illusion, because vision changes what is heard.

    How it worksThe brain fuses auditory and visual speech cues into one best guess; 'da' is a compromise between the lip movement of 'ga' and the sound of 'ba'.

    Exam tipMcGurk effect = audiovisual speech; ventriloquism effect = sound location pulled toward the seen source. Both show multisensory integration.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Size illusionSize illusions, such as the Ponzo or Ebbinghaus illusion, distort perceived size within vision alone and involve no sound.
    • (3) Muller-Lyer illusionThe Müller-Lyer illusion makes two equal lines look unequal because of arrow-like fins; it is purely visual.
    • (4) Visual illusionA visual illusion stays within one sense; here vision alters hearing, so the illusion crosses two modalities.

  81. Retrieval is more commonly explained as the basis of which generation-recognition theory?

    • (a) Information Processing
    • (b) Iconic Image
    • (c) Sensory Register
    • (d) Primacy Effect

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a), (b) and (c) only
    • 2(a) only
    • 3(b) and (c) only
    • 4(b) and (d) only
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a) only
    Generate-recognise (two-stage) theory of recall
    The concept

    Why this oneThe generate-recognise theory explains retrieval as an information-processing sequence: first generate candidate items from memory, then run a recognition check on each.

    How it worksRecall needs both stages, while recognition needs only the second. This explains why recognition is usually easier than recall.

    Exam tipLinked to John Anderson and Gordon Bower, and to Walter Kintsch; Endel Tulving's recognition failure of recallable words later challenged it.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (b) and (c) onlyAdds (b) and (c): the iconic image and sensory register are the brief sensory store at input, not retrieval processes.
    • (3) (b) and (c) onlyThe iconic image and sensory register belong to sensory memory, which holds raw input for under a second; they play no part in retrieval theory.
    • (4) (b) and (d) onlyThe iconic image is visual sensory memory and the primacy effect is better recall of early list items; neither is the basis of generate-recognise theory.

  82. Which of the following is the correct explanation of the outgroup homogeneity effect?

    • 1Members of an in-group assume that other in-group members share their attitudes and values
    • 2Perception that members of an outgroup are more similar to each other than members of an in-group are to each other
    • 3The tendency to give more favourable evaluation and greater rewards to members of an out-group
    • 4People are arbitrarily classified into groups and then allowed to allocate rewards to each other
