    ✓ Answer: (2) Perception that members of an outgroup are more similar to each other than members of an in-group are to each other
    Outgroup homogeneity effect, social categorisation
    The concept

    Why this oneThe outgroup homogeneity effect is seeing outgroup members as all alike ('they are all the same') while seeing one's own group as varied.

    Why it happensWe know many in-group members as individuals and meet outgroup members less, so we hold a simpler, more stereotyped picture of them.

    Exam tipOutgroup homogeneity → 'they are all alike'; in-group bias → favouring 'us'; minimal group paradigm → Henri Tajfel's arbitrary groups.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Members of an in-group assume that other in-group members share their attitudes and valuesThis describes assumed similarity within the in-group, close to the false consensus effect, not the perceived sameness of outgroup members.
    • (3) The tendency to give more favourable evaluation and greater rewards to members of an out-groupThis reverses in-group favouritism; people normally give more favourable evaluations and rewards to their own group, not the outgroup.
    • (4) People are arbitrarily classified into groups and then allowed to allocate rewards to each otherThis describes Henri Tajfel's minimal group paradigm, the method that showed in-group bias even with trivial, arbitrary groups.

  83. Which of the following sequences of Bandura's observational learning is correct?

    • 1Attention process - retention - production - motivation
    • 2Retention - attention process - production - motivation
    • 3Attention process - motivation - retention - production
    • 4Attention process - retention - motivation - production
    ✓ Answer: (1) Attention process - retention - production - motivation
    Albert Bandura, observational learning (1977)
    The concept

    Correct orderAlbert Bandura: attention (notice the model) → retention (store it as images or words) → production (reproduce the act) → motivation (a reason to perform it).

    Why this orderYou cannot store what you did not notice or copy what you did not store; motivation then decides whether the learned behaviour is shown.

    Exam tipThe Bobo doll studies showed learning without performance: children who had seen the model punished imitated more once rewards were offered.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Retention - attention process - production - motivationPuts retention before attention, but a model's behaviour must be noticed before it can be stored in memory.
    • (3) Attention process - motivation - retention - productionPuts motivation second; motivation governs whether already learned behaviour is performed, so it comes last.
    • (4) Attention process - retention - motivation - productionSwaps the last two steps; motivation is the final step that decides whether the reproduced behaviour is actually shown.

  84. Projective measures, particularly inkblot tests, index which of the following?

    • 1Novelty context of creativity
    • 2Meaning context of creativity
    • 3Domain-specific creativity
    • 4Everyday creativity
    ✓ Best answer: (2) Meaning context of creativity
    Creativity assessment: inkblots as meaning-making tasks
    The concept

    Why this oneInkblots have no fixed content, so the person must assign meaning to an ambiguous shape. Creativity then shows as rich, apt interpretations, that is, creativity in a meaning context.

    How it worksThis is like the pattern meanings and line meanings tasks of Michael Wallach and Nathan Kogan, where children say what abstract drawings could be.

    Exam tipDivergent-thinking tests stress fluency and originality; expert ratings of products tap domain-specific creativity; self-report checklists tap everyday creativity.

    Not certainThe source of this classification is unclear; inkblot responses are also scored for originality, which would point to novelty (1).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Novelty context of creativityNovelty is indexed mainly by originality scores on divergent-thinking tasks such as unusual uses; inkblots ask the person to interpret, that is, to make meaning.
    • (3) Domain-specific creativityDomain-specific creativity is judged from real products in one field, such as poems or collages, rated by experts, as in Teresa Amabile's consensual assessment technique.
    • (4) Everyday creativityEveryday (little-c) creativity is measured by self-report checklists of creative acts in daily life, not by responses to ambiguous inkblots.

  85. Which of the following theorists used the idiographic approach to personality?

    • (a) Allport
    • (b) Kelley
    • (c) Cattell
    • (d) Eysenck

    Select the correct option:

    • 1Only (a) and (b)
    • 2Only (a) and (c)
    • 3Only (c) and (d)
    • 4Only (a), (c) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (1) Only (a) and (b)
    Gordon Allport and George Kelly, idiographic approach
    The concept

    Why this oneGordon Allport championed the idiographic study of the unique person (as in his Letters from Jenny), and George Kelly (printed 'Kelley') mapped each person's own constructs with the Rep test.

    What it meansIdiographic = in-depth study of one person's unique pattern; nomothetic = general laws and traits measured across many people.

    Exam tipRaymond Cattell (16PF) and Hans Eysenck (PEN model) were nomothetic factor analysts; Allport brought the idiographic and nomothetic terms into psychology.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Only (a) and (c)Adds Raymond Cattell, whose 16PF trait model came from factor analysis of large samples, a nomothetic approach.
    • (3) Only (c) and (d)Cattell and Hans Eysenck both used factor analysis to find traits common to everyone, the nomothetic approach, not the idiographic one.
    • (4) Only (a), (c) and (d)Adds Cattell and Eysenck, both nomothetic factor analysts, and leaves out George Kelly, whose Rep test maps each person's own constructs.

  86. Match List-I with List-II:

    List-I (Concept): (a) Impairment; (b) Disability; (c) Handicap; (d) Disadvantage

    List-II (Description): (i) Any loss or abnormality of psychological, physiological or anatomical structure or function; (ii) Any restriction or lack of ability to perform an activity in an age-appropriate manner; (iii) Perception and assessment of one's abilities to be lower relative to others; (iv) Inability to perform within expected norms

    Select the correct option:

    • 1a-i; b-ii; c-iii; d-iv
    • 2a-ii; b-iv; c-i; d-iii
    • 3a-iii; b-i; c-iv; d-ii
    • 4a-iv; b-iii; c-ii; d-i
    ✓ Answer: (1) a-i; b-ii; c-iii; d-iv
    WHO International Classification of Impairments, Disabilities and Handicaps (1980)
    The concept

    Correct pairs(a) Impairment = (i) loss or abnormality of structure or function; (b) Disability = (ii) restricted ability to perform an activity; (c) Handicap = (iii) judged lower relative to others; (d) Disadvantage = (iv) inability to perform within expected norms.

    What it isThe WHO ICIDH chain runs impairment (body level) → disability (activity level) → handicap (social disadvantage in fulfilling a normal role).

    ExampleLoss of a leg is an impairment, being unable to walk is a disability, and losing a job because of it is a handicap.

    Exam tipOnly option 1 pairs impairment with (i) and disability with (ii), the textbook WHO definitions, so the remaining pairs follow.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) a-ii; b-iv; c-i; d-iiiGives impairment the 'restriction of activity' description, which is the WHO definition of disability, not impairment.
    • (3) a-iii; b-i; c-iv; d-iiGives impairment the 'lower relative to others' description; impairment is the loss or abnormality of structure or function itself.
    • (4) a-iv; b-iii; c-ii; d-iMatches impairment with 'inability to perform within norms'; WHO defines impairment at the level of body structure or function.

  87. According to Jung, what is made up of all the archetypes taken together?

    • 1Personal unconscious
    • 2Collective unconscious
    • 3Mandala
    • 4Shadow
    ✓ Answer: (2) Collective unconscious
    Carl Jung, analytical psychology
    The concept

    Why this oneFor Carl Jung, the collective unconscious is the inherited layer of the psyche shared by all humans, and its contents are the archetypes.

    What archetypes areArchetypes are universal, inborn patterns, such as the persona, shadow, anima and animus, and the self, that appear in myths, dreams and religions.

    Exam tipPersonal unconscious holds complexes; collective unconscious holds archetypes.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Personal unconsciousJung's personal unconscious holds an individual's forgotten and repressed experiences, organised into complexes, not the inherited archetypes.
    • (3) MandalaThe mandala is a circular symbol of wholeness that expresses the self archetype; it is one symbol, not the sum of all archetypes.
    • (4) ShadowThe shadow is a single archetype: the dark, unacceptable, animal side of the personality.

  88. Which of the following are steps of the problem-solving cycle?

    • (a) Problem identification
    • (b) Problem definition
    • (c) Organization
    • (d) Allocation of resources

    Select the correct option:

    • 1(a) and (b) only
    • 2(c) and (d) only
    • 3(b), (c) and (d) only
    • 4(a), (b), (c) and (d)
    ✓ Answer: (4) (a), (b), (c) and (d)
    Robert Sternberg, problem-solving cycle
    The concept

    Why this oneRobert Sternberg's problem-solving cycle includes all four: identify the problem, define it, organise the information, and allocate resources.

    Full cycleProblem identification → problem definition → strategy formulation → organisation of information → allocation of resources → monitoring → evaluation.

    Exam tipIt is a cycle because solving one problem often reveals a new one, which restarts identification.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a) and (b) onlyCovers only the first two steps; organising information and allocating resources are also steps in Sternberg's cycle.
    • (2) (c) and (d) onlyLeaves out problem identification and definition, which open the cycle and must come before any organising.
    • (3) (b), (c) and (d) onlyLeaves out problem identification, the first step: a problem must be recognised before it can be defined.

  89. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): The likelihood that fetal alcohol syndrome will occur depends on the social environment.

    Reason (R): Social influences also act upon the developing brain and mind.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is false
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    Prenatal development in social context (biocultural view)
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. Fetal alcohol syndrome is far more common among poor mothers: poor nutrition, stress, heavier drinking and little prenatal care all raise the risk.

    ReasonTrue. Development is biocultural: social conditions reach the fetus and the developing brain through the mother's body, diet and behaviour.

    LinkR explains A: because social influences act on the developing brain, the same teratogen (alcohol) has different effects in different social settings.

    Exam tipA teratogen's harm depends on dose, timing (critical periods), genes and the environment.

    Not certainOne can argue that poverty acts through drinking patterns and nutrition rather than through social influence on the brain, which would give (2).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)R is not a side fact: it is the principle that makes A true, since social conditions shape how alcohol affects prenatal brain growth.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseR is true; prenatal and early brain development are shaped by social factors such as poverty, nutrition and maternal stress.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is falseA is true: the risk of fetal alcohol syndrome rises with poverty and poor nutrition, so its likelihood does depend on the social environment.

  90. Given below are two statements, one labelled as Assertion (A) and the other labelled as Reason (R). Read the statements.

    Assertion (A): Changing a client's perception requires persuasive skills and direction from the counsellor, and such input is known as a lead.

    Reason (R): The counsellor should make minimum verbal responses and maintain silence so that clients feel some pressure to continue, and will choose to continue with minimum input from the counsellor, thus the counsellor taking a good lead.

    Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
    • 2Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    • 3(A) is true but (R) is false
    • 4(A) is false but (R) is true
    ✓ Best answer: (2) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)
    Counselling leads and minimal encouragers
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. A lead is a counsellor response that directs the client's thinking; changing a client's perception calls for strong leads such as interpretation, confrontation and persuasion.

    ReasonTrue. Minimal encouragers ('mm-hmm', a nod) and silence are the weakest leads: they put gentle pressure on the client to keep talking with little counsellor input.

    LinkR does not explain A: A is about strong, directive leads that change perceptions, while R describes minimal leads that only keep the client talking.

    Exam tipLeads run on a continuum from silence (least lead) through restatement and questions to interpretation and advice (most lead).

    Not certainR uses 'lead' loosely; if minimal responses are judged not to be a lead at all, R is false and the answer is (3).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)Both are true, but R describes minimal leads that keep the client talking, not the persuasive direction needed to change perceptions in A.
    • (3) (A) is true but (R) is falseR is true: minimal verbal responses and silence are recognised low-level leads that encourage the client to continue.
    • (4) (A) is false but (R) is trueA is true: directive responses that steer a client's thinking, including persuasion, are what counselling texts call leads.

  91. Read the following paragraph and answer the five questions which follow. A psychologist wanted to develop an achievement test in Chemistry for SSC students of central schools. For this purpose he wrote 110 items having a multiple-choice format and administered this draft version to a sample of 300 subjects. Item analysis was carried out by computing item-remainder correlations, and retained 80 items. The eighty-item version was administered to a fresh sample of 400 subjects, which yielded a mean of 40.00 and a standard deviation of 8.00. The scores were found to be normally distributed. Cronbach's alpha for the eighty-item version was found to be 0.94. The psychologist also computed odd-even reliability and Kuder-Richardson (K-R) reliability for this version. On this version, one of the subjects, Manas, had a score of 32. Since the eighty-item version was considered long for administration, two forms, Form A and Form B, were prepared by randomly assigning 40 items to each form.

    The expected Cronbach's alpha for Form A would be:

    • 10.47
    • 20.89
    • 30.62
    • 40.94
    ✓ Answer: (2) 0.89
    Spearman-Brown prophecy formula
    The concept

    Why this oneForm A has 40 of the 80 items, half the length. The Spearman-Brown formula predicts the reliability of a test whose length changes by a factor n.

    Workingrnew = n r / [1 + (n - 1) r], with n = 0.5 and r = 0.94: (0.5 × 0.94) / (1 - 0.5 × 0.94) = 0.47 / 0.53 ≈ 0.89.

    Exam tipHalving a test lowers reliability but does not halve it; 0.47 is the trap. Lengthening raises reliability with diminishing returns.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 0.47Simply halves 0.94; reliability does not fall in proportion to length, as the Spearman-Brown formula shows.
    • (3) 0.62Too low; starting from an alpha of 0.94, a half-length test keeps reliability near 0.89 by the Spearman-Brown formula.
    • (4) 0.94That is the alpha of the full 80-item test; removing half the items must lower reliability.

  92. In the above study, the psychologist computed three reliability coefficients:

    • (a) Cronbach's alpha
    • (b) Split-half reliability coefficient
    • (c) Kuder-Richardson (K-R) reliability coefficient

    Which one of the following expectations is true?

    • 1(a), (b) and (c) would be the same
    • 2(a) and (c) would be the same, but (b) can be different
    • 3(b) and (c) would be the same but (a) would be different
    • 4(a), (b) and (c), all three would be different
    ✓ Answer: (2) (a) and (c) would be the same, but (b) can be different
    Lee Cronbach's alpha and Kuder-Richardson KR-20
    The concept

    Why this oneMultiple-choice items scored right or wrong are dichotomous, and for such items Cronbach's alpha and KR-20 are the same formula, so (a) and (c) match.

    Why (b) differsSplit-half (odd-even) reliability rests on one particular split and then needs the Spearman-Brown correction, so it can be higher or lower than alpha.

    Exam tipLee Cronbach showed alpha is the average of all possible split-half coefficients, so any single split can come out different.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) (a), (b) and (c) would be the sameSplit-half depends on how the items are split and so can differ from alpha; only alpha and KR-20 are identical for dichotomous items.
    • (3) (b) and (c) would be the same but (a) would be differentSplit-half and K-R are computed differently and need not agree; it is alpha and K-R that coincide for right or wrong items.
    • (4) (a), (b) and (c), all three would be differentAlpha and KR-20 cannot differ here: KR-20 is the special case of alpha for items scored 0 or 1.

  93. For computing item-remainder correlations, which one of the following correlations would be suitable?

    • 1Phi-coefficient
    • 2Contingency coefficient
    • 3Point-biserial correlation
    • 4Tetrachoric correlation
    ✓ Answer: (3) Point-biserial correlation
    Point-biserial correlation in item analysis
    The concept

    Why this oneAn item-remainder correlation links one item scored 0 or 1 (a true dichotomy) with the total on the remaining items (continuous). The point-biserial r is built for exactly this pairing.

    Why the remainderLeaving the item out of the total stops the correlation being inflated by the item correlating with itself.

    Exam tipDichotomous + continuous = point-biserial (biserial if the dichotomy is artificial); two dichotomies = phi or tetrachoric.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Phi-coefficientPhi is for two genuinely dichotomous variables, such as two items scored right or wrong, not an item paired with a continuous total.
    • (2) Contingency coefficientThe contingency coefficient measures association between two nominal variables in a cross-table, based on chi-square.
    • (4) Tetrachoric correlationTetrachoric r is for two variables that are both artificially dichotomised from underlying normal distributions.

  94. The score of 32 (score obtained by Manas on the eighty-item version) would correspond to percentile rank:

    • 116
    • 232
    • 380
    • 484
    ✓ Answer: (1) 16
    Normal curve: z score to percentile rank
    The concept

    Workingz = (X - M) / SD = (32 - 40) / 8 = -1.00. About 15.87% of a normal distribution lies below z = -1, so the percentile rank is about 16.

    Why34.13% of cases lie between the mean and 1 SD, so 50 - 34.13 = 15.87% lie more than one SD below the mean.

    Exam tipRemember 16, 50, 84 for z = -1, 0, +1, and about 2 and 98 for z = -2 and +2.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) 3232 is Manas's raw score, not his relative standing; a raw score is not a percentile rank.
    • (3) 8080 is the number of items on the test, not the percentage of people scoring below 32.
    • (4) 8484 is the percentile for z = +1, a score of 48; Manas is one SD below the mean, not above it.

  95. The 95% confidence interval for the true score of 32 would be:

    • 132 &plusmn; 0.94
    • 232 &plusmn; 1.96
    • 332 &plusmn; 6.00
    • 432 &plusmn; 3.84
    ✓ Answer: (4) 32 &plusmn; 3.84
    Standard error of measurement, confidence interval
    The concept

    WorkingSEM = SD √(1 - r) = 8 × √(1 - 0.94) = 8 × 0.245 ≈ 1.96. The 95% interval is X ± 1.96 SEM = 32 ± 1.96 × 1.96 = 32 ± 3.84.

    What it isThe standard error of measurement is the spread of a person's observed scores around their true score over many hypothetical retests.

    Exam tip68% interval = ±1 SEM; 95% = ±1.96 SEM; 99% = ±2.58 SEM. Higher reliability gives a narrower band.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 32 &plusmn; 0.940.94 is the reliability coefficient itself, not a band in score units; the band must come from the SEM.
    • (2) 32 &plusmn; 1.96This band is one SEM wide, which gives only about a 68% interval; 95% needs 1.96 × SEM = 3.84.
    • (3) 32 &plusmn; 6.00Too wide; with an SD of 8 and reliability of 0.94, the 95% band is 1.96 × 1.96 = 3.84 points.

  96. Read the following passage and answer the questions that follow. A researcher wanted to study the effect of organization of material (A) on recall. She expected that retention interval (B) would moderate the effect of organization of material. She varied A at three levels (low organization, moderate organization, and high organization) and varied B at two levels (short retention interval and long retention interval). The subjects were randomly assigned to the different groups. Each subject received the list of words with the specified level of organization, learnt the list up to one errorless trial, and was tested after the specified retention interval. All the subjects had also received an intelligence test. The number of items recalled by the subject was the dependent variable. The data were analysed by the appropriate ANOVA, treating intelligence as a covariate. The partial results are: (i) the sum of squares for organization of material (A) was 255.00; (ii) the degrees of freedom for error variance was 115; (iii) the F ratio for interaction (A x B) was 0.95. The obtained results were appropriately interpreted.

    The design in the above study can be best labelled as:

    • 1randomized single-factor six-group design with a covariate
    • 2randomized balanced 3x2 factorial design with a covariate
    • 3randomized unbalanced 3x2 factorial design with a covariate
    • 4randomized 3x2 blocked factorial design with a covariate
    ✓ Answer: (3) randomized unbalanced 3x2 factorial design with a covariate
    Factorial ANCOVA design with unequal cell sizes
    The concept

    Why this oneTwo factors are crossed (A: 3 levels × B: 2 levels), subjects are randomly assigned, and intelligence is a covariate. Error df = N - 6 - 1 = 115 gives N = 122, which cannot split equally into 6 cells, so the design is unbalanced.

    What it meansBalanced = equal n in every cell; unbalanced = unequal cell sizes, so the effects are no longer independent and need adjusted sums of squares.

    Exam tipModeration (B changing the effect of A) can only be tested in a factorial design, through the A × B interaction.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) randomized single-factor six-group design with a covariateCalling it single-factor hides the two crossed factors; a factorial design is needed to test whether retention interval moderates organisation.
    • (2) randomized balanced 3x2 factorial design with a covariateBalanced needs equal cells, but 122 subjects cannot be divided equally among 6 groups (122 / 6 is not a whole number).
    • (4) randomized 3x2 blocked factorial design with a covariateBlocking groups subjects on a variable before assignment; here intelligence is a covariate adjusted statistically, and subjects were simply randomised.

  97. The mean square for organization of material would be:

    • 142.50
    • 2127.50
    • 385.00
    • 4255.00
    ✓ Answer: (2) 127.50
    Mean square = sum of squares / df
    The concept

    WorkingMS = SS / df. Organisation of material has 3 levels, so df = 3 - 1 = 2, and MS = 255.00 / 2 = 127.50.

    Exam tipdf for a factor = levels - 1; df for an interaction = product of the factors' df (here 2 × 1 = 2).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 42.50Divides by 6, the number of cells; a main effect's sum of squares is divided by its own df (levels - 1).
    • (3) 85.00Divides by 3, the number of levels, instead of the degrees of freedom (3 - 1 = 2).
    • (4) 255.00This is the sum of squares itself; it would equal the mean square only if df were 1.

  98. From the above results, it can be concluded that:

    • 1Main effect of organization of material is significant
    • 2Main effect of organization of material is non-significant
    • 3Retention interval does not moderate the effect of organization of material
    • 4Retention interval moderates the effect of organization of material
    ✓ Answer: (3) Retention interval does not moderate the effect of organization of material
    Interpreting F ratios in factorial ANCOVA
    The concept

    Why this oneThe A × B F ratio is 0.95, below 1, so it cannot be significant. The interaction is the test of moderation, so retention interval does not moderate the effect of organisation.

    Main effect unknownMS for organisation is known (127.50) but MS error is not given, so its F ratio cannot be computed and no conclusion is possible.

    Exam tipAn F below 1 is never significant, because the effect variance is smaller than the error variance.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Main effect of organization of material is significantCannot be judged: MS error is not given, so the F ratio for organisation of material cannot be computed.
    • (2) Main effect of organization of material is non-significantEqually untestable: without MS error there is no F ratio for organisation and no basis to call it non-significant.
    • (4) Retention interval moderates the effect of organization of materialModeration would need a significant A × B interaction, but F = 0.95 is below 1 and cannot be significant.

  99. How many subjects have been employed in this study?

    • 1116
    • 2120
    • 3121
    • 4122
    ✓ Answer: (4) 122
    ANCOVA error degrees of freedom
    The concept

    WorkingTotal df (N - 1) splits into between cells (6 - 1 = 5), covariate (1) and error. So error df = N - 6 - 1; 115 = N - 7, giving N = 122.

    Exam tipIn ANCOVA each covariate takes 1 df from the error term, so error df = N - number of cells - number of covariates.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 116116 is error df + 1, which ignores the 5 df used by the cells and the 1 df used by the covariate.
    • (2) 120120 assumes 20 per cell, but that would give error df = 120 - 6 - 1 = 113, not 115.
    • (3) 121121 forgets the covariate's 1 df; with a covariate, error df = N - 6 - 1.

  100. What would be the degrees of freedom for the covariate?

    • 11
    • 22
    • 35
    • 46
    ✓ Answer: (1) 1
    ANCOVA: one df per covariate
    The concept

    Why this oneEach covariate is one continuous predictor with one regression slope, so it uses exactly 1 df. Intelligence is the only covariate here.

    How it worksANCOVA removes the variance in recall predicted by intelligence, then tests A, B and A × B on the adjusted scores.

    Exam tipdf for k covariates = k, and they come out of the error df.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) 22 is the df for organisation of material (3 levels - 1) and for the A × B interaction, not for a single covariate.
    • (3) 55 is the between-cells df (6 groups - 1), not the df for the covariate.
    • (4) 66 is the number of groups in the 3 × 2 design, not a degrees-of-freedom value.

5. 2020 NET paper (answers worked out)

This is the UGC NET Psychology 2020 paper, reproduced from a candidate's computer-based-test response sheet (digialm portal). No official answer key was ever found for it, so every answer below is worked out from the standard textbooks and checked against a second, separate solve. The picks that candidate made are no longer shown: they were one test-taker's choices, not answers. Each question shows the answer and who it comes from, the concept in short chunks, and one line on why each other option is wrong.

Trap

These answers are worked out, not an official key. Best answer marks the 16 questions that are ambiguous, come from a niche source or look miscopied; read the "Not certain" line there, it names the runner-up option.

  1. Which of the following is useful for testing univariate normality of distribution? (A) Q-Q plot (B) Shapiro-Wilk test (C) Jarque-Bera test (D) Scatter plot

    • 1A and B only
    • 2B and D only
    • 3A, B and D only
    • 4A, B and C only
    ✓ Answer: (4) A, B and C only
    Tests of univariate normality
    The concept

    Why this oneA Q-Q plot, the Shapiro-Wilk test and the Jarque-Bera test all check whether a single variable follows a normal distribution. A scatter plot needs two variables, so it cannot test univariate normality.

    How each worksA Q-Q plot puts the data's quantiles against normal quantiles, and points on a straight line mean normality; Shapiro-Wilk compares ordered scores with expected normal scores, and Jarque-Bera tests whether skewness and kurtosis match a normal curve.

    Exam tipA significant Shapiro-Wilk or Jarque-Bera result (p below .05) means the data depart from normality. Scatter plots are used to check linearity and homoscedasticity between two variables.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A and B onlyQ-Q plot and Shapiro-Wilk are correct, but the list is incomplete: the Jarque-Bera test also tests univariate normality from skewness and kurtosis.
    • (2) B and D onlyShapiro-Wilk fits, but a scatter plot shows the relationship between two variables, and the Q-Q plot and Jarque-Bera test are left out.
    • (3) A, B and D onlyWrongly includes the scatter plot, a bivariate graph for linearity, and leaves out the Jarque-Bera test, which checks skewness and kurtosis against the normal curve.

  2. Which is the correct sequence of speech and language development according to Vygotsky? (A) Naive (B) Primitive (C) Egocentric (D) Ingrowth

    • 1A, B, C, D
    • 2B, A, C, D
    • 3A, B, D, C
    • 4B, A, D, C
    ✓ Answer: (2) B, A, C, D
    Lev Vygotsky, Thought and Language (1934)
    The concept

    Why this oneLev Vygotsky described four stages of speech: primitive (natural), naive psychology, egocentric speech and ingrowth. That is B, A, C, D.

    Correct order(B) Primitive: crying, babbling and other pre-intellectual sounds; (A) Naive: words used correctly before their logic is grasped; (C) Egocentric: talking aloud to guide one's own actions; (D) Ingrowth: speech goes inward as silent inner speech.

    Exam tipFor Vygotsky, egocentric speech does not simply die out, as Jean Piaget thought; it turns into inner speech that guides thinking.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, B, C, DPuts naive psychology before the primitive stage, but the primitive (natural) stage of crying and babbling is the starting point in Vygotsky's scheme.
    • (3) A, B, D, CStarts with naive instead of primitive and puts ingrowth before egocentric speech, when inner speech grows out of egocentric speech.
    • (4) B, A, D, CGets the first two stages right but reverses the last two: egocentric speech comes first and only later turns inward as ingrowth (inner speech).

  3. Match List I with List II. List I (Types of Reliability): (A) Test-retest (B) Alternate Form (delayed) (C) Coefficient alpha (D) Cohen's Kappa. List II (Source of Error Variance): (I) Content heterogeneity (II) Inter-scorer differences (III) Time sampling (IV) Content sampling and time sampling. Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1A-IV, B-II, C-I, D-III
    • 2A-IV, B-I, C-III, D-III
    • 3A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II
    • 4A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I
    ✓ Answer: (3) A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II
    Anne Anastasi, sources of error variance in reliability
    The concept

    Why this oneEach reliability method picks up a different source of error: test-retest catches time sampling, delayed alternate forms catch content and time sampling, alpha catches content heterogeneity, and Cohen's kappa catches inter-scorer differences.

    Correct pairs(A) Test-retest = (III) Time sampling; (B) Alternate form (delayed) = (IV) Content sampling and time sampling; (C) Coefficient alpha = (I) Content heterogeneity; (D) Cohen's kappa = (II) Inter-scorer differences.

    How it worksDelayed alternate forms change both the items and the occasion, so two errors enter. Cohen's kappa measures agreement between two raters beyond chance, so its error is scorer disagreement.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A-IV, B-II, C-I, D-IIIGives test-retest the double error of delayed alternate forms and pairs Cohen's kappa with time sampling; kappa is about agreement between raters, not occasions.
    • (2) A-IV, B-I, C-III, D-IIIUses time sampling twice, for alpha and kappa; coefficient alpha reflects item heterogeneity and kappa reflects inter-scorer differences.
    • (4) A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-IGets A and B right but swaps C and D: coefficient alpha reflects content heterogeneity, while inter-scorer differences belong to Cohen's kappa.

  4. Which of the following are involved in decreased food intake and weight loss? (A) Insulin (B) Leptin (C) Cholecystokinin (D) Orexin

    • 1A and B only
    • 2A, B and C only
    • 3A, B and D only
    • 4B, C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (2) A, B and C only
    Satiety signals: leptin, insulin, cholecystokinin
    The concept

    Why this oneLeptin, insulin and cholecystokinin (CCK) all act as satiety signals that lower food intake. Orexin does the opposite: it increases hunger and eating.

    How it worksLeptin from fat cells and insulin from the pancreas tell the hypothalamus how much fat the body stores, so high levels cut long-term intake. CCK is released by the gut during a meal and ends the meal sooner.

    Exam tipEating up: ghrelin, orexin, neuropeptide Y; eating down: leptin, insulin (in the brain), CCK. Leptin-deficient ob/ob mice become very obese.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A and B onlyLeaves out cholecystokinin, a gut hormone released during a meal that signals fullness and shortens the meal.
    • (3) A, B and D onlyIncludes orexin (hypocretin), a lateral hypothalamic peptide that increases food intake and wakefulness, and leaves out CCK, which reduces intake.
    • (4) B, C and D onlyLeaves out insulin, which acts in the brain as a long-term satiety signal, and wrongly includes orexin, which stimulates eating.

  5. Jensen put forward the 'Regression' argument to account for which of the following?

    • 1To understand inter-generation changes in intelligence
    • 2To understand intra-generation changes in intelligence
    • 3To understand group differences in intelligence
    • 4To understand the role of genetics in intelligence
    ✓ Answer: (3) To understand group differences in intelligence
    Arthur Jensen, regression to the mean argument
    The concept

    Why this oneArthur Jensen used the regression argument in the debate on Black-White IQ differences in the USA, so it was meant to account for group differences in intelligence.

    How it worksChildren of very bright parents score closer to the average than their parents (regression to the mean). Jensen argued that children of high-IQ Black and White parents regress towards different group means, and took this as evidence for a partly genetic group difference.

    Exam tipCritics replied that regression towards different group means is expected whatever causes the gap, so it does not prove a genetic cause.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) To understand inter-generation changes in intelligenceRising IQ scores across generations is the Flynn effect, described by James Flynn; Jensen's regression argument dealt with differences between racial groups.
    • (2) To understand intra-generation changes in intelligenceChanges within one generation, such as IQ gains with age or schooling, were not its target; the argument compared groups.
    • (4) To understand the role of genetics in intelligenceToo broad: Jensen did hold that genes matter, but the regression argument was used specifically to explain the gap between groups.

  6. Match List I with List II. List I: (A) Happiness results from experiences of personal expressiveness (B) Happiness is self-realization, meaning the expression and fulfilment of inner potentials (C) Happiness reflects an individual's own judgement about the quality of his or her life (D) Happiness results from the fulfilment of three basic psychological needs: autonomy, competence and relatedness. List II: (I) Ryan & Deci (II) Diener (III) Aristotle (IV) Waterman. Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV
    • 2A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II
    • 3A-IV, B-III, C-II, D-I
    • 4A-II, B-I, C-IV, D-III
    ✓ Answer: (3) A-IV, B-III, C-II, D-I
    Eudaimonic and hedonic views of well-being
    The concept

    Why this oneAlan Waterman linked happiness to personal expressiveness, Aristotle to self-realisation (eudaimonia), Ed Diener to one's own judgement of life, and Richard Ryan and Edward Deci to three basic needs.

    Correct pairs(A) Personal expressiveness = (IV) Waterman; (B) Self-realisation = (III) Aristotle; (C) Own judgement of life quality = (II) Diener; (D) Autonomy, competence, relatedness = (I) Ryan and Deci.

    Two traditionsHedonic views stress pleasure and life satisfaction (Diener's subjective well-being); eudaimonic views stress living true to one's potential (Aristotle, Waterman, and Ryan and Deci's self-determination theory).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IVGives self-realisation to Diener and the three basic needs to Waterman; the three needs come from Ryan and Deci's self-determination theory.
    • (2) A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-IIGives personal expressiveness to Aristotle and life judgement to Ryan and Deci; judging one's own life quality is Diener's subjective well-being.
    • (4) A-II, B-I, C-IV, D-IIIGives personal expressiveness to Diener and the three needs to Aristotle; autonomy, competence and relatedness come from Ryan and Deci.

  7. Match List I with List II. List I: (A) The theory that an individual's self-concept is derived partly from membership in a group (B) The theory that prejudice arises from a real conflict of interest between groups (C) The theory that all societies are organised in group hierarchies and sustained through discrimination (D) The most important social perspective in social psychology. List II: (I) Realistic group conflict theory (II) Social Dominance theory (III) Social Identity theory (IV) Interdependence theory. Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1A-I, B-III, C-II, D-IV
    • 2A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I
    • 3A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV
    • 4A-II, B-III, C-IV, D-I
    ✓ Best answer: (1) A-I, B-III, C-II, D-IV
    Henri Tajfel, Muzafer Sherif, Jim Sidanius, Harold Kelley
    The concept

    Why this oneOption (1) gets C and D right and only swaps the labels of A and B, the likeliest copying slip. The descriptions themselves match four well-known intergroup theories.

    Correct pairs(A) Self-concept from group membership = Social identity theory (Henri Tajfel, John Turner); (B) Real conflict of interest = Realistic group conflict theory (Muzafer Sherif); (C) Hierarchies kept by discrimination = Social dominance theory (Jim Sidanius, Felicia Pratto); (D) = Interdependence theory (John Thibaut, Harold Kelley).

    Exam tipSherif's Robbers Cave study is the classic test of realistic conflict; Tajfel's minimal group studies show bias even without any real conflict.

    Not certainAs printed no option fits the true pairs A-III, B-I, C-II, D-IV; option 1 fits if I and III were swapped in copying, and option 2 (also two pairs right) is the runner-up.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-IGets A and C right but gives real conflict of interest to interdependence theory, when it is realistic group conflict theory (Muzafer Sherif).
    • (3) A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IVGives the hierarchy description to social identity theory and real conflict to social dominance theory; only D is right.
    • (4) A-II, B-III, C-IV, D-IGives self-concept to social dominance theory and hierarchies to interdependence theory; self-concept from groups is Tajfel and Turner's social identity theory.

  8. The enhancement of dominant responses is strongest when people think they are being evaluated. What is the correct sequence of this theory? (A) Evaluation apprehension (B) Well learned (C) Presence of others (D) Increased arousal (E) Social facilitation

    • 1B, A, C, D, E
    • 2D, A, B, E
    • 3A, B, C, D, E
    • 4A, C, D, B, E
    ✓ Best answer: (4) A, C, D, B, E
    Nickolas Cottrell, evaluation apprehension; Robert Zajonc, drive theory
    The concept

    Why this oneOption (4) gets the core chain right: arousal boosts the dominant (well-learned) response, giving social facilitation. Strictly, the presence of others comes before evaluation apprehension.

    Correct order(C) Presence of others → (A) evaluation apprehension (worry about being judged) → (D) increased arousal → (B) the well-learned, dominant response becomes more likely → (E) social facilitation on easy tasks.

    What it isRobert Zajonc said the mere presence of others raises arousal. Nickolas Cottrell added that the effect is strongest when the others can evaluate us; blindfolded onlookers produced little effect.

    Not certainThe textbook chain is C, A, D, B, E (presence of others comes first), which no option prints; option 4 only swaps the first two steps, while option 1 wrongly starts with the well-learned response.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) B, A, C, D, EStarts with the well-learned response, but the dominant response is the result of arousal, near the end of the chain, not its trigger.
    • (2) D, A, B, ELists only four steps, leaves out the presence of others, and starts with arousal, when arousal follows evaluation apprehension.
    • (3) A, B, C, D, EPuts the well-learned response before the presence of others and arousal; in the theory, arousal comes first and then strengthens the dominant response.

  9. Which technique of studying the brain involves injecting the patient with radioactive glucose?

    • 1EEG
    • 2MRI
    • 3PET
    • 4CT
    ✓ Answer: (3) PET
    Positron emission tomography (PET)
    The concept

    Why this oneIn a PET scan the patient receives radioactive glucose or a similar tracer. Busy brain areas use more glucose, so they show up as active on the scan.

    How it worksDetectors pick up the positrons given off by the tracer and a computer builds a colour map of activity. PET shows brain function, not just structure.

    Exam tipStructure: CT, MRI. Function: PET, fMRI (blood oxygen, no injection), EEG (electrical activity).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) EEGEEG records the brain's electrical activity through electrodes on the scalp; nothing is injected.
    • (2) MRIMRI uses a strong magnetic field and radio waves to image brain structure; no radioactive tracer is used.
    • (4) CTCT combines many X-ray images taken from different angles to show brain structure, not glucose use.

  10. Given below are two statements, one is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R. Assertion A: The NEO-PI-R does not have a response style scale. Reason R: NEO-PI-R developers argued that adjustment of social desirability leads to a reduction in validity. In light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A
    • 2Both A and R are correct and R is NOT the correct explanation of A
    • 3A is correct but R is not correct
    • 4A is not correct but R is correct
    ✓ Answer: (1) Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A
    Paul Costa and Robert McCrae, NEO-PI-R
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. The NEO-PI-R by Paul Costa and Robert McCrae has no lie, faking or social desirability scale, unlike the MMPI with its L, F and K scales.

    ReasonTrue. Costa and McCrae argued that socially desirable answers often reflect real traits, so correcting scores for social desirability makes them less valid, not more.

    Why R explains AThey left a response style scale out of the test precisely because they believed such corrections lower validity.

    Why the other options are not the answer
    • (2) Both A and R are correct and R is NOT the correct explanation of AR is not a side fact: it is the stated reason why the authors chose not to build a response style scale into the test.
    • (3) A is correct but R is not correctR is true: Costa and McCrae did argue that adjusting scores for social desirability reduces validity.
    • (4) A is not correct but R is correctA is true: the NEO-PI-R has no formal response style or validity scale like the MMPI's L, F and K.

  11. Which of the following are comprised in executive functions? (A) Planning (B) Attention (C) Set shifting (D) Declarative memory

    • 1A, B and C only
    • 2A, B and D only
    • 3A, C and D only
    • 4B, C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (1) A, B and C only
    Executive functions of the prefrontal cortex
    The concept

    Why this oneExecutive functions are the control processes that run goal-directed behaviour: planning, controlling attention and set shifting. Declarative memory is a memory store, not a control process.

    What it isThey depend mainly on the prefrontal cortex. Common tests are the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test (set shifting), Tower of London (planning) and Stroop (inhibition and attention).

    Exam tipAkira Miyake's model names three core executive functions: shifting, updating of working memory and inhibition.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) A, B and D onlyIncludes declarative memory, the conscious memory for facts and events that depends on the hippocampus, and leaves out set shifting, a core executive function.
    • (3) A, C and D onlyLeaves out attention control and wrongly includes declarative memory, which is a long-term memory system, not an executive process.
    • (4) B, C and D onlyLeaves out planning, a classic executive function, and includes declarative memory, which is stored knowledge rather than control.

  12. Given below are two statements, one is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R. Assertion A: According to Classical Test Theory (CTT) total variance is equal to the sum of true variance and error variance. Reason R: Error variance and true variance are independent of each other as per CTT. In light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A
    • 2Both A and R are correct but R is NOT the correct explanation of A
    • 3A is correct but R is not correct
    • 4A is not correct but R is correct
    ✓ Answer: (1) Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A
    Classical test theory, X = T + E
    The concept

    AssertionTrue. In classical test theory an observed score is true score plus error (X = T + E), and total variance splits as σ2X = σ2T + σ2E.

    ReasonTrue. CTT assumes error is random and uncorrelated with true scores.

    Why R explains AThe variance of a sum equals the sum of the variances plus twice their covariance. Because T and E are independent, the covariance is zero, so total variance is simply true plus error variance.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both A and R are correct but R is NOT the correct explanation of AR is the very condition that makes A hold: without zero covariance between true and error scores, an extra covariance term would appear.
    • (3) A is correct but R is not correctR is true: independence of true and error scores is a core assumption of classical test theory.
    • (4) A is not correct but R is correctA is true: in classical test theory total variance equals true variance plus error variance.

  13. What is the retention without conscious recollection of skills and dispositions called?

    • 1Iconic memory
    • 2Implicit memory
    • 3Explicit memory
    • 4Semantic memory
    ✓ Answer: (2) Implicit memory
    Daniel Schacter, implicit memory
    The concept

    Why this oneImplicit memory is retention shown in behaviour without conscious recollection, such as skills, habits and conditioned dispositions.

    ExamplesRiding a bicycle, typing, or feeling uneasy near dogs after a bite. Priming is also implicit: an earlier exposure speeds later recognition without awareness.

    Exam tipAmnesic patients such as H.M. (Henry Molaison) lose explicit memory but still learn new skills such as mirror drawing, showing the two systems are separate.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Iconic memoryIconic memory is the brief visual sensory store, lasting under a second, shown by George Sperling's partial report studies.
    • (3) Explicit memoryExplicit (declarative) memory is the opposite: facts and events that are consciously recalled.
    • (4) Semantic memorySemantic memory, named by Endel Tulving, is consciously accessible general knowledge such as word meanings and facts, a part of explicit memory.

  14. Arrange in sequence what Yerkes described commonly takes place in "insight learning". (A) Sharpening and frequent recurrent attention to the goal (B) Trial (C) Survey/Inspection (D) Attitude of concentrated attention (E) Critical point

    • 1B, C, A, D, E
    • 2B, D, C, A, E
    • 3C, D, B, A, E
    • 4B, C, A, E, D
    ✓ Answer: (3) C, D, B, A, E
    Robert Yerkes, insight in great apes
    The concept

    Why this oneRobert Yerkes, watching apes solve problems, saw them first survey the situation, pause in concentrated attention, try a response, keep attending to the goal, and finally reach a critical point of sudden solution.

    Correct order(C) Survey/inspection → (D) attitude of concentrated attention → (B) trial → (A) sharpening and recurrent attention to the goal → (E) critical point, after which the act is readily repeated.

    Exam tipInsight learning is linked with Wolfgang Köhler's chimpanzee Sultan, who joined sticks and stacked boxes to reach bananas. Insight is sudden, transfers well and is not blind trial and error.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) B, C, A, D, EStarts with trial, but in Yerkes' account the animal first surveys and inspects the problem; trial comes only after concentrated attention.
    • (2) B, D, C, A, EStarts with trial before survey; trial comes third, after inspection and a pause of concentrated attention.
    • (4) B, C, A, E, DStarts with trial and puts the critical point before concentrated attention; the critical point, the sudden solution, is the last step.

  15. Who among the following is credited with proposing the investment theory of creativity?

    • 1Getzels and Jackson
    • 2Kaufman
    • 3Wallach and Kogan
    • 4Sternberg
    ✓ Answer: (4) Sternberg
    Robert Sternberg and Todd Lubart, investment theory
    The concept

    Why this oneRobert Sternberg and Todd Lubart proposed the investment theory: creative people 'buy low and sell high', backing unpopular ideas and moving on once they become accepted.

    How it worksCreativity needs six resources working together: intellectual abilities, knowledge, thinking styles, personality, motivation and environment.

    Exam tipSternberg also gave the triarchic theory of intelligence and the triangular theory of love.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Getzels and JacksonJacob Getzels and Philip Jackson studied gifted school pupils and showed that high creativity and high IQ were only weakly related.
    • (2) KaufmanJames Kaufman, with Ronald Beghetto, proposed the Four C model of creativity (mini-c, little-c, Pro-c, Big-C).
    • (3) Wallach and KoganMichael Wallach and Nathan Kogan tested children's creativity in a relaxed, game-like, untimed setting and found it fairly separate from intelligence.

  16. Which of the following principle is violated when a psychologist intentionally misrepresents his research results?

    • 1Beneficence
    • 2Fidelity and responsibility
    • 3Integrity
    • 4Confidentiality
    ✓ Answer: (3) Integrity
    APA Ethics Code, Principle C: Integrity
    The concept

    Why this oneAPA Principle C, Integrity, asks psychologists to promote accuracy, honesty and truthfulness. Knowingly misrepresenting results is dishonest, so it breaks integrity.

    What it isThe APA Ethics Code has five general principles: (A) Beneficence and non-maleficence, (B) Fidelity and responsibility, (C) Integrity, (D) Justice, (E) Respect for people's rights and dignity.

    Exam tipMaking up data is fabrication; presenting others' work as one's own is plagiarism; both are research misconduct.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) BeneficencePrinciple A, beneficence and non-maleficence, is about benefiting clients and avoiding harm, not about honesty in reporting.
    • (2) Fidelity and responsibilityPrinciple B, fidelity and responsibility, is about building trust and accepting professional responsibility towards clients and society.
    • (4) ConfidentialityConfidentiality is a specific standard about protecting client information, not one of the five general principles, and it is not about falsifying results.

  17. In multiple approach-avoidance conflict, one has to choose between which of the following?

    • 1Two goals possessing both negative and positive aspects
    • 2More than two goals; each having both positive and negative aspects
    • 3More than two goals; one having negative aspects and others having positive valences
    • 4More than two goals; at least one having positive aspects while others may have negative aspects
    ✓ Answer: (2) More than two goals; each having both positive and negative aspects
    Kurt Lewin, types of motivational conflict
    The concept

    Why this oneIn a multiple approach-avoidance conflict there are more than two options and each has both attractive and unattractive features, for example choosing between three job offers.

    Conflict typesApproach-approach: two attractive goals; avoidance-avoidance: two unpleasant ones; approach-avoidance: one goal with good and bad sides; double approach-avoidance: two goals, each with both sides.

    Exam tipKurt Lewin described the basic types. Approach-avoidance conflicts often cause vacillation, because avoidance rises faster than approach as the goal comes near.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Two goals possessing both negative and positive aspectsThis is a double approach-avoidance conflict: only two goals, each with good and bad sides.
    • (3) More than two goals; one having negative aspects and others having positive valencesWrongly gives only one goal negative features; in the multiple form, every option has both positive and negative sides.
    • (4) More than two goals; at least one having positive aspects while others may have negative aspectsAllows some goals to be purely positive or purely negative; in multiple approach-avoidance every option has both sides.

  18. Match List I with List II. List I: (A) Cancer that originates in the blood-forming cells such as stem cells (B) Cancer of the epithelial tissue, cells that line the outer and inner surface of the body such as skin, stomach etc. (C) Cancer that arises from the cells in connective tissue such as bone, muscles (D) Cancer of the sympathetic system which is one of the rare types of cancer. List II: (I) Carcinoma (II) Sarcoma (III) Leukaemia (IV) Lymphoma. Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IV
    • 2A-I, B-II, C-IV, D-III
    • 3A-IV, B-II, C-III, D-I
    • 4A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV
    ✓ Best answer: (4) A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV
    Cancer types by tissue of origin
    The concept

    Why this oneAs printed, no option is fully right; option (4) keeps D-IV (lymphoma) and shares pairs with every other option, so it is the most likely intended answer.

    Correct pairs(A) Blood-forming cells = (III) Leukaemia; (B) Epithelial tissue = (I) Carcinoma; (C) Connective tissue such as bone and muscle = (II) Sarcoma; (D) Lymphatic system ('sympathetic' is a copying slip) = (IV) Lymphoma.

    Exam tipCarcinomas are by far the most common type. Hooks: sarc = flesh (Greek sarx), leuk = white (white blood cells), lymph = the lymphatic system.

    Not certainAs printed no option fits the true pairs A-III, B-I, C-II, D-IV and D says 'sympathetic' for lymphatic; option 4 fits if the lists were reordered in copying, and option 1 (also only D right) is the runner-up.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IVMatches D correctly but gives blood-forming cells to sarcoma and epithelium to leukaemia; sarcoma arises in connective tissue such as bone and muscle.
    • (2) A-I, B-II, C-IV, D-IIIGets every pair wrong as printed: it calls the connective tissue cancer lymphoma and the lymphatic cancer leukaemia.
    • (3) A-IV, B-II, C-III, D-IGets every pair wrong as printed: it calls the blood-forming cancer lymphoma and the lymphatic cancer carcinoma, which is a cancer of epithelial tissue.

  19. Which of the following characterise the hardy person? (A) He is high on sense of commitment (B) He is more likely to act to 'cover-up' (C) He doesn't avoid problems, yet he is often angry (D) He is always under the control of his life and events

    • 1A and C only
    • 2A and D only
    • 3A and B only
    • 4A, B and D only
    ✓ Answer: (2) A and D only
    Suzanne Kobasa, hardiness (commitment, control, challenge)
    The concept

    Why this oneSuzanne Kobasa found that hardy people combine commitment, a sense of control over life events, and seeing change as a challenge. A is commitment; D, read as being in control of one's life, is control.

    What it isHardiness buffers stress: highly stressed executives high in hardiness fell ill less often. Hardy people use active, transformational coping instead of avoiding or denying problems.

    Exam tipRemember the three Cs: commitment, control, challenge. Hostility and anger belong to the toxic core of the Type A pattern, not to hardiness.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A and C onlyStatement C is false: hardy people face problems, but frequent anger and hostility mark the Type A pattern, which raises heart disease risk.
    • (3) A and B onlyStatement B is false: covering up or denying problems is avoidant coping, the opposite of the active coping hardy people use.
    • (4) A, B and D onlyCommitment and control fit, but statement B does not: hardy people face and transform problems rather than covering them up.

  20. A researcher is studying the group process of software startup developers by staying with them for six months. Which of the following is used by her?

    • 1Grounded theory
    • 2Ethnography
    • 3Narrative analysis
    • 4Phenomenological research
    ✓ Answer: (2) Ethnography
    Ethnography, John Creswell's five qualitative approaches
    The concept

    Why this oneEthnography studies the shared patterns of a culture-sharing group through long fieldwork, living with and observing the group. Staying six months with a startup team to study its group process fits exactly.

    How it worksThe main method is participant observation, backed by interviews and field notes. It grew out of anthropological fieldwork.

    Exam tipJohn Creswell's five approaches: narrative (one person's story), phenomenology (lived experience), grounded theory (build a theory), ethnography (a culture-sharing group), case study (a bounded case).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Grounded theoryGrounded theory (Barney Glaser and Anselm Strauss) aims to build a new theory from data through coding and constant comparison, not to describe a group's culture.
    • (3) Narrative analysisNarrative analysis studies the life stories of one or a few individuals and retells them in time order.
    • (4) Phenomenological researchPhenomenological research describes the shared lived experience of a phenomenon, such as grief, across several people, mainly through in-depth interviews.

  21. What do you call the neurological procedure involving the implantation of a medical device under the skin in the chest that sends electrical impulses to electrodes surgically placed in a specific location in the brain?

    • 1Deep Brain Stimulation (DBS)
    • 2Repetitive Transcranial Magnetic Stimulation (rTMS)
    • 3Electroconvulsive Therapy (ECT)
    • 4Vagus Nerve Stimulation (VNS)
    ✓ Answer: (1) Deep Brain Stimulation (DBS)
    Deep brain stimulation (DBS)
    The concept

    Why this oneIn deep brain stimulation a pulse generator, like a pacemaker, is placed under the skin of the chest and wired to electrodes implanted deep in the brain.

    UsesIt is used for Parkinson's disease, essential tremor and dystonia, and is being studied for severe, treatment-resistant OCD and depression. A common target is the subthalamic nucleus.

    Exam tipChest device + electrodes in the brain = DBS; chest device + wire to a neck nerve = VNS; magnetic coil on the scalp = rTMS.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Repetitive Transcranial Magnetic Stimulation (rTMS)rTMS is non-invasive: a magnetic coil held against the scalp induces currents in the cortex, and nothing is implanted.
    • (3) Electroconvulsive Therapy (ECT)ECT passes a brief current through scalp electrodes under anaesthesia to cause a controlled seizure; nothing is implanted.
    • (4) Vagus Nerve Stimulation (VNS)VNS also uses a chest pulse generator, but its wire goes to the vagus nerve in the neck, not to electrodes inside the brain.

  22. According to Lazarus, what comes between the stimulus and the emotional response to that stimulus to mediate?

    • 1Physiological reactions
    • 2Labeling
    • 3Social and situational cues
    • 4Cognitive appraisal
    ✓ Answer: (4) Cognitive appraisal
    Richard Lazarus, cognitive appraisal theory
    The concept

    Why this oneRichard Lazarus held that emotion depends on how we judge an event. This cognitive appraisal sits between the stimulus and the emotional response.

    How it worksPrimary appraisal asks whether the event is relevant, harmful or threatening; secondary appraisal asks whether I can cope. Reappraisal updates the judgement.

    Exam tipLazarus debated Robert Zajonc, who claimed that feeling can come before thinking (affective primacy).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Physiological reactionsPhysiological reactions come first in the James-Lange theory, where emotion is the perception of bodily changes.
    • (2) LabelingLabelling of arousal is the central step in Stanley Schachter and Jerome Singer's two-factor theory.
    • (3) Social and situational cuesIn the two-factor theory, Schachter and Singer said people use social and situational cues to label unexplained arousal; Lazarus stressed appraisal of the event's meaning instead.

  23. Several types of heuristics have been identified by researchers. Which of these are included in it? (A) Lexicographic strategy (B) Satisficing strategy (C) Mental set (D) Elimination by aspects

    • 1A, B and C only
    • 2A, B and D only
    • 3A, C and D only
    • 4B, C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (2) A, B and D only
    Decision heuristics: Herbert Simon, Amos Tversky
    The concept

    Why this oneThe lexicographic strategy, satisficing and elimination by aspects are short-cut decision rules, that is, heuristics. Mental set is a problem-solving block, not a decision heuristic.

    How each worksLexicographic: choose the option that is best on the single most important attribute; satisficing (Herbert Simon): take the first option that is good enough; elimination by aspects (Amos Tversky): drop options that fail on one attribute at a time.

    Exam tipMental set is shown in Abraham Luchins' water jar problems: people keep using a method that worked before even when a simpler one exists.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, B and C onlyIncludes mental set, a fixed way of approaching problems that blocks new solutions, and leaves out elimination by aspects.
    • (3) A, C and D onlyLeaves out satisficing, Herbert Simon's rule of taking the first good-enough option, and wrongly includes mental set.
    • (4) B, C and D onlyLeaves out the lexicographic strategy and wrongly includes mental set, which is a barrier to problem solving.

  24. Which one of the following is the correct description of Old Fashioned Racism?

    • 1A contemporary form of antagonism towards a racial group based on prejudice and values rather than on self-interest
    • 2Beliefs in racial superiority, segregation and formal discrimination
    • 3Attitude towards a racial group, combining egalitarian social values and negative emotion resulting in avoidance of that group
    • 4Well learned sets of association that can be automatically activated, uncontrollable and unintentional
    ✓ Answer: (2) Beliefs in racial superiority, segregation and formal discrimination
    John McConahay, old-fashioned versus modern racism
    The concept

    Why this oneOld-fashioned racism is open, blatant prejudice: beliefs in racial superiority, support for segregation and formal, legal discrimination.

    How it differsAfter the civil rights era open racism became socially unacceptable and gave way to subtler forms such as modern racism, measured by John McConahay's Modern Racism Scale.

    Exam tipBlatant = old-fashioned; values not self-interest = symbolic; egalitarian values plus avoidance = aversive; automatic associations = implicit.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A contemporary form of antagonism towards a racial group based on prejudice and values rather than on self-interestThis describes symbolic racism (David Sears), a modern form rooted in values such as individualism rather than personal self-interest.
    • (3) Attitude towards a racial group, combining egalitarian social values and negative emotion resulting in avoidance of that groupThis describes aversive racism (Samuel Gaertner and John Dovidio): egalitarian beliefs plus hidden negative feelings that lead to avoidance.
    • (4) Well learned sets of association that can be automatically activated, uncontrollable and unintentionalThis describes implicit prejudice or stereotypes: automatic associations, measured with tools like the Implicit Association Test.

  25. Match List I with List II. List I (Description): (A) A laboratory device for testing depth in infants and young animals (B) Refers to the differences in two eyes' location of corner angles (C) Refers to two points which fall on non-corresponding retinal positions one nearer and the other farther (D) Perceptional adaptability to adjust to an artificially displaced visual field. List II (Concept): (I) Perceptual adaptation (II) Horizontal disparity (III) Vertical disparity (IV) Visual cliff. Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV
    • 2A-II, B-III, C-IV, D-I
    • 3A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II
    • 4A-IV, B-III, C-II, D-I
    ✓ Answer: (4) A-IV, B-III, C-II, D-I
    Eleanor Gibson and Richard Walk, visual cliff; binocular disparity
    The concept

    Why this oneThe visual cliff tests depth perception in infants and perceptual adaptation is adjusting to a displaced visual field; only option (4) has both. B and C are the two kinds of binocular disparity.

    Correct pairs(A) Depth test for infants = (IV) Visual cliff; (B) Different positions of corners and angles in the two eyes = (III) Vertical disparity; (C) Points on non-corresponding retinal positions, nearer or farther = (II) Horizontal disparity; (D) Adjusting to a displaced field = (I) Perceptual adaptation.

    How it worksEleanor Gibson and Richard Walk found most crawling infants would not cross the 'deep' side of the cliff. Horizontal disparity is the main cue for stereopsis, and people wearing prism goggles that shift the visual field soon adapt their reaching.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IVPuts perceptual adaptation on the infant depth test and the visual cliff on the displaced field; the visual cliff is Gibson and Walk's infant depth test.
    • (2) A-II, B-III, C-IV, D-IGets D right but gives the infant depth device to horizontal disparity and the retinal-position description to the visual cliff.
    • (3) A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-IIGives the infant depth test to vertical disparity and the displaced field to horizontal disparity; these are the visual cliff and perceptual adaptation.

  26. Which one of the following branches of philosophy of science deals with the question of existence?

    • 1Post-positivism
    • 2Structuralism
    • 3Ontology
    • 4Scientific realism
    ✓ Answer: (3) Ontology
    Ontology, the study of being
    The concept

    Why this oneOntology is the branch of philosophy that asks what exists and what kinds of things are real. In science it asks which entities (atoms, minds, traits) truly exist.

    Three questionsOntology asks "what is there?"; epistemology asks "how do we know it?"; methodology asks "how do we study it?".

    Exam tipResearch paradigms are compared on these three. Positivism assumes one real world (realist ontology); constructivism assumes reality is socially built (relativist ontology).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Post-positivismA research paradigm holding that reality exists but can only be known imperfectly, so claims are probabilistic and open to falsification; it is a position, not the branch about existence.
    • (2) StructuralismA school of thought that breaks things into basic elements or underlying structures (Edward Titchener in psychology), not the philosophical study of existence.
    • (4) Scientific realismA specific position within the debate, not a branch: it holds that the unobservable entities of successful theories, such as electrons or genes, really exist.

  27. Which of the following is the correct response pattern for the semantic differential test to measure a trait 'active'?

    • 1I am active: SA A N D SD
    • 2I am active: True/False
    • 3Choose one: Active-Trusting
    • 4Active...Neutral...Passive
    ✓ Answer: (4) Active...Neutral...Passive
    Charles Osgood, semantic differential (1957)
    The concept

    Why this oneCharles Osgood's semantic differential rates a concept on a line between two bipolar adjectives, such as active and passive, usually with seven steps and a neutral middle.

    How it worksRatings group into three factors: evaluation (good to bad), potency (strong to weak) and activity (active to passive). "Active versus passive" is an activity scale.

    Exam tipOpposite adjectives with steps between = Osgood; strongly agree to strongly disagree = Likert; true or false = inventory format such as the MMPI.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) I am active: SA A N D SDThis is the Likert (summated ratings) format: agreement with a statement on five steps from strongly agree to strongly disagree.
    • (2) I am active: True/FalseA dichotomous true/false item format, as used in inventories such as the MMPI, not a scale between two opposite adjectives.
    • (3) Choose one: Active-TrustingActive and trusting are not opposites, so this is a forced choice between two unrelated traits, not a bipolar semantic scale.

  28. Which approach focussed on the nature of a task and its influence on a person's ability in solving problems?

    • 1Associationist approach
    • 2Information processing approach
    • 3Gestalt approach
    • 4Behaviourist approach
    ✓ Best answer: (2) Information processing approach
    Allen Newell and Herbert Simon, information processing approach
    The concept

    Why this oneAllen Newell and Herbert Simon analysed the task environment: the structure of the task sets what the solver must represent and which moves are possible.

    How it worksThe solver builds a problem space (start state, goal state, operators) and searches it with heuristics such as means-end analysis, limited by working memory capacity.

    Exam tipAssociationist = trial and error, habit strength; Gestalt = restructuring and insight; information processing = task analysis, problem space and computer simulation (General Problem Solver).

    Not certainGestalt psychologists also stressed how the structure of a problem shapes its solution (restructuring, functional fixedness), so (3) is the runner-up.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Associationist approachExplains problem solving as trial and error that strengthens stimulus-response bonds (Edward Thorndike), so it focuses on learned habits rather than the structure of the task.
    • (3) Gestalt approachWolfgang Köhler, Max Wertheimer and Karl Duncker stressed perceptual restructuring, sudden insight and blocks such as functional fixedness, not a formal analysis of the task.
    • (4) Behaviourist approachTreats problem solving as responses shaped by reinforcement history and ignores how the solver mentally represents the task.

  29. Match List I with List II. List I: (A) Pratibha (B) Prajna (C) Vak (D) Bhavas. List II: (I) Samkhya philosophy (II) Nyaya Vaisesika (III) Tantrik tradition (IV) Patanjali Yoga system. Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1A-I, B-II, C-IV, D-III
    • 2A-II, B-IV, C-I, D-III
    • 3A-II, B-IV, C-III, D-I
    • 4A-I, B-IV, C-II, D-III
    ✓ Answer: (3) A-II, B-IV, C-III, D-I
    Classical Indian philosophical systems (darshanas) and Tantra
    The concept

    Correct pairs(A) Pratibha = (II) Nyaya Vaisesika; (B) Prajna = (IV) Patanjali Yoga system; (C) Vak = (III) Tantrik tradition; (D) Bhavas = (I) Samkhya philosophy.

    Pratibha and prajnaPratibha is a flash of intuitive knowledge that arises without the senses, discussed in Nyaya Vaisesika as the knowledge of seers. Prajna in the Yoga Sutras is insight wisdom reached in samadhi.

    Vak and bhavasIn Tantra, vak (speech) unfolds in levels from para to vaikhari (spoken word). In Samkhya, the eight bhavas are dispositions of buddhi: virtue, knowledge, detachment, power and their opposites.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A-I, B-II, C-IV, D-IIIEvery pair is wrong: pratibha belongs to Nyaya Vaisesika, prajna to Yoga, vak to the Tantrik tradition and the bhavas to Samkhya.
    • (2) A-II, B-IV, C-I, D-IIIGets pratibha and prajna right but swaps the last two: vak belongs to the Tantrik tradition and the bhavas to Samkhya.
    • (4) A-I, B-IV, C-II, D-IIIOnly prajna (Yoga) is right; pratibha is Nyaya Vaisesika, vak is Tantrik and the bhavas are Samkhya.

  30. In the famous experiment by Schachter and Singer involving epinephrine, what was found to govern the emotions?

    • 1Perceptual cues only
    • 2Social appraisal only
    • 3Both cognitive appraisal and physiological arousal
    • 4Both cognitive and social appraisal
    ✓ Answer: (3) Both cognitive appraisal and physiological arousal
    Stanley Schachter and Jerome Singer, two-factor theory (1962)
    The concept

    Why this oneIn Stanley Schachter and Jerome Singer's two-factor theory, emotion needs both physiological arousal and a cognitive label (appraisal) explaining why one is aroused.

    The experimentParticipants got epinephrine. Those not told about its side effects took on the mood of a euphoric or angry stooge, labelling their arousal from the situation; informed participants blamed the drug and copied less.

    Exam tipJames-Lange = bodily change first, then emotion; Cannon-Bard = both at once via the thalamus; Schachter-Singer = arousal plus label.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Perceptual cues onlyCues from the situation guided the labelling, but on their own they did not produce emotion; arousal from the drug was also needed.
    • (2) Social appraisal onlyThe stooge's behaviour supplied the cue for labelling, but the theory also requires physiological arousal, so social appraisal alone is not enough.
    • (4) Both cognitive and social appraisalLeaves out the bodily factor; the core claim is that arousal must be present and then explained cognitively.

  31. Who among the following has laid emphasis on examining the relationship of culturally specific practices with development?

    • 1Piaget
    • 2Fodor
    • 3Vygotsky
    • 4Kohlberg
    ✓ Answer: (3) Vygotsky
    Lev Vygotsky, sociocultural theory
    The concept

    Why this oneLev Vygotsky argued that development cannot be separated from its cultural and social context: the tools, language and practices of a culture shape how children think.

    Key ideasChildren learn through interaction with more skilled people within the zone of proximal development, supported by scaffolding. Higher mental functions appear first between people, then within the child.

    Exam tipPiaget = child as lone scientist passing universal stages; Vygotsky = child as apprentice whose path depends on culture.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) PiagetJean Piaget proposed universal stages driven by the child's own exploration and gave culture only a minor role.
    • (2) FodorJerry Fodor is a philosopher of mind known for the modularity of mind and the language of thought, not cultural development.
    • (4) KohlbergLawrence Kohlberg proposed universal stages of moral reasoning and has been criticised for underplaying cultural variation.

  32. Mr A is very good at discriminating between progressive and regressive ways of behaviour. His choices are clearly perceived by him and adequately symbolised. According to Rogers, these are the characteristics of which of the following? (A) Individual tendency towards 'growth' (B) Tendency to 'self actualise' (C) His constructive creativity (D) His self-affirmation

    • 1A and C only
    • 2A, C and D only
    • 3A and B only
    • 4B only
    ✓ Answer: (3) A and B only
    Carl Rogers, actualising tendency
    The concept

    Why this oneCarl Rogers held that the forward-moving growth tendency, the drive to self-actualise, works only when choices are clearly perceived and adequately symbolised. A person must tell progressive from regressive behaviour to actualise.

    What it isThe actualising tendency is the one master motive to maintain and enhance the organism, moving it towards greater differentiation, autonomy and maturity.
