    Exam tipDenied or distorted experience (incongruence) blurs this choice; congruence lets the person see and take the growth path.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A and C onlyIncludes (C) and drops (B); Rogers ties clear symbolisation of choices to the tendency to self-actualise, not to creativity as such.
    • (2) A, C and D onlyAdds (C) and (D) and drops (B); creativity and self-affirmation are not what Rogers links to telling progressive from regressive behaviour.
    • (4) B onlySelf-actualising is right, but Rogers describes it as the forward-moving growth tendency, so (A) belongs with it.

  33. For an intelligence test in an educational setting, which of the following difficulty value shows maximum discrimination?

    • 10.8
    • 20.6
    • 30.5
    • 40.3
    ✓ Answer: (3) 0.5
    Item difficulty index and discrimination
    The concept

    Why this oneAn item passed by 50% (p = 0.5) splits the group into two equal halves, giving the largest number of pass-fail contrasts between people, so it discriminates most.

    How it worksItem difficulty p is the proportion who answer correctly. At p = 1.0 or 0 everyone gets the same result, so the item tells you nothing about differences.

    Exam tipAnne Anastasi: for maximum differentiation choose items near p = 0.5. With guessing on multiple choice the optimum rises to about (1 + chance)/2, for example 0.625 for four options.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 0.8An easy item: 80% pass, so it only separates the weakest fifth from the rest and makes fewer distinctions between people.
    • (2) 0.6Close to the optimum and near the value adjusted for guessing on four-option items, but without that adjustment 0.5 discriminates more.
    • (4) 0.3A hard item: only 30% pass, so most people fail it and it separates fewer pairs of test takers.

  34. Making decisions by the use of mental short cuts that focus on one aspect of a problem, while ignoring others refers to which one of the following?

    • 1Framing
    • 2Belief perseverance
    • 3Heuristics
    • 4Mental set
    ✓ Answer: (3) Heuristics
    Amos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman, heuristics
    The concept

    Why this oneHeuristics are mental shortcuts or rules of thumb that give fast answers by using only part of the information, so they can bias decisions.

    ExamplesAmos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman described availability (judging frequency by how easily examples come to mind), representativeness (judging by resemblance to a prototype) and anchoring.

    Exam tipHeuristic = quick but error-prone; algorithm = step-by-step method that guarantees a solution but is slow.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) FramingThe effect of how options are worded (as gains or as losses) on the choice people make, shown by Tversky and Kahneman.
    • (2) Belief perseveranceClinging to a belief even after the evidence for it has been discredited.
    • (4) Mental setA tendency to keep using a strategy that worked before even when a better one exists, as in Abraham Luchins' water jar problems.

  35. Which of the following are the parts of mental logic theories of reasoning? (A) A set of inference schemas (B) Accumulation of concepts (C) A reasoning program (D) A set of pragmatic principles

    • 1A, B and C only
    • 2A, B and D only
    • 3A, C and D only
    • 4B, C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (3) A, C and D only
    Martin Braine and David O'Brien, mental logic theory
    The concept

    Why this oneMental logic theories, such as that of Martin Braine and David O'Brien, have three parts: a set of inference schemas, a reasoning program that applies them, and pragmatic principles that shape how premises are understood.

    How it worksPeople are assumed to carry abstract rules such as modus ponens in the head; the program chains them to reach conclusions, while pragmatic principles handle context and wording.

    Exam tipMental logic (Braine; Lance Rips' PSYCOP) = rules of inference; mental models (Philip Johnson-Laird) = imagined scenarios of the premises.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, B and C onlyIncludes (B) and drops (D); accumulation of concepts is not a component, while pragmatic principles are.
    • (2) A, B and D onlyIncludes (B) and drops (C); the reasoning program that applies the schemas is a core part of the theory.
    • (4) B, C and D onlyDrops (A), the inference schemas that are the heart of any mental logic theory, and adds (B), which is not a part.

  36. In which sequence, according to Piaget, are the following cognitive milestones achieved? (A) Object permanence (B) Reversibility (C) Abstraction (D) Centration

    • 1A, B, C, D
    • 2C, A, B, D
    • 3D, A, B, C
    • 4A, D, B, C
    ✓ Answer: (4) A, D, B, C
    Jean Piaget, stages of cognitive development
    The concept

    Correct order(A) Object permanence, sensorimotor (0 to 2 years) → (D) Centration, preoperational (2 to 7) → (B) Reversibility, concrete operational (7 to 11) → (C) Abstraction, formal operational (11 onwards).

    What each meansObject permanence: knowing objects exist when unseen. Centration: attending to one feature only, such as water height. Reversibility: mentally undoing an action, which allows conservation. Abstraction: reasoning about hypothetical ideas.

    Exam tipJean Piaget saw centration as a limit of preoperational thought, overcome by decentration and reversibility in the concrete stage.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, B, C, DPuts reversibility before centration; centration is a preoperational limit that reversibility later overcomes.
    • (2) C, A, B, DStarts with abstraction, the last formal operational achievement, and ends with centration, a preoperational feature.
    • (3) D, A, B, CPuts centration before object permanence, but object permanence develops in infancy, before preoperational thought.

  37. Arrange the following in sequence as per the Excitation Transfer Theory of Aggression. (A) Frustration (B) Residual Arousal (C) Arousing Events (D) Increased Arousal (E) Arousal attributed to Frustration

    • 1A, C, B, D, E
    • 2D, B, C, A, E
    • 3C, D, B, A, E
    • 4A, C, D, B, E
    ✓ Answer: (3) C, D, B, A, E
    Dolf Zillmann, excitation transfer theory
    The concept

    Correct order(C) Arousing event, such as exercise → (D) Increased arousal → (B) Residual arousal that lingers → (A) A new frustration → (E) Leftover arousal attributed to the frustration, which intensifies aggression.

    How it worksDolf Zillmann argued that physiological arousal fades slowly, so arousal from one source can transfer to a later situation. If the person does not notice it, they misattribute it to the provocation.

    Exam tipThe effect is strongest when the person is unaware of the leftover arousal; it builds on Schachter's two-factor idea of labelling arousal.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, C, B, D, EStarts with frustration, but the unrelated arousing event comes first; frustration is the later trigger onto which residual arousal is transferred.
    • (2) D, B, C, A, EBegins with increased arousal before the event that caused it and puts residual arousal before the arousing event.
    • (4) A, C, D, B, EStarts with frustration; in the theory the earlier arousing event comes first, and frustration arrives only after arousal has become residual.

  38. Arrange the stages of personality development in the theory given by Rollo May. (A) Innocence (B) Rebellion (C) Ordinary consciousness of self (D) Creative consciousness of self

    • 1A, C, B, D
    • 2A, B, C, D
    • 3B, C, A, D
    • 4B, C, D, A
    ✓ Answer: (2) A, B, C, D
    Rollo May, existential stages of development
    The concept

    Correct order(A) Innocence (infancy) → (B) Rebellion (childhood and adolescence) → (C) Ordinary consciousness of self → (D) Creative consciousness of self.

    What each meansInnocence: acting on instinct without self-awareness. Rebellion: wanting freedom without accepting its responsibility. Ordinary consciousness: learning from mistakes and living responsibly. Creative consciousness: rare moments of seeing beyond everyday limits.

    About MayRollo May, an American existential psychologist, treated these as forms of consciousness rather than strict age stages; few adults reach the creative level.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, C, B, DPuts ordinary consciousness of self before rebellion, but rebellion against parents and authority comes first, in childhood and adolescence.
    • (3) B, C, A, DStarts with rebellion, but the innocence of infancy comes before any wish for independence.
    • (4) B, C, D, APuts innocence last, but innocence is the first, infant stage.

  39. Which of the following internal roadblocks to effective listening is exemplified by the statement "We have never done it that way before"? (A) Resistance to change (B) Halo Effect (C) Stereotyping (D) Hearing only, not feeling

    • 1A only
    • 2A and B only
    • 3A, B and C only
    • 4A, B, C and D
    ✓ Answer: (1) A only
    Internal roadblocks to effective listening
    The concept

    Why this one"We have never done it that way before" shows resistance to change: the listener rejects a new idea because it differs from habit, without really hearing it.

    What it isInternal roadblocks are barriers inside the listener, such as fixed attitudes, bias and emotional reactions, that stop the message being understood.

    Exam tipHalo effect = judging the message by one liked or disliked trait of the speaker; stereotyping = judging it by the speaker's group.

    Why the other options are not the answer
    • (2) A and B onlyAdds the halo effect, letting one overall impression of the speaker colour the whole message; the statement is about an unfamiliar idea, not the speaker.
    • (3) A, B and C onlyAdds the halo effect and stereotyping, which both judge the speaker; the statement rejects an idea only because it is new.
    • (4) A, B, C and DAlso adds hearing only, not feeling (taking in words but missing the speaker's emotions), which the statement does not show.

  40. Which of the following are associated with the Path in Sufism? (A) Establishing self and assertion (B) Repentance (C) Renunciation (D) Poverty (E) Annihilation

    • 1B, C and E only
    • 2A, B and D only
    • 3A, B, C and D only
    • 4B, C, D and E only
    ✓ Answer: (4) B, C, D and E only
    Sufi tariqa: stations (maqamat) of the Path
    The concept

    Why this oneThe Sufi Path (tariqa) passes through stations (maqamat) such as repentance (tawba), renunciation (zuhd), poverty (faqr), patience and trust in God, and ends in annihilation (fana) of the ego in God.

    Why not (A)The Path aims to dissolve the lower self (nafs), so establishing and asserting the self is the opposite of the Sufi goal.

    Exam tipFana (passing away of self) is followed by baqa (abiding in God). Maqamat are stations earned by effort; ahwal are states given by God.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) B, C and E onlyRepentance, renunciation and annihilation fit, but it leaves out poverty (faqr), a classic station of the Path.
    • (2) A, B and D onlyIncludes self-assertion, the opposite of the Path's aim of effacing the ego, and leaves out renunciation and annihilation.
    • (3) A, B, C and D onlyIncludes self-assertion and leaves out annihilation (fana), the final goal of the Path.

  41. A group of sixty newly diagnosed diabetic patients were tested for anxiety using a standardized inventory. They were retested for anxiety again after some months using the same inventory. The mean difference in anxiety was evaluated by a suitable t-test. The degrees of freedom associated with the t-test would be

    • 1120
    • 2118
    • 359
    • 458
    ✓ Answer: (3) 59
    Paired samples t-test, df = n - 1
    The concept

    Why this oneThe same 60 patients are measured twice, so this is a paired (dependent) t-test. It works on 60 difference scores, giving df = n - 1 = 60 - 1 = 59.

    How it worksEach patient's retest score is subtracted from the first score; t = mean difference ÷ (SD of differences ÷ √n), read with n - 1 degrees of freedom.

    Exam tipTwo independent groups: df = n1 + n2 - 2. Paired or one-sample t: df = n - 1. Testing a correlation: df = n - 2.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 120The total number of scores (60 × 2), not degrees of freedom; it wrongly treats the retest as 60 new people.
    • (2) 118This is n1 + n2 - 2 for two independent groups of 60, but here one group is retested.
    • (4) 58Subtracts 2 from a single group; df = n - 2 applies when testing a correlation, not a paired t-test.

  42. Which of the following is true about Hypovolemia? (A) It causes thirst (B) It increases the blood pressure (C) Angiotensin is released during this condition (D) The volume of blood increases (E) Blood pressure reduces

    • 1A, B and C only
    • 2A, C and E only
    • 3A, D and E only
    • 4B, C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (2) A, C and E only
    Volumetric thirst and the renin-angiotensin system
    The concept

    Why this oneHypovolemia is low blood volume, as after bleeding or diarrhoea. Blood pressure falls, the kidneys release renin, which forms angiotensin II, and this triggers volumetric thirst. So (A), (C) and (E) are true.

    Why (B), (D) failBoth are reversed: in hypovolemia blood volume has dropped, so blood pressure falls rather than rises.

    How it worksAngiotensin II constricts blood vessels, releases aldosterone to retain sodium and acts on the subfornical organ to cause thirst. Baroreceptors in the heart and vessels also detect the drop.

    Exam tipOsmotic thirst = cells lose water to salty extracellular fluid; volumetric thirst = loss of blood volume.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, B and C onlyIncludes (B), but low blood volume lowers blood pressure; it does not raise it.
    • (3) A, D and E onlyIncludes (D), but hypovolemia means blood volume has fallen, not increased.
    • (4) B, C and D onlyIncludes (B) and (D), both reversed: blood pressure and blood volume drop in hypovolemia.

  43. Testing the scientific hypothesis is impossible due to the need of auxiliary assumptions. What is this associated with?

    • 1Occam's razor
    • 2Logical empiricism
    • 3Duhem-Quine thesis
    • 4Incommensurability
    ✓ Answer: (3) Duhem-Quine thesis
    Pierre Duhem and Willard Van Orman Quine, confirmation holism
    The concept

    Why this oneThe Duhem-Quine thesis (Pierre Duhem, Willard Van Orman Quine) says a hypothesis is never tested alone but together with auxiliary assumptions about instruments, conditions and background theory.

    Why it mattersWhen a prediction fails, logic cannot tell which part is wrong, so no single experiment decisively refutes a hypothesis. This is a problem for Karl Popper's falsificationism.

    Exam tipAlso called confirmation holism: theories face evidence as a whole network, not one statement at a time.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Occam's razorThe principle of parsimony, linked to William of Ockham: prefer the simplest explanation that fits the facts.
    • (2) Logical empiricismThe Vienna Circle view that meaningful statements must be verifiable by observation, which assumed hypotheses could be checked fairly directly.
    • (4) IncommensurabilityThomas Kuhn and Paul Feyerabend's idea that rival paradigms lack a common measure, so they cannot be compared point by point.

  44. Which one of the following attitude scaling methods is especially unsuitable for scaling a large number of attitude statements?

    • 1Paired comparison
    • 2Equal appearing intervals
    • 3Summated ratings
    • 4Successive intervals
    ✓ Answer: (1) Paired comparison
    Louis Thurstone, method of paired comparisons
    The concept

    Why this oneIn paired comparison, every statement is compared with every other, so n statements need n(n - 1)/2 judgements: 20 statements need 190 pairs and 50 need 1,225, which is unmanageable.

    How it worksJudges pick the more favourable statement in each pair; the proportions are turned into scale values using Louis Thurstone's law of comparative judgement.

    Exam tipSorting methods (equal appearing and successive intervals) cope with large item pools; Rensis Likert's summated ratings are the quickest to build.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Equal appearing intervalsThurstone's sorting method: judges place each statement once into one of 11 ordered piles, so it handles many statements easily.
    • (3) Summated ratingsRensis Likert's method: each statement is rated once on agree to disagree steps, so large item pools are no problem.
    • (4) Successive intervalsAlso sorts each statement once into ordered categories without pairing, so it suits large sets of statements.

  45. Which one of the following personality test/s employ/s a 'Lie' scale? (A) Rotter's Locus of control scale (B) Eysenck Personality Profiler (C) Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory-2 (D) Edwards Personal Preference Schedule

    • 1C only
    • 2B and C only
    • 3A, B and D only
    • 4B, C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (2) B and C only
    Lie scales as validity checks in inventories
    The concept

    Why this oneThe MMPI-2 has the L (Lie) scale among its validity scales. Hans Eysenck's Eysenck Personality Profiler, like the EPQ, includes a lie scale to catch people presenting themselves too favourably.

    How it worksLie items describe small faults almost everyone has ("I sometimes get angry"); denying many of them suggests the person is faking good.

    Why not (A), (D)Julian Rotter's I-E scale uses filler items to hide its purpose. Allen Edwards' EPPS controls social desirability with matched forced-choice pairs and a consistency score, not a lie scale.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) C onlyThe MMPI-2 does have the L scale, but the Eysenck Personality Profiler also has a lie scale, so (C) alone is incomplete.
    • (3) A, B and D onlyIncludes Rotter's I-E scale and the EPPS, neither of which has a lie scale, and leaves out the MMPI-2.
    • (4) B, C and D onlyIncludes the EPPS, which handles faking with matched forced-choice pairs and a consistency score rather than a lie scale.

  46. Which of the following statements are true about Unity Thema? (A) It is a compound of interrelated needs linked to press (B) It is a compound of conflicting needs linked to press (C) It operates largely as an unconscious force (D) It operates largely as a conscious force

    • 1A, B and C only
    • 2A and C only
    • 3A and D only
    • 4B and D only
    ✓ Answer: (1) A, B and C only
    Henry Murray, unity thema
    The concept

    Why this oneHenry Murray defined the unity thema as a compound of interrelated, collaborating or conflicting dominant needs linked to press met in early childhood, so (A) and (B) are both true.

    How it worksThe thema forms from a gratifying or traumatic childhood experience and then shapes much of later behaviour. It operates largely as an unconscious force, so (C) is true and (D) false.

    Exam tipNeed plus press = thema; the unity thema is the single underlying pattern that gives a life its coherence.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) A and C onlyLeaves out (B); Murray's definition covers collaborating or conflicting needs, so conflict among needs is part of the unity thema.
    • (3) A and D onlyIncludes (D), but Murray said the unity thema works largely outside awareness, not as a conscious force.
    • (4) B and D onlyIncludes (D) and drops (A) and (C); the thema is largely unconscious and its needs are interrelated.

  47. Match List I with List II. List I (Description): (A) A reinforcement that reinforces a response at unpredictable time intervals (B) A reinforcement that reinforces a response only after a specified time has lapsed (C) A reinforcement that reinforces a response after an unpredictable number of responses (D) A reinforcement that reinforces a response only after a specified number of responses. List II (Type of Reinforcement): (I) Fixed-ratio schedule (II) Variable-ratio schedule (III) Fixed-interval schedule (IV) Variable-interval schedule. Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV
    • 2A-II, B-III, C-IV, D-I
    • 3A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II
    • 4A-IV, B-III, C-II, D-I
    ✓ Answer: (4) A-IV, B-III, C-II, D-I
    B. F. Skinner, schedules of reinforcement
    The concept

    Correct pairs(A) Unpredictable time = (IV) variable-interval; (B) set time lapse = (III) fixed-interval; (C) unpredictable number of responses = (II) variable-ratio; (D) set number of responses = (I) fixed-ratio.

    How it worksIn B. F. Skinner's partial schedules, ratio depends on the number of responses and interval on time since the last reinforcer. Fixed = same each time; variable = changes around an average.

    Exam tipVariable-ratio (slot machines) gives the highest, steadiest rate and most resistance to extinction; fixed-interval gives a scallop, with responding speeding up as reward time nears.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IVEvery pair is wrong: it gives the time-based descriptions to ratio schedules and the response-count descriptions to interval schedules.
    • (2) A-II, B-III, C-IV, D-IGets (B) and (D) right but swaps (A) and (C): unpredictable time is variable-interval and an unpredictable number of responses is variable-ratio.
    • (3) A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-IIEvery pair is wrong; for example, a set number of responses is fixed-ratio, not variable-ratio, and a set time lapse is fixed-interval.

  48. Which of the following is the correct explanation of "An intolerance for weakness, a punitive attitude and submissive respect for their in-group authorities as reflected in their agreement"?

    • 1Social Dominance Orientation
    • 2Ethnocentrism
    • 3Conformity
    • 4Inequality
    ✓ Best answer: (2) Ethnocentrism
    Theodor Adorno et al., The Authoritarian Personality (1950)
    The concept

    Why this oneTheodor Adorno and colleagues found that people prejudiced against one minority were prejudiced against most outgroups (ethnocentrism). These ethnocentric people shared authoritarian tendencies: intolerance for weakness, a punitive attitude and submissive respect for in-group authorities.

    What it isEthnocentrism is belief in the superiority of one's own group with contempt for outgroups. The F scale measured the authoritarian traits behind it, with items like "Obedience and respect for authority are the most important virtues children should learn".

    Exam tipAuthoritarianism = submission to in-group authority plus hostility to outgroups; social dominance orientation = wanting one's group to dominate others.

    Not certainThe traits described are those of the authoritarian personality, which is not offered; social dominance orientation (1) is the nearest rival construct, but textbooks attach these traits to ethnocentric people.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Social Dominance OrientationJim Sidanius and Felicia Pratto's preference for group hierarchy, with one's own group on top; it is about dominance, not submission to in-group authority.
    • (3) ConformityChanging behaviour or belief to match a group, as in Solomon Asch's line studies; a general social influence process, not a prejudiced personality pattern.
    • (4) InequalityThe unequal spread of status and resources in a society; it describes social structure, not a set of personal attitudes.

  49. Vaibhasika and Sautrantika are associated with which of the following?

    • 1Theravada
    • 2Cetasikas
    • 3Madhyamikavada
    • 4Sarvastivadin
    ✓ Answer: (4) Sarvastivadin
    Sarvastivada schools of Buddhist Abhidharma
    The concept

    Why this oneVaibhasika and Sautrantika are the two schools of the Sarvastivada (Hinayana) tradition. Vaibhasika follows the Mahavibhasa commentary; Sautrantika relies on the sutras rather than the Abhidharma.

    Key ideasBoth are realist. Vaibhasikas hold that external objects are perceived directly (direct realism); Sautrantikas hold that they are only inferred from mental images (representationalism).

    Exam tipFour classic Buddhist schools: Vaibhasika and Sautrantika (Hinayana, realist); Yogacara (Mahayana, mind-only) and Madhyamika (Mahayana, emptiness).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) TheravadaThe surviving Pali school of Sri Lanka and South-East Asia, a Hinayana lineage separate from the Sarvastivada.
    • (2) CetasikasThe mental factors (such as attention, feeling, greed) listed in Theravada Abhidhamma psychology; a category of mind, not a school.
    • (3) MadhyamikavadaNagarjuna's Mahayana school of emptiness (shunyata), which criticised the realism of the Sarvastivadins.

  50. Adult neurons rely heavily on which of the following? (A) Glucose (B) Protein (C) Potassium (D) Sodium

    • 1A only
    • 2A and B only
    • 3C and D only
    • 4A, C and D only
    ✓ Best answer: (1) A only
    Neuronal energy metabolism: glucose dependence
    The concept

    Why this oneAdult neurons use glucose almost exclusively as fuel and store very little of it, so they need a steady supply from the blood. The brain uses about a fifth of the body's energy.

    How it worksGlucose is carried across the blood-brain barrier by transporter proteins; using it needs oxygen and thiamine (vitamin B1). Low blood sugar quickly impairs brain function.

    Exam tipIn starvation the brain can partly switch to ketone bodies, but in normal adult life glucose is its main fuel. Sodium and potassium are signalling ions, not fuel.

    Not certainNeurons also depend on sodium and potassium gradients for signalling, so (4) is defensible if the stem means more than fuel.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) A and B onlyNeurons use protein to build structures, enzymes and neurotransmitters, but not as their main energy source.
    • (3) C and D onlyThese ions create the resting potential and action potentials, but they are pumped, not used as fuel; the sodium-potassium pump itself runs on ATP made from glucose.
    • (4) A, C and D onlyGlucose is right, but sodium and potassium are needed for signalling rather than relied on for energy.

  51. Johnson-Laird (1995) has some contingencies in the scientific study of logic, which comprise: (A) Relational inferences (B) Propositional inferences (C) Focus gambling (D) Syllogisms

    • 1A, B and C only
    • 2A, B and D only
    • 3A, C and D only
    • 4B, C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (2) A, B and D only
    Philip Johnson-Laird, mental models theory of deduction
    The concept

    Why this onePhilip Johnson-Laird reviewed the study of deduction under three kinds of inference: relational inferences, propositional inferences and syllogisms. Focus gambling is not a form of logic at all.

    What they areRelational: 'A is taller than B, B is taller than C, so A is taller than C'. Propositional: built on if, or, and, not; syllogisms: two quantified premises ('All A are B') and a conclusion.

    How it worksIn his mental models theory, people build a mental picture of what the premises describe and read the conclusion off it. Problems that need more models are harder and cause more errors.

    Exam tipFocus gambling is a concept-attainment strategy from Jerome Bruner, Jacqueline Goodnow and George Austin (1956), alongside conservative focusing and scanning.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, B and C onlyIncludes focus gambling, a concept-attainment strategy from Jerome Bruner's work, and leaves out syllogisms, a core domain of deduction.
    • (3) A, C and D onlyDrops propositional inferences (reasoning with if, or, not), one of the three domains, and adds focus gambling, which is not a type of logical inference.
    • (4) B, C and D onlyDrops relational inferences such as transitive reasoning (A taller than B, B taller than C), and adds focus gambling, a concept-learning strategy.

  52. Which is true about coefficient alpha? (A) It is a lower bound of a test of parallel items (B) It is an upper bound of a test of parallel items (C) It is an average of all combinations of split-half reliabilities (D) It is independent of inter-item correlations

    • 1C only
    • 2A and B only
    • 3A and C only
    • 4C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (3) A and C only
    Lee Cronbach, coefficient alpha (1951)
    The concept

    Why this oneCoefficient alpha is a lower bound to a test's true reliability (A), and Lee Cronbach showed it equals the mean of all possible split-half coefficients (C).

    What it isAlpha estimates internal consistency, how far the items measure the same thing. It rises with the number of items and with the average inter-item correlation.

    Why a lower boundAlpha equals true reliability only when items are parallel or essentially tau-equivalent. When items differ, it tends to underestimate reliability, so it is treated as a floor.

    Exam tipKR-20 is the special case of alpha for items scored right or wrong (0 or 1).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) C onlyC is true, but A is true as well: alpha is the standard lower-bound estimate of reliability, so C alone is incomplete.
    • (2) A and B onlyB is false: alpha is a lower bound of reliability, not an upper bound, so A and B cannot both hold.
    • (4) C and D onlyD is false: alpha is computed from inter-item covariances, so it rises as the items correlate more highly with one another.

  53. Which of the following are considered in favour of scientific realism? (A) Corroboration (B) Empiricism (C) Miracle argument (D) Selective optimism

    • 1A and B only
    • 2B and D only
    • 3B and C only
    • 4A, C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (4) A, C and D only
    Hilary Putnam's no-miracles argument; selective realism
    The concept

    Why this oneCorroboration, the no-miracles argument and selective optimism are all moves realists make. Empiricism is the main anti-realist rival, so every option containing B fails.

    What realism claimsScientific realism holds that mature, successful theories are approximately true and that unobservable entities, such as electrons, really exist.

    The argumentsHilary Putnam: the success of science would be a miracle if its theories were not roughly true. Corroboration: independent methods agreeing on the same entity is unlikely by chance; selective optimism: trust the parts of past theories that did the predictive work.

    Exam tipBas van Fraassen's constructive empiricism says science aims only at empirical adequacy (truth about observables), so it argues against realism.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A and B onlyIncludes empiricism, the anti-realist view (for example Bas van Fraassen's constructive empiricism) that science need only be true to observables, and omits the no-miracles argument.
    • (2) B and D onlyIncludes empiricism, which argues against realism, and omits corroboration and the no-miracles argument, the best-known case for realism.
    • (3) B and C onlyThe no-miracles argument fits, but empiricism is the leading anti-realist position, not a defence of realism.

  54. In depth perception, what kind of images are used?

    • 1Uni-dimensional image
    • 2Two-dimensional images
    • 3Three-dimensional images
    • 4Four-dimensional images
    ✓ Answer: (2) Two-dimensional images
    Depth perception from two-dimensional retinal images
    The concept

    Why this oneThe image on each retina is flat, two-dimensional. Depth perception is the brain's ability to build a three-dimensional world from these 2D images.

    How it worksThe brain compares the two eyes' slightly different images through binocular cues (retinal disparity, convergence). It also uses monocular cues such as linear perspective, interposition, texture gradient and relative size.

    Exam tipThe third dimension is the output of perception, not the input: the input is two flat images, one per eye.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Uni-dimensional imageA line has only one dimension; the retina receives a full flat image with height and width, not a line.
    • (3) Three-dimensional imagesThis is what we perceive, not what the eye receives; the brain infers the third dimension from cues in flat retinal images.
    • (4) Four-dimensional imagesVision has no such image type; time is sometimes called the fourth dimension, while depth is the third spatial dimension, built from flat images.

  55. In the independent groups' design, selective subject loss occurs because of which of the following? (A) Subjects are lost differently across conditions (B) Subject characteristics are responsible for subject loss (C) Subjects are lost uniformly across conditions (D) Subject characteristics are independent of subject loss

    • 1A and B only
    • 2A and C only
    • 3B and D only
    • 4C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (1) A and B only
    John Shaughnessy and Eugene Zechmeister, Research Methods in Psychology
    The concept

    Why this oneSelective subject loss happens when participants drop out differently across conditions (A) and a subject characteristic linked to the outcome causes the dropout (B).

    Why it mattersRandom assignment made the groups equivalent, but losing, say, the least able people from a hard condition leaves groups that differ before the treatment acts. The independent variable is then confounded.

    ContrastMechanical subject loss (equipment failure, experimenter error) has nothing to do with the person, so it does not bias the groups.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) A and C onlyC contradicts A: loss spread evenly across conditions leaves the groups comparable, so it is not selective loss.
    • (3) B and D onlyD is the opposite of B: if subject characteristics were unrelated to dropping out, the loss would not bias the groups.
    • (4) C and D onlyBoth C and D describe loss that leaves the groups equivalent, which is harmless, unbiased loss, the opposite of selective loss.

  56. Immediately after an action potential, the cell does not produce another action potential due to

    • 1Absolute refractory period
    • 2Relative refractory period
    • 3Resting state
    • 4Active state
    ✓ Answer: (1) Absolute refractory period
    Absolute refractory period of the neuron
    The concept

    Why this oneDuring the absolute refractory period, about 1 to 2 milliseconds after a spike, no stimulus of any strength can trigger another action potential.

    How it worksVoltage-gated sodium channels are inactivated and cannot reopen until the membrane repolarises.

    What followsNext comes the relative refractory period, when only a stronger-than-normal stimulus can fire the cell because the membrane is briefly hyperpolarised.

    Exam tipRefractory periods make action potentials travel one way down the axon and set a ceiling on firing rate.

    Why the other options are not the answer
    • (2) Relative refractory periodHere a second spike is possible, but only with a stronger-than-normal stimulus, because the membrane is briefly hyperpolarised.
    • (3) Resting stateAt rest (about -70 mV) the neuron is ready to fire; firing is not blocked.
    • (4) Active stateNot a technical stage; the active phase is the action potential itself, not the period that follows it.

  57. Given below are two statements, one is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R. Assertion A: Schertzer and Stone (1974) specified that acceptance requires respect for the individual as a person of warmth. Reason R: Therapists relate to their patients/clients in a non-judgemental, non-critical and non-punitive way. In light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A
    • 2Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A
    • 3A is true but R is false
    • 4A is false but R is true
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A
    Bruce Shertzer and Shelley Stone, Fundamentals of Counseling
    The concept

    AssertionBruce Shertzer and Shelley Stone defined acceptance as the counsellor's respect for the client as a person of worth. Reading 'warmth' as a copying slip, A is true.

    ReasonR is true and explains A: respecting a client's worth is shown by relating in a non-judgemental, non-critical, non-punitive way.

    What it isAcceptance is close to Carl Rogers' unconditional positive regard: valuing the client as a person without approving every behaviour.

    Not certainAs printed, A says 'warmth' where Bruce Shertzer and Shelley Stone wrote 'worth', probably a copying slip; if the change is deliberate, A is false and (4) is right.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of AR is not a separate fact: the non-judgemental, non-critical stance is what respecting a client as a person of worth means, so it does explain A.
    • (3) A is true but R is falseR is true; relating without judging, criticising or punishing is the standard description of an accepting counsellor.
    • (4) A is false but R is trueHolds only if 'warmth' is a deliberate change; read as 'worth', Shertzer and Stone's definition is true.

  58. In Bronfenbrenner's model, which one of the following systems is independent of cultural bias?

    • 1Micro system
    • 2Meso system
    • 3Exo system
    • 4Macro system
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Micro system
    Urie Bronfenbrenner, ecological systems theory
    The concept

    Why this oneThe microsystem is the child's immediate, face-to-face setting: family, school, peers. Every child in every culture has one, so it is the level least tied to any one culture's values.

    The nested systemsUrie Bronfenbrenner placed the child inside nested systems: microsystem, mesosystem (links between microsystems), exosystem (settings that affect the child indirectly), macrosystem (culture) and chronosystem (time).

    Exam tipCulture sits in the macrosystem, the outermost layer, which makes it the most culture-loaded level.

    Not certainNo standard text ranks the systems on cultural bias; the microsystem is chosen as the most immediate, universal setting, with the mesosystem as runner-up for the same reason.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Meso systemLinks between microsystems, such as home and school; how these links work depends on cultural norms like parents' involvement in schooling.
    • (3) Exo systemSettings the child is not in, such as a parent's workplace, local government and media, which are shaped by each society's institutions.
    • (4) Macro systemThe culture itself: its values, laws, customs and ideology, so it is the level least free of cultural bias.

  59. Match List I with List II. List I: (A) Efficacy (B) Tolerance (C) Downregulation (D) Withdrawal. List II: (I) Response activated when drugs bind with a receptor (II) Repeated exposure with less activation of receptors (III) Compensatory reduction in receptor availability (IV) Unpleasant physiological responses. Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IV
    • 2A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV
    • 3A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I
    • 4A-IV, B-I, C-II, D-III
    ✓ Answer: (2) A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV
    Drug-receptor pharmacology terms
    The concept

    Correct pairs(A) Efficacy = (I) response activated when the drug binds the receptor; (B) Tolerance = (II) less receptor activation after repeated exposure; (C) Downregulation = (III) compensatory reduction in receptor availability; (D) Withdrawal = (IV) unpleasant physiological responses.

    EfficacyAffinity is how readily a drug binds a receptor; efficacy is how strongly it activates the receptor once bound.

    How tolerance happensWith repeated use the brain compensates, for example by downregulation (fewer receptors), so the same dose has less effect. Stopping the drug then unmasks these changes as withdrawal.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IVSwaps A and B: efficacy is the activation produced by binding, while reduced activation after repeated exposure is tolerance.
    • (3) A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-IWrong throughout: efficacy is not receptor reduction, and withdrawal is the unpleasant aftermath of stopping a drug, not receptor activation.
    • (4) A-IV, B-I, C-II, D-IIIWrong throughout: efficacy is activation on binding, downregulation is fewer receptors, and withdrawal is the unpleasant response, not receptor loss.

  60. Which one of the following is considered as correct for Poverty Gap?

    • 1Gap between Rich and Poor
    • 2Gap between developed and developing nations
    • 3Difference between poverty line and actual income level of all those living below that line
    • 4Gap in the infrastructure facilities between developed nations
    ✓ Answer: (3) Difference between poverty line and actual income level of all those living below that line
    Poverty gap, a measure of poverty depth
    The concept

    Why this oneThe poverty gap is the shortfall between the poverty line and the actual income of each person below it, added up or averaged across the population.

    How it worksIf the line is 100 and a person earns 70, the gap is 30. The poverty gap index averages these shortfalls over the whole population (the non-poor count as zero) as a share of the line.

    Exam tipThe headcount ratio tells how many people are poor; the poverty gap tells how poor they are.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Gap between Rich and PoorThis is income inequality, measured by tools such as the Gini coefficient, not the poverty gap.
    • (2) Gap between developed and developing nationsThis is the development divide between rich and poor countries, not a measure of individual poverty.
    • (4) Gap in the infrastructure facilities between developed nationsThis is an infrastructure or development gap between countries; the poverty gap concerns individual incomes below the poverty line.

  61. A harsh father always restricts the curiosity motive of his son and the son develops a general hostility towards his superiors. Which of the following is correct about this?

    • 1The father represses the curiosity
    • 2Such change is permanent
    • 3It is the most modifiable response
    • 4It is conditioned hostility
    ✓ Best answer: (4) It is conditioned hostility
    Classical conditioning and stimulus generalisation
    The concept

    Why this oneThe son learned hostility towards the punishing father, and it has spread to other authority figures. That is conditioned hostility carried by stimulus generalisation.

    How it worksAuthority (the father) is repeatedly paired with frustration, so authority itself comes to trigger anger. Superiors resemble the father and trigger the same response, as Little Albert's conditioned fear spread from a rat to other furry objects.

    Exam tipLearned emotional responses can be weakened by extinction or counter-conditioning, so calling such a change permanent goes too far.

    Not certainThe item has no clear textbook source; conditioned, generalised hostility fits best, with (1) the runner-up if the setter used 'represses' loosely to mean 'suppresses'.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) The father represses the curiosityRepression in Sigmund Freud's sense is a person's own unconscious defence; the father blocks or punishes the curiosity, he does not repress it.
    • (2) Such change is permanentLearned emotional responses can be reduced by extinction or counter-conditioning, so the change is not necessarily permanent.
    • (3) It is the most modifiable responseA broad attitude learned early and generalised to all superiors is usually resistant to change, not the most modifiable kind of response.

  62. Which of the following character strengths are included in the VIA classification of strengths? (A) Creativity (B) Forgiveness (C) Resilience (D) Prudence

    • 1A, B and C only
    • 2D, C and A only
    • 3A, B and D only
    • 4A and C only
    ✓ Answer: (3) A, B and D only
    Christopher Peterson and Martin Seligman, VIA classification (2004)
    The concept

    Why this oneThe VIA lists 24 character strengths. Creativity falls under wisdom, and forgiveness and prudence under temperance; resilience is not one of the 24.

    What it isChristopher Peterson and Martin Seligman grouped the 24 strengths under six virtues: wisdom, courage, humanity, justice, temperance and transcendence.

    Exam tipCourage covers bravery, perseverance, honesty and zest. Resilience is studied in positive psychology but is not a VIA strength.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, B and C onlyIncludes resilience, which is not among the 24 VIA strengths, and leaves out prudence, a temperance strength.
    • (2) D, C and A onlyIncludes resilience, which the VIA does not list, and leaves out forgiveness, a temperance strength.
    • (4) A and C onlyIncludes resilience, which is not a VIA strength, and omits forgiveness and prudence, which both are.

  63. The psychosocial stage of Intimacy v/s Isolation is preceded and succeeded by which of the following? (A) Generativity v/s Stagnation (B) Identity v/s Identity confusion (C) Industry v/s Diffusion

    • 1A only
    • 2A and C only
    • 3A and B only
    • 4B and C only
    ✓ Answer: (3) A and B only
    Erik Erikson, psychosocial stages (1950)
    The concept

    Why this oneIn Erik Erikson's scheme, intimacy vs isolation (stage 6, young adulthood) follows identity vs role confusion (stage 5, adolescence) and precedes generativity vs stagnation (stage 7, middle adulthood).

    Correct orderTrust vs mistrust → autonomy vs shame and doubt → initiative vs guilt → industry vs inferiority → identity vs role confusion → intimacy vs isolation → generativity vs stagnation → integrity vs despair.

    Exam tipA secure identity must come first; only then can a person risk sharing it with another in intimacy.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A onlyGives only the stage that follows; it leaves out identity vs role confusion, the stage just before intimacy.
    • (2) A and C onlyC is not an Erikson stage: industry pairs with inferiority (stage 4, school age), and 'diffusion' belongs to identity diffusion in stage 5.
    • (4) B and C onlyIndustry (stage 4) is not next to intimacy, and C misprints it as industry vs diffusion; generativity, the following stage, is missing.

  64. Given below are two statements, one is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R. Assertion A: The obtained t-value will follow the theoretical t-distribution if the underlying assumptions are fulfilled. Reason R: The application of t-test makes an assumption of heterogeneity of variance. In light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A
    • 2Both A and R are correct but R is NOT the correct explanation of A
    • 3A is correct but R is not correct
    • 4A is not correct but R is correct
    ✓ Answer: (3) A is correct but R is not correct
    William Gosset ('Student'), t-test assumptions
    The concept

    AssertionA is true: the computed t follows the theoretical t distribution for its degrees of freedom only when the test's assumptions hold, so its p-values are then accurate.

    ReasonR is false: the independent t-test assumes homogeneity of variance (equal population variances), not heterogeneity.

    Other assumptionsThe data should come from normal populations and the observations should be independent; Levene's test checks equal variances.

    Exam tipWhen variances are unequal, use Welch's t-test, which adjusts the degrees of freedom.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of AR is false: the t-test assumes equal (homogeneous) variances, so it cannot explain A.
    • (2) Both A and R are correct but R is NOT the correct explanation of AR is false, not true: heterogeneity of variance violates the t-test's assumption of equal variances.
    • (4) A is not correct but R is correctA is correct: when normality, independence and equal variances hold, the sampling distribution of t matches the theoretical t distribution.

  65. Client: (Silent and shifting nervously in her chair. She is facing the counselor, but her eyes scan anxiously about). Counselor: "It must be difficult for you to decide what you are going to do here with me." This counselor response is an example of:

    • 1Dealing with content speculated to be motivating the non-verbal behaviour by the counselor
    • 2Encouraging the client to deal with non-verbal behaviour
    • 3Encouraging the client to understand the feelings
    • 4Reflection of feelings
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Dealing with content speculated to be motivating the non-verbal behaviour by the counselor
    Responding to client nonverbal behaviour (counselling skills)
    The concept

    Why this oneThe counsellor does not name the behaviour or a feeling. Instead the counsellor speculates about the content behind it, that the client is unsure what to do in the session, and responds to that.

    The choicesA counsellor can respond to nonverbal behaviour by ignoring it, by inviting the client to look at it ('What are you feeling as you shift in your chair?'), or by addressing what seems to be motivating it.

    Exam tipA reflection of feeling names the emotion directly ('You seem anxious'); a speculation adds the counsellor's guess about its cause.

    Not certainThe counsellor's line also sounds like a reflection of feeling, so (4) is the runner-up, but it names no feeling and guesses at what lies behind the fidgeting.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Encouraging the client to deal with non-verbal behaviourThat would point to the behaviour itself, for example 'I notice you keep looking around the room', and invite the client to talk about it.
    • (3) Encouraging the client to understand the feelingsThat would invite the client to explore her own emotions ('What are you feeling right now?'); here the counsellor offers a guess about the cause instead.
    • (4) Reflection of feelingsA reflection names the emotion the client shows, such as 'You seem nervous'; this response names no feeling and guesses at its cause.

  66. A teacher engages students in hands-on, inquiry-based and relevant learning activities in his class. What does this illustrate? (A) Application of the law of exercise (B) Application of the law of effect (C) Creating intrinsic motivation (D) Creating extrinsic motivation

    • 1B only
    • 2B and C only
    • 3A and C only
    • 4C only
    ✓ Answer: (4) C only
    Edward Deci and Richard Ryan, intrinsic motivation
    The concept

    Why this oneHands-on, inquiry-based, relevant tasks make learning interesting in itself, so students work because they want to. That builds intrinsic motivation; no reward or drill is involved.

    What it isIntrinsic motivation is doing a task for its own sake: curiosity, interest, challenge. Edward Deci and Richard Ryan's self-determination theory says it grows when learners feel autonomy, competence and relatedness.

    Exam tipExtrinsic motivation comes from outside rewards such as grades and prizes. Edward Thorndike's laws of exercise and effect are about practice and reward.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) B onlyEdward Thorndike's law of effect says responses followed by satisfying outcomes are strengthened; the activities here involve no reward, so it does not apply.
    • (2) B and C onlyC is right, but B, Edward Thorndike's law of effect, depends on rewarding consequences, which the stem does not mention.
    • (3) A and C onlyC is right, but A, Edward Thorndike's law of exercise, concerns strengthening bonds through repetition and drill, not inquiry or relevance.

  67. What do you call the attention that is drawn automatically to a stimulus without the intention of the participant?

    • 1Divided attention
    • 2Shadowing
    • 3Endogenous attention
    • 4Exogenous attention
    ✓ Answer: (4) Exogenous attention
    Michael Posner, exogenous vs endogenous orienting
    The concept

    Why this oneExogenous attention is captured automatically, bottom-up, by a salient stimulus such as a sudden flash or a loud noise, whatever the person intends.

    ContrastEndogenous attention is voluntary and top-down: you direct it on purpose, to serve a goal or follow a cue such as an arrow.

    Exam tipIn Michael Posner's cueing task, a flash at the target location works fast and automatically; a central arrow works more slowly and needs intention.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Divided attentionThis means spreading attention across two or more tasks at once, as in driving while talking; it concerns capacity, not automatic capture.
    • (2) ShadowingThis is the dichotic listening technique of repeating one ear's message aloud, used by Colin Cherry to study selective attention.
    • (3) Endogenous attentionThe opposite: voluntary, goal-directed (top-down) attention, which needs the participant's intention.

  68. Which one of the following terms denotes that there are qualities of experience that cannot be explained in terms of combinations of traditional kinds of sensations?

    • 1Zeitgeist
    • 2Tropism
    • 3Gestalt Qualitaten
    • 4Conarium
    ✓ Answer: (3) Gestalt Qualitaten
    Christian von Ehrenfels, form qualities (1890)
    The concept

    Why this oneChristian von Ehrenfels coined Gestaltqualitäten (form qualities). A melody stays the same tune in a new key though every note has changed, so the whole is not a sum of sensations.

    Why it mattersThis challenged the structuralism of Wilhelm Wundt and Edward Titchener, which built experience from elementary sensations. It paved the way for Gestalt psychology (Max Wertheimer, Wolfgang Köhler, Kurt Koffka).

    Exam tipGestalt motto: the whole is different from the sum of its parts.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) ZeitgeistMeans 'spirit of the times', the intellectual climate of an era, a term Edwin Boring used in his history of psychology.
    • (2) TropismIn Jacques Loeb's work, an automatic, forced orientation of an organism towards or away from a stimulus such as light.
    • (4) ConariumThe old name for the pineal gland, which René Descartes saw as the point where mind and body interact.

  69. Antidiuretic hormone (ADH) is released by which of the following?

    • 1Adrenal cortex
    • 2Adrenal medulla
    • 3Anterior pituitary
    • 4Posterior pituitary
    ✓ Answer: (4) Posterior pituitary
    Hypothalamus and posterior pituitary (neurohypophysis)
    The concept

    Why this oneADH (vasopressin) is made by neurons in the hypothalamus, travels down their axons and is released into the blood from the posterior pituitary.

    What it doesADH makes the kidneys reabsorb water, so less urine is produced. It is released when the body is dehydrated or blood volume falls.

    Exam tipThe posterior pituitary releases only two hormones, ADH and oxytocin; the anterior pituitary makes its own hormones (GH, ACTH, TSH, FSH, LH, prolactin).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Adrenal cortexSecretes corticosteroids: cortisol (a glucocorticoid), aldosterone (a mineralocorticoid) and adrenal sex hormones.
    • (2) Adrenal medullaReleases adrenaline (epinephrine) and noradrenaline during the fight-or-flight response.
    • (3) Anterior pituitaryMakes and releases its own hormones, such as growth hormone, ACTH, TSH, FSH, LH and prolactin, under control of hypothalamic releasing hormones.

  70. Which of the following dimensions are essential to be mastered in multicultural counseling, as per McFadden's transcultural perspective? (A) Cultural-Historical (B) Historical hostility management (C) Scientific-Ideological (D) Minority identity management (E) Social

    • 1A, E and B only
    • 2B, D and A only
    • 3B, D and E only
    • 4A, C and E only
    ✓ Answer: (4) A, C and E only
    John McFadden, stylistic model of transcultural counselling
    The concept

    Why this oneJohn McFadden's transcultural model says counsellors must master three dimensions: cultural-historical, psychosocial and scientific-ideological. 'Social' in (E) stands for the psychosocial dimension.

    What each meansCultural-historical: knowing the client's culture and its history. Psychosocial: how the group's social and psychological patterns shape the client; scientific-ideological: choosing counselling approaches that fit those realities.

    Exam tipMcFadden = three dimensions, C-P-S: cultural-historical, psychosocial, scientific-ideological.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, E and B onlyHistorical hostility management is not one of McFadden's three dimensions, and this set leaves out the scientific-ideological dimension.
    • (2) B, D and A onlyNeither historical hostility management nor minority identity management is a McFadden dimension; psychosocial and scientific-ideological are missing.
    • (3) B, D and E onlyOnly (E) is a McFadden dimension; historical hostility and minority identity management are not, and cultural-historical and scientific-ideological are missing.

  71. Which one of the following models proposes that there are three major functional units of the brain: focussing on sustaining attention; receiving and storing information; and planning and self-monitoring?

    • 1PASS model
    • 2Kar and Parrila's model
    • 3Atkinson and Shiffrin model
    • 4Hunt's model
    ✓ Answer: (1) PASS model
    J. P. Das, Jack Naglieri, John Kirby, after Alexander Luria
    The concept

    Why this oneThe PASS model (Planning, Attention, Simultaneous, Successive) of J. P. Das, Jack Naglieri and John Kirby is built on Alexander Luria's three functional units of the brain.

    The three unitsUnit 1 (brainstem, reticular system): arousal and attention. Unit 2 (occipital, parietal, temporal lobes): receiving, coding and storing information; Unit 3 (frontal lobes): planning and self-monitoring.

    Exam tipThe Cognitive Assessment System (CAS) by Jack Naglieri and J. P. Das measures the four PASS processes.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Kar and Parrila's modelKar and Parrila co-wrote on cognitive planning with J. P. Das, but the three functional units framework is Luria's, formalised as the PASS model.
    • (3) Atkinson and Shiffrin modelRichard Atkinson and Richard Shiffrin's multi-store model describes memory stores (sensory, short-term, long-term), not functional units of the brain.
    • (4) Hunt's modelEarl Hunt studied intelligence through speed of information processing (the cognitive correlates approach), not Luria's three brain units.

  72. Which of the following are the components of Metacognition? (A) Metacognitive knowledge (B) Metacognitive experience (C) Metacognitive control (D) Metacognitive regulation

    • 1A and B only
    • 2A and D only
    • 3A, B and C only
    • 4A, B and D only
    ✓ Best answer: (4) A, B and D only
    John Flavell (1979); Ann Brown, metacognitive regulation
    The concept

    Why this oneMetacognition is usually split into three parts: metacognitive knowledge (what you know about thinking), metacognitive experiences (feelings during a task, such as 'I don't get this') and metacognitive regulation (planning, monitoring, evaluating).

    OriginsJohn Flavell introduced metacognition, 'thinking about thinking', with knowledge and experiences. Ann Brown stressed the regulation of cognition.

    Exam tipRegulation and control mean much the same; when both appear, regulation is the standard textbook label.

    Not certainControl and regulation are near synonyms (Thomas Nelson and Louis Narens speak of monitoring and control), so (3) is the runner-up; most texts use the term regulation.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A and B onlyKnowledge and experiences are John Flavell's two parts, but this leaves out metacognitive regulation, the planning and monitoring side.
    • (2) A and D onlyKnowledge and regulation is the two-part split used by Ann Brown, but it omits metacognitive experiences, the third standard component.
    • (3) A, B and C onlyControl is close in meaning to regulation, but the standard three-part list names the third component metacognitive regulation.

  73. Given below are two statements, one is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R. Assertion A: Many companies now enable their employees to share profits or even to participate in company ownership. Reason R: Workers' productivity boosts rewards from employers. In light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below:

    • 1Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A
    • 2Both A and R are correct and R is NOT the correct explanation of A
    • 3A is correct but R is not correct
    • 4A is not correct but R is correct
    ✓ Best answer: (1) Both A and R are correct and R is the correct explanation of A
    Profit-sharing and employee ownership, pay for performance
    The concept

    AssertionA is true: many firms run profit-sharing and employee share ownership schemes, so workers gain when the company does well.

    ReasonR is true and explains A: under these schemes, higher worker productivity raises profits, which raises workers' rewards, so pay is tied to performance.

    Why it worksLinking rewards to results acts as positive reinforcement and gives workers a sense of ownership, which motivates effort.

    Not certainR can be read as reversing cause and effect (rewards are meant to boost productivity), in which case (2) is the runner-up.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Both A and R are correct and R is NOT the correct explanation of AR is not unrelated: profit-sharing exists precisely so that higher productivity turns into higher rewards, which is why firms offer it.
    • (3) A is correct but R is not correctR is true; in pay-for-performance schemes such as profit-sharing and gainsharing, higher productivity does raise what employees receive.
    • (4) A is not correct but R is correctA is true; profit-sharing plans and employee share ownership plans are common in modern companies.

  74. Who among the following conducted one of the most famous and controversial experiments in the area of obedience?

    • 1Sherif
    • 2Asch
    • 3Milgram
    • 4Cialdini
    ✓ Answer: (3) Milgram
    Stanley Milgram, obedience studies at Yale (1963)
    The concept

    Why this oneStanley Milgram had participants, as 'teachers', give what they thought were rising electric shocks to a 'learner'. About 65 percent went to the maximum 450 volts when the experimenter insisted.

    Why controversialThe learner was an actor and no shocks were real, but participants were deceived and suffered real stress. This drove later ethics rules on deception and debriefing.

    Exam tipObedience = following orders from an authority; conformity (Solomon Asch) = going along with peers; compliance (Robert Cialdini) = yielding to a direct request.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) SherifMuzafer Sherif studied norm formation with the autokinetic effect and intergroup conflict in the Robbers Cave study, not obedience.
    • (2) AschSolomon Asch studied conformity: people agreeing with a group's obviously wrong judgements of line length.
    • (4) CialdiniRobert Cialdini studies influence and compliance tactics such as reciprocity and commitment, not obedience to authority.

  75. Teplov, while discussing strong/weak nervous systems, believed that the point of 'transmarginal inhibition' is the point beyond which:

    • 1The magnitude of response decreases as stimulus intensity decreases
    • 2The magnitude of response increases as stimulus intensity decreases
    • 3The magnitude of response increases irrespective of stimulus intensity decrease/increase
    • 4The magnitude of response decreases irrespective of stimulus intensity decrease/increase
    ✓ Best answer: (2) The magnitude of response increases as stimulus intensity decreases
    Boris Teplov, after Ivan Pavlov's law of strength
    The concept

    Why this oneBelow the threshold of transmarginal inhibition, response grows with stimulus intensity. Beyond it the relation reverses: stronger stimuli give weaker responses, so lowering intensity makes the response rise, as (2) says.

    How it worksIvan Pavlov saw transmarginal (protective) inhibition as the nervous system shielding itself from overload. Boris Teplov measured strength of the nervous system by how high this threshold lies.

    Strong vs weakA strong nervous system tolerates intense stimulation before inhibition sets in; a weak one is more sensitive and reaches the point sooner.

    Not certainThe options look garbled: the real idea is that response falls as intensity rises, which (2) states in reverse, while (1) would be right if its second 'decreases' was meant to be 'increases'.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) The magnitude of response decreases as stimulus intensity decreasesBelow the threshold, response falls when intensity falls; that is the ordinary law of strength, not what happens beyond transmarginal inhibition.
    • (3) The magnitude of response increases irrespective of stimulus intensity decrease/increaseBeyond the threshold, response still depends on intensity, but inversely; it does not keep rising whatever the stimulus does.
    • (4) The magnitude of response decreases irrespective of stimulus intensity decrease/increaseResponse does not fall whatever happens: beyond the threshold it falls when intensity rises but recovers when intensity is lowered.

  76. Which of the following were considered to be appropriate for psychological study by Wundt? (A) Analysis of cultural products (B) Higher mental processes (C) The totality of psychological phenomena (D) Self-observation (E) Physiological type of experimentation

    • 1B, C and D only
    • 2B, C, D and E only
    • 3A, D and E only
    • 4A, C, D and E only
    ✓ Best answer: (4) A, C, D and E only
    Wilhelm Wundt, experimental psychology and Völkerpsychologie
    The concept

    Why this oneWilhelm Wundt defined psychology as the study of the whole content of immediate experience (C). He studied it by controlled self-observation (D) using the experimental method of physiology (E).

    Two psychologiesFor complex processes such as language, thought and morals, Wundt said lab experiments were unsuitable. He studied them through cultural products like language, myth and custom (A) in his ten-volume Völkerpsychologie (folk psychology).

    Why not (B)That is the trap: Wundt held that higher mental processes could not be studied directly by experiment; only their cultural products could be analysed.

    Exam tipWundt = trained introspection plus the physiological method for simple processes; Völkerpsychologie for complex social and cultural ones.

    Not certainWundt did study higher mental processes (B), but only indirectly through cultural products, so the item is loosely worded; if 'totality of phenomena' (C) is counted as wrong, the answer becomes (3).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) B, C and D onlyLeaves out cultural products (A) and physiological experimentation (E), both central to Wundt, and includes higher mental processes, which he kept out of experimental study.
    • (2) B, C, D and E onlyIncludes higher mental processes (B), which Wundt kept out of the laboratory, and drops the analysis of cultural products (A), the core of his Völkerpsychologie.
    • (3) A, D and E onlyRightly keeps A, D and E but drops C; Wundt defined psychology as the study of the whole content of immediate experience.

  77. Match List I with List II. List I: (A) Aggression (B) Anti-Social Aggression (C) Prosocial Aggression (D) Sanctioned Aggression. List II: (I) Aggression that is permissible according to the norms of the individual's social group (II) Aggressive acts that support commonly accepted social norms (III) Any action intended to hurt another person (IV) Aggressive acts that violate commonly accepted social norms. Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1A-IV, B-II, C-III, D-I
    • 2A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I
    • 3A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV
    • 4A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IV
    ✓ Answer: (2) A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I
    Shelley Taylor, Letitia Peplau, David Sears: kinds of aggression
    The concept

    Correct pairs(A) Aggression = (III) any action intended to hurt another person; (B) Anti-social aggression = (IV) acts that violate accepted social norms; (C) Prosocial aggression = (II) acts that support accepted norms; (D) Sanctioned aggression = (I) acts the group's norms permit.

    How to tellThe test is the social norm. Anti-social aggression breaks it (a mugging); prosocial aggression enforces it (police stopping a gunman); sanctioned aggression is not required but allowed (hitting an attacker in self-defence).

    Exam tipThe general definition of aggression rests on the intent to harm, not on whether harm actually happens.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A-IV, B-II, C-III, D-IGives the general definition (III) to prosocial aggression and the norm-breaking meaning (IV) to aggression in general; prosocial aggression is the kind that supports norms.
    • (3) A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IVEvery pair is wrong: anti-social aggression violates norms (IV) rather than supporting them, and sanctioned aggression is what group norms permit (I).
    • (4) A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IVEvery pair is wrong: aggression in general is any act meant to hurt (III), and prosocial aggression is the kind that supports norms (II).

  78. Arrange the pairings of US and CS in classical conditioning in terms of onset of US in relation to CS, starting from the earliest. (A) Backward (B) Simultaneous (C) Delayed (D) Trace

    • 1A, B, C, D
    • 2D, B, C, A
    • 3C, D, B, A
    • 4D, C, A, B
    ✓ Answer: (1) A, B, C, D
    Pavlovian conditioning: CS-US timing procedures
    The concept

    Correct orderBackward (US comes before the CS) → Simultaneous (US starts with the CS) → Delayed (US starts while the CS is still on) → Trace (US starts only after the CS has ended).

    How it worksThe conditioned stimulus (CS) is the signal and the unconditioned stimulus (US) is the event it predicts. Moving from backward to trace, the US arrives later and later relative to CS onset.

    Exam tipDelayed conditioning usually gives the strongest learning; trace weakens as the gap grows; backward conditioning is the weakest and often produces inhibition.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) D, B, C, APuts trace first, but in trace conditioning the US arrives only after the CS has ended, so its onset is the latest, not the earliest.
    • (3) C, D, B, AStarts with delayed and ends with backward, nearly reversing the order; backward is the only procedure in which the US comes before the CS.
    • (4) D, C, A, BPlaces trace and delayed ahead of backward and simultaneous, when those two have the earliest US onset.

  79. The Door in the Face Technique is based on which tactic of compliance?

    • 1Tactics based on reciprocity
    • 2Tactics based on commitment or consistency
    • 3Tactics based on friendship or liking
    • 4Tactics based on scarcity
    ✓ Answer: (1) Tactics based on reciprocity
    Robert Cialdini, door-in-the-face technique
    The concept

    Why this oneIn the door-in-the-face technique a large request is refused, then the asker retreats to a smaller one. The retreat looks like a concession, and the norm of reciprocity pushes the target to concede in return by agreeing.

    Classic exampleRobert Cialdini's team asked students to counsel young offenders two hours a week for two years (refused), then to take them on a two-hour zoo trip; far more agreed than when asked for the zoo trip alone.

    Exam tipRobert Baron and Donn Byrne group door-in-the-face and that's-not-all under reciprocity; foot-in-the-door and lowball fall under commitment or consistency.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) Tactics based on commitment or consistencyThis group covers foot-in-the-door and lowball, which build on an earlier small yes, not on a refused large request.
    • (3) Tactics based on friendship or likingThis group covers ingratiation, flattery and self-presentation, which work by making the target like the requester.
    • (4) Tactics based on scarcityThis group covers playing hard to get and the deadline technique, which make an offer seem rare or short-lived.

  80. Cognitive theory contends that dependency behaviours arise from and are maintained by cognitive distortions and maladaptive thought processes. Which of the following are associated with cognitive distortions? (A) Availability (B) Hindsight bias (C) Flexible attribution (D) Illusion of control

    • 1A, B, C and D
    • 2A, B and C only
    • 3B, C and D only
    • 4C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (1) A, B, C and D
    Cognitive model of gambling and other dependencies
    The concept

    Why this oneAll four are recognised cognitive distortions in problem gambling and other dependencies. Each keeps the person believing that carrying on will pay off.

    Availability, hindsightAvailability: vivid, easily recalled wins seem more common than they are. Hindsight bias: after an outcome the person feels it was predictable, so they trust their future predictions.

    Attribution, controlFlexible attribution: wins are credited to skill, losses blamed on bad luck or near misses. Illusion of control (Ellen Langer): belief that one can influence chance outcomes.

    Exam tipAdd the gambler's fallacy (a win is 'due' after a run of losses) to the same list.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) A, B and C onlyLeaves out illusion of control, the best known gambling distortion, in which a person believes skill or rituals can sway chance outcomes.
    • (3) B, C and D onlyLeaves out availability: vividly remembered wins feel more frequent than they are, which feeds continued gambling or use.
    • (4) C and D onlyLeaves out availability and hindsight bias, both standard distortions that make wins seem common and outcomes seem predictable.

  81. What does 'Absolute Poverty' mean?

    • 1Poverty in terms of absolute number of people
    • 2Poverty in terms of prevailing price level
    • 3Poverty in terms of absolute level of unemployment
    • 4Basic minimum calorie requirement
    ✓ Answer: (4) Basic minimum calorie requirement
    Indian poverty line, minimum calorie norm
    The concept

    Why this oneAbsolute poverty means income too low to meet basic needs. In India it has been measured by the money needed for a minimum calorie intake: 2,400 kcal per person per day in rural areas and 2,100 in urban areas.

    Absolute vs relativeRelative poverty compares a person's income with others in the same society; absolute poverty uses a fixed minimum standard, however others live.

    Exam tipThe poverty line is the money value of that minimum consumption basket; people below it are counted as absolutely poor.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Poverty in terms of absolute number of peopleThe number of poor people is the headcount, a way of counting poverty, not its meaning; absolute poverty refers to a fixed minimum standard of living.
    • (2) Poverty in terms of prevailing price levelPrices are used to turn the minimum consumption basket into a money poverty line, but absolute poverty itself is defined by the minimum needs.
    • (3) Poverty in terms of absolute level of unemploymentUnemployment is a cause and correlate of poverty, not the definition of absolute poverty.

  82. In cognitive therapy by Ellis, arrange the sequence of the following after recognising the activating event. (A) Discriminating the new emotional/behavioural consequences (B) Recognising the emotional/behavioural consequences of belief (C) Understanding the corresponding beliefs (D) Challenging and counteracting these beliefs (E) Recognising new feelings

    • 1B-C-A-E-D
    • 2C-B-D-A-E
    • 3C-B-A-E-D
    • 4B-C-D-A-E
    ✓ Best answer: (4) B-C-D-A-E
    Albert Ellis, REBT ABCDE model
    The concept

    Correct orderAfter the activating event: recognise the emotional and behavioural consequences (B), find the beliefs behind them (C), dispute the beliefs (D), discriminate the new consequences (A), recognise new feelings (E). So B-C-D-A-E.

    Why consequence firstThe client arrives with the upsetting feeling, so the therapist assesses the consequence first and then looks for the corresponding irrational belief that produced it.

    How it worksAlbert Ellis held that events do not upset us directly; our irrational beliefs about them do. Disputing those beliefs produces a more effective outlook and healthier feelings.

    Exam tipABCDEF = Activating event, Belief, Consequence, Disputing, Effect (new effective philosophy), new Feelings, as set out by Gerald Corey.

    Not certainRead by the letters of the ABC model, the belief comes before the consequence, which gives C-B-D-A-E, option (2), the runner-up.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) B-C-A-E-DStarts right with consequences then beliefs, but leaves disputing to the end, when disputing must come before any new consequences or feelings appear.
    • (2) C-B-D-A-EPuts the belief first, the literal letter order of ABC; in practice the therapist starts from the upsetting consequence and then finds the corresponding belief.
    • (3) C-B-A-E-DPuts the belief first and places the new consequences and feelings before disputing, when they are the result of disputing.

  83. Match List I with List II. List I (Sources of Personal Efficacy by Bandura): (A) Performance accomplishment (B) Vicarious experiences (C) Verbal persuasion (D) Emotional arousal. List II (Therapeutic processes): (I) Live models, symbolic modelling (II) Attribution, relaxation biofeedback, symbolic desensitisation (III) Participant modelling, performance exposure (IV) Suggestion, exhortation, self-instruction. Choose the correct answer from the options given below:

    • 1A-III, B-II, C-IV, D-I
    • 2A-I, B-III, C-II, D-IV
    • 3A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IV
    • 4A-III, B-I, C-IV, D-II
    ✓ Answer: (4) A-III, B-I, C-IV, D-II
    Albert Bandura, self-efficacy theory (1977)
    The concept

    Correct pairs(A) Performance accomplishment = (III) participant modelling, performance exposure; (B) Vicarious experience = (I) live and symbolic modelling; (C) Verbal persuasion = (IV) suggestion, exhortation, self-instruction; (D) Emotional arousal = (II) attribution, relaxation biofeedback, symbolic desensitisation.

    How it worksSelf-efficacy is the belief that one can carry out a behaviour. Albert Bandura named four sources of it, and each therapy raises efficacy through one of them.

    Exam tipPerformance accomplishments (mastery experiences) are the strongest source, because they rest on one's own success.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A-III, B-II, C-IV, D-IGives vicarious experience the arousal methods (II) and emotional arousal the modelling methods (I); watching models is the vicarious source.
