    • (2) A-I, B-III, C-II, D-IVSwaps the first two sources: participant modelling is a performance method, while live and symbolic modelling are vicarious; it also swaps persuasion and arousal.
    • (3) A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IVEvery pair is wrong: relaxation and biofeedback lower emotional arousal, live models are vicarious, and suggestion and exhortation are verbal persuasion.

  84. Who believed that meaning in life can be uncovered by creative, experiential and attitudinal values?

    • 1Rollo May
    • 2Victor Frankl
    • 3Friedrich Nietzsche
    • 4Jean Paul Sartre
    ✓ Answer: (2) Victor Frankl
    Viktor Frankl, logotherapy
    The concept

    Why this oneViktor Frankl said meaning is found three ways: creative values (what we give the world through work or deeds), experiential values (what we take from it, such as love or beauty) and attitudinal values (the stand we take toward unavoidable suffering).

    BackgroundFrankl, a concentration camp survivor, founded logotherapy and saw the will to meaning as the main human motive (Man's Search for Meaning).

    Exam tipAttitudinal values remain even when nothing can be changed: a person can still choose their attitude to suffering.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Rollo MayAmerican existential psychologist who wrote on anxiety, love and will and the courage to create, not on the three value routes to meaning.
    • (3) Friedrich NietzscheGerman philosopher of the will to power and the Übermensch; Frankl quoted his line that one who has a why to live can bear almost any how.
    • (4) Jean Paul SartreFrench existentialist who held that existence precedes essence and that we create meaning through free choice, not through three types of value.

  85. Which of the following is an attitude measure that places a single adjective in the centre of an even number of numerical values?

    • 1Edwards scale
    • 2Thurstone scale
    • 3Stapel scale
    • 4Semantic differential scale
    ✓ Answer: (3) Stapel scale
    Jan Stapel, unipolar rating scale
    The concept

    Why this oneThe Stapel scale puts one adjective (say 'Friendly') in the middle of a column of numbers, usually +5 to -5 with no zero. The respondent picks how well the word describes the object.

    How it differsIt is a one-pole version of the semantic differential, so there is no need to find an exact opposite adjective for each item.

    Exam tipStapel = single adjective, even number of points (10). Semantic differential = two bipolar adjectives at the ends of a usually seven-point line.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Edwards scaleAllen Edwards is known for the scale discrimination technique, which blends Thurstone and Likert methods, and for work on social desirability; no central adjective is used.
    • (2) Thurstone scaleLouis Thurstone's method of equal-appearing intervals has judges rate statements on an 11-point scale; respondents then endorse statements of known scale value.
    • (4) Semantic differential scaleCharles Osgood's tool places the object between two opposite adjectives (good to bad) on a usually seven-point scale, not one adjective in the centre.

  86. Total cholesterol is determined by adding which of the following values?

    • 1HDL + LDL
    • 2HDL + LDL + 30% of VLDL
    • 3HDL + LDL + 10% of VLDL
    • 4HDL + LDL + 20% of Triglycerides
    ✓ Answer: (4) HDL + LDL + 20% of Triglycerides
    Friedewald formula: VLDL cholesterol = triglycerides ÷ 5
    The concept

    Why this oneTotal cholesterol is the cholesterol carried in HDL, LDL and VLDL. VLDL cholesterol is estimated as triglycerides ÷ 5, that is 20% of triglycerides (in mg/dL).

    How it worksLabs use the same relation rearranged, the Friedewald formula: LDL = total cholesterol - HDL - (triglycerides ÷ 5).

    Exam tipHigh LDL ('bad') raises the risk of coronary heart disease; high HDL ('good') protects against it.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) HDL + LDLLeaves out VLDL cholesterol, which carries part of the blood's cholesterol, so this sum would underestimate the total.
    • (2) HDL + LDL + 30% of VLDLVLDL counts in full, not as 30%; the one-fifth rule applies to triglycerides, which are used to estimate VLDL.
    • (3) HDL + LDL + 10% of VLDLSame error: the whole VLDL value counts; only triglycerides are scaled, by one fifth, to estimate it.

  87. What is the correct sequence of transformation in integral yoga? (A) Transformation of physical (B) Transformation of subconscient (C) Transformation of mind (D) Transformation of vital (E) Transformation of inconscient

    • 1C, D, A, B, E
    • 2A, C, D, B, E
    • 3A, D, C, B, E
    • 4C, A, D, B, E
    ✓ Answer: (1) C, D, A, B, E
    Sri Aurobindo, Integral Yoga
    The concept

    Correct orderMind (C) → vital (D) → physical (A) → subconscient (B) → inconscient (E).

    How it worksIn Sri Aurobindo's Integral Yoga the higher consciousness descends from above, so the parts of the being change from the top down: first the mind, then the vital (life force, desires, emotions), then the body, and lastly the hidden subconscient and inconscient bases.

    Exam tipThe deeper the level, the later and harder its change; the inconscient is the last and most resistant.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) A, C, D, B, EStarts with the physical, but in Integral Yoga the body is among the last parts to change; the descent begins with the mind.
    • (3) A, D, C, B, EReverses mind, vital and physical, working upward from the body, whereas Aurobindo's transformation works downward from the mind.
    • (4) C, A, D, B, EPuts the physical before the vital, but the vital (desires and emotions) lies between mind and body and is transformed before the body.

  88. What comprises haptic perception? (A) Shape (B) Size (C) Volume (D) Identity

    • 1A, B and C only
    • 2A, C and D only
    • 3B, C and D only
    • 4A, B and D only
    ✓ Best answer: (4) A, B and D only
    James J. Gibson, active touch; Susan Lederman and Roberta Klatzky
    The concept

    Why this oneHaptic perception is perception by active touch: the hand moves over an object. It gives the object's shape and size and lets us recognise what it is (its identity), often within a couple of seconds without looking.

    How it worksHand movements combine skin input with kinaesthetic information from muscles and joints. Susan Lederman and Roberta Klatzky described exploratory procedures: contour following for shape, lateral motion for texture, pressure for hardness.

    Exam tipTactile (passive) perception = touch applied to a still hand; haptic = active exploration, the distinction drawn by James J. Gibson.

    Not certainVolume can also be judged by enclosing an object in the hand, so (1) is defensible; the item seems to follow a definition listing shape, size and identity.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, B and C onlyDrops identity, yet recognising what an object is by touch alone (haptic object recognition) is a main function of haptic perception.
    • (2) A, C and D onlyDrops size, which active touch judges directly, for example from how far the fingers spread around an object.
    • (3) B, C and D onlyDrops shape, the property most clearly given by haptic exploration, especially by tracing an object's contours with the fingers.

  89. Which neurotransmitter is helpful in synaptic plasticity?

    • 1Acetylcholine
    • 2Glutamate
    • 3GABA
    • 4Dopamine
    ✓ Answer: (2) Glutamate
    Timothy Bliss and Terje Lømo, long-term potentiation
    The concept

    Why this oneGlutamate, the brain's main excitatory transmitter, drives long-term potentiation (LTP), the best studied form of synaptic plasticity, especially in the hippocampus.

    How it worksWhen the cell is depolarised enough to remove the magnesium block, glutamate opens NMDA receptors. Calcium flows in and triggers changes, such as extra AMPA receptors, that strengthen the synapse.

    Exam tipThe NMDA receptor is a coincidence detector (it needs glutamate and depolarisation together), a cellular version of Donald Hebb's rule.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) AcetylcholineThe transmitter at the neuromuscular junction and in attention and memory circuits (lost in Alzheimer's disease); LTP itself depends mainly on glutamate.
    • (3) GABAThe main inhibitory transmitter; it opposes the depolarisation that NMDA-dependent plasticity needs.
    • (4) DopamineSignals reward and prediction error and can modulate plasticity, but it does not directly induce LTP.

  90. What is the correct sequence of the Decision Making Perspective of Pro-social behaviour to help a person in distress? (A) Taking personal responsibility (B) Weighing the cost and benefits (C) Perceiving a need (D) Deciding how to help

    • 1A, C, B, D
    • 2B, A, C, D
    • 3A, B, C, D
    • 4C, A, B, D
    ✓ Answer: (4) C, A, B, D
    Shelley Taylor, Letitia Peplau, David Sears: decision model of helping
    The concept

    Correct orderPerceiving a need (C) → taking personal responsibility (A) → weighing costs and benefits (B) → deciding how to help (D), then acting.

    How it worksA bystander must first notice the event and interpret it as an emergency, then feel personally responsible, then judge whether the rewards of helping outweigh the costs, and only then choose what to do.

    Exam tipDiffusion of responsibility blocks the second step: the more bystanders, the less each feels responsible (Bibb Latané and John Darley's bystander studies).

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A, C, B, DPuts responsibility before noticing the need; no one can take responsibility for a problem they have not yet perceived.
    • (2) B, A, C, DStarts by weighing costs and benefits, which comes only after the need is perceived and responsibility accepted.
    • (3) A, B, C, DPuts taking responsibility first and perceiving the need third; the model begins with noticing and interpreting the situation.

  91. Comprehension (Q91-Q95): A researcher wanted to predict academic achievement using Intelligence Test-A (IT-A), Intelligence Test-B (IT-B), Type of school (rural/urban), and conscientiousness as predictors, using multiple regression analysis on data from 200 students. The OLS estimator was used. For the entire model F = 8.9, p < .01. In individual predictor analysis, IT-B was the insignificant predictor. A suitable test indicated homoscedasticity. The model R² = 0.64 and adjusted R² = 0.59. Both intelligence tests are significantly correlated with the criterion variable and with each other.

    What is indicated by R² in the passage?

    • 164% variance is explained
    • 280% variance is explained
    • 332% variance is explained
    • 436% variance is explained
    ✓ Answer: (1) 64% variance is explained
    Coefficient of determination (R²)
    The concept

    Why this oneR², the coefficient of determination, is the share of criterion variance explained by all predictors together. R² = 0.64, so 0.64 × 100 = 64% of the variance in academic achievement is explained.

    Adjusted R²Adjusted R² (0.59) corrects for the number of predictors and sample size, so it is slightly smaller; 59% is the better estimate for the population.

    Exam tipUnexplained variance = 1 - R² = 0.36 (36%). The multiple correlation R = √0.64 = 0.80.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) 80% variance is explainedThis is the multiple correlation R (√0.64 = 0.80) read as a percentage; variance explained uses the squared value, R².
    • (3) 32% variance is explainedHalf of R²; nothing in the conversion halves R², which reads directly as 64%.
    • (4) 36% variance is explainedThis is 1 - R², the share of variance the predictors leave unexplained.

  92. Which of the following is correct about the residual variance in the passage?

    • 1It is correlated with the predictor variable
    • 2It is correlated with the criterion variable
    • 3It is systematically distributed
    • 4It is randomly distributed
    ✓ Answer: (4) It is randomly distributed
    OLS regression assumptions, homoscedastic residuals
    The concept

    Why this oneThe test showed homoscedasticity: residuals spread equally at every level of the predicted score. With the OLS assumptions met, residuals are randomly distributed around zero with no pattern.

    What residuals areA residual is observed minus predicted score (Y - Ŷ). OLS picks the weights that make the sum of squared residuals smallest, and by construction leaves residuals uncorrelated with the predictors.

    Exam tipA funnel or curve in the residual plot signals heteroscedasticity or non-linearity, that is, systematic error.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) It is correlated with the predictor variableOLS residuals are uncorrelated with every predictor by construction; any such correlation would mean the model had missed predictable variance.
    • (2) It is correlated with the criterion variableResiduals are part of the criterion score, so some overlap with it is built in; the assumption that matters is that they show no systematic pattern.
    • (3) It is systematically distributedA systematic pattern, such as a funnel or curve, would signal heteroscedasticity or a mis-specified model, the opposite of what the test found.

  93. What kind of a variable is 'type of school' in the passage?

    • 1Moderator variable
    • 2Mediator variable
    • 3Dummy variable
    • 4Quasi-continuous variable
    ✓ Answer: (3) Dummy variable
    Dummy coding of categorical predictors
    The concept

    Why this oneType of school has two categories (rural, urban). To enter it in a regression it is coded 0 and 1 (rural = 0, urban = 1), which makes it a dummy variable.

    How it worksThe weight of a dummy variable is the difference in predicted achievement between urban and rural students, other predictors held constant. A variable with k categories needs k - 1 dummies.

    Exam tipA moderator changes the strength of an X-Y link (tested with an interaction term); a mediator carries the effect from X to Y. The passage tests neither.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) Moderator variableThat role changes the strength or direction of a predictor-criterion link and is tested with an interaction term; school type here is simply one more predictor.
    • (2) Mediator variableThat role lies on the causal path from predictor to criterion (X → M → Y); the passage does not model school type that way.
    • (4) Quasi-continuous variableThat label fits variables with many ordered values, like rating scale totals; rural/urban has just two unordered categories.

  94. Which of the following is the reason for the insignificance of IT-B?

    • 1Heteroscedasticity
    • 2Poor predictive power
    • 3Small sample size
    • 4Multicollinearity
    ✓ Answer: (4) Multicollinearity
    Multicollinearity in multiple regression
    The concept

    Why this oneIT-B correlates with achievement but also with IT-A. Once IT-A is in the model, IT-B adds little unique variance and its standard error inflates, so its weight is not significant. This is multicollinearity.

    How it worksRegression weights reflect each predictor's unique contribution. When two predictors overlap, the variance they share is not credited to either one.

    Exam tipCheck it with the variance inflation factor (VIF) or tolerance; VIF above 10 (tolerance below 0.1) is a common warning sign.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) HeteroscedasticityRuled out: the passage says a suitable test showed homoscedasticity.
    • (2) Poor predictive powerIT-B is significantly correlated with achievement, so on its own it does predict; it loses significance only because its variance overlaps with IT-A.
    • (3) Small sample size200 students for four predictors is ample, and the other predictors reached significance in the same sample.

  95. Which of the following is the df for F for the entire model?

    • 14; 195
    • 24; 199
    • 33; 196
    • 43; 199
    ✓ Answer: (1) 4; 195
    F-test degrees of freedom in regression
    The concept

    Why this oneFor the whole model, df1 (regression) = k = number of predictors = 4, and df2 (residual) = N - k - 1 = 200 - 4 - 1 = 195.

    How it worksF = mean square regression ÷ mean square residual, each mean square being its sum of squares divided by its df. Total df = N - 1 = 199 = 4 + 195.

    Exam tipThe extra 1 subtracted in the residual df is for the intercept.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) 4; 199199 is the total df (N - 1); the residual df must also lose one df for each of the four predictors.
    • (3) 3; 196There are four predictors (IT-A, IT-B, school type, conscientiousness), not three, so df1 = 4 and df2 = 195.
    • (4) 3; 199Uses three predictors and the total df; the model has four predictors, and the residual df is 195.

  96. Comprehension (Q96-Q100): A psychologist developed a scale measuring warmth and competence dimensions. She factor-analysed 25 items to retain 20 items, choosing Exploratory Factor Analysis (EFA). Bartlett's test = 237.8, p < .001. The overall Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin (KMO) measure was 0.89. Principal Components Analysis (PCA) was used to extract components. The first eight eigenvalues were 9.9, 6.5, 1.6, 1.00, 0.85, 0.83, 0.82 and 0.79. Factors were retained on the basis of the scree plot and rotated using the varimax criterion. Three items showed cross-loadings, eighteen items loaded on only one factor, and the remaining items did not load on any factor.

    The Bartlett's test results in the above study suggested that:

    • 1The population correlation matrix is not an identity matrix
    • 2The population correlation matrix is an identity matrix
    • 3The sample correlation matrix is not an identity matrix
    • 4The sample correlation matrix is an identity matrix
    ✓ Answer: (1) The population correlation matrix is not an identity matrix
    Bartlett's test of sphericity
    The concept

    Why this oneBartlett's test of sphericity tests the null hypothesis that the population correlation matrix is an identity matrix. Here the value is 237.8, p < .001, so the null is rejected: the items do correlate in the population.

    What it isAn identity matrix has 1s on the diagonal and 0s elsewhere, meaning no item correlates with any other. Then there would be no common factors to extract.

    Exam tipA significant Bartlett test plus KMO above 0.6 (here 0.89) shows the data suit factor analysis.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (2) The population correlation matrix is an identity matrixThis is the null hypothesis, which the significant result (p < .001) rejects; only a non-significant test would point to an identity matrix.
    • (3) The sample correlation matrix is not an identity matrixSignificance tests make inferences about the population; the sample matrix is observed directly and is almost never exactly an identity matrix.
    • (4) The sample correlation matrix is an identity matrixThe test concerns the population, not the sample, and the significant result points away from an identity matrix, not towards one.

  97. Which of the following is correct for the Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin test? (A) It is a test of sampling adequacy (B) Chi-square test is used to test the significance (C) Values smaller than 0.5 are desirable (D) Smaller partial correlation leads to high KMO

    • 1A only
    • 2A and B only
    • 3A and D only
    • 4B, C and D only
    ✓ Answer: (3) A and D only
    Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin measure of sampling adequacy
    The concept

    Why this oneA is true: KMO is a measure of sampling adequacy. D is true: KMO compares simple correlations with partial correlations, so small partials give a high KMO.

    Why B, C falseB: KMO is an index from 0 to 1 with no chi-square test; chi-square belongs to Bartlett's test. C: values below 0.5 are unacceptable; higher is better.

    How it worksKMO = Σr² ÷ (Σr² + Σpartial r²). When items share common factors, partial correlations are small and KMO nears 1. The passage's 0.89 is 'meritorious' on Henry Kaiser's labels.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) A onlyA is true but incomplete: D is also true, because KMO rises as partial correlations shrink relative to simple correlations.
    • (2) A and B onlyB is false: KMO is a descriptive index from 0 to 1; the chi-square significance test belongs to Bartlett's test of sphericity.
    • (4) B, C and D onlyB and C are false: KMO has no chi-square test, and values below 0.5 make the data unacceptable for factor analysis; it also omits the true statement A.

  98. Which of the following is correct in case of the three items having cross-loading?

    • 1They have excellent validity
    • 2They are conceptually sound items
    • 3The scale has poor reliability
    • 4They should not be retained in the final scale
    ✓ Answer: (4) They should not be retained in the final scale
    Louis Thurstone, simple structure
    The concept

    Why this oneA cross-loading item loads substantially on two or more factors, so it does not measure one dimension cleanly. In scale building such items are usually dropped.

    Simple structureLouis Thurstone's simple structure asks that each item load highly on one factor and near zero on the rest; varimax rotation aims at this.

    Exam tipItems that load on no factor, like the remaining items in the passage, are also deleted.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) They have excellent validityCross-loading weakens construct validity, because the item does not clearly belong to warmth or to competence.
    • (2) They are conceptually sound itemsAn item that loads on two dimensions is conceptually ambiguous, not sound; its wording probably taps both constructs.
    • (3) The scale has poor reliabilityCross-loading is a problem of factorial validity for three items; by itself it says nothing about the reliability of the whole scale.

  99. In the above example, what percentage of the variance would be explained by the first unrotated factor?

    • 19.9
    • 239.6
    • 365.6
    • 472.0
    ✓ Answer: (2) 39.6
    Variance explained = eigenvalue ÷ number of variables
    The concept

    Why this oneIn PCA each standardised item contributes 1 unit of variance, so total variance = number of items analysed = 25. Variance explained = eigenvalue ÷ items × 100 = 9.9 ÷ 25 × 100 = 39.6%.

    What an eigenvalue isAn eigenvalue is the sum of squared loadings of all items on a component, that is, how much of the total variance it captures.

    Exam tipRotation (varimax) redistributes variance among the retained factors, which is why the question asks about the unrotated factor.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 9.9This is the eigenvalue itself, not a percentage; it must be divided by the 25 items to give the share of total variance.
    • (3) 65.6This is the cumulative variance of the first two components: (9.9 + 6.5) ÷ 25 = 16.4 ÷ 25 = 65.6%.
    • (4) 72.0This is the cumulative variance of the first three components: (9.9 + 6.5 + 1.6) ÷ 25 = 18 ÷ 25 = 72%.

  100. Using the Kaiser-Guttman criterion, how many factors would be retained in the above study?

    • 14
    • 28
    • 32
    • 43
    ✓ Answer: (4) 3
    Louis Guttman and Henry Kaiser, eigenvalue-greater-than-one rule
    The concept

    Why this oneThe Kaiser-Guttman criterion keeps components with eigenvalues greater than 1. Only 9.9, 6.5 and 1.6 exceed 1, so 3 factors are kept; the fourth value, 1.00, is not greater than 1.

    Why 1A component with an eigenvalue below 1 explains less variance than a single standardised item, so it is not worth keeping.

    Exam tipThe rule often keeps too many factors; the scree plot (Raymond Cattell) and parallel analysis are better guides. Here the sharp drop after 6.5 points to two factors, warmth and competence.

    Why the other options are wrong
    • (1) 4Counts the eigenvalue of exactly 1.00, but the criterion keeps only values greater than 1, so the fourth component is dropped.
    • (2) 8This is just the number of eigenvalues reported; four of them are below 1 and one is exactly 1.
    • (3) 2This is what the scree plot suggests (the elbow after 6.5) and fits the two-dimension theory, but the Kaiser-Guttman count is 3.
